    Lena is acutely aware of the touch of Kara’s hand as it continues on its downward path. “Kara,” she warns, staring resolutely ahead, still aware of where she is but now significantly more worried about dying of mortification rather than from traumatic aortic transection, “What are you doing?”

    “I’m distracting you,” Kara mutters. “Is it not working?”

    Lena slants her eyes surreptitiously at the strangers standing nearby, boisterously ribbing their friends or exclaiming loudly about the view. She finds herself beginning to relax at the realization that no one is watching them — and then she meets the full force of Alex Danvers’ homicidal glare.

    She looks more than willing to inflict a little aortic trauma, herself.

    Alex is the first one to reunite with them after the doors of the tram slide open to spit its passengers out onto the slopes. A modest-looking ski lodge is settled snugly against the side of the mountain, its pale wood paneling gleaming in the sunlight. Lena can practically taste her first sip of Zinfandel already. If she’s learned anything over the past few days, it’s that it’s always wine o’clock in the mountains.

    Alex is so mad she can barely breathe, blushing furiously as she pulls them aside. “Could you two stop groping each other for five damn seconds?”

    Lena bristles. “Uh, excuse me—”

    “We were being discrete,” Kara argues, which is precisely not the point Lena was going to make.

    “Right,” Alex spits, “It’s not as if, as Sam remarked, you can ‘spot the heart eyes you’ve been giving each other from space’. For fuck’s sake, try to muster at least a speck of self-control.”

    Kara giggles. Lena seethes. “Bite me, Danvers,” she sneers.

    “Um,” Kara interjects hastily, “which Danvers did you mean to address, there? Because I believe I’m much better suited to the—”

    Lena smacks her.

    “Yes. Good. Better. Let’s see more of that energy.” Alex glares at her sister, who smiles serenely in return. And to Lena, gesturing at Kara, “Could you just, like. Keep her restrained, or something?”

    Lena can’t resist. “Not exactly what I had in mind,” she tells Alex suavely, gratified when two jaws drop in response.

    “What has gotten into you?” Alex asks, stupidly, perfectly, as Kara beams, looking proud.

    “I didn’t think you wanted the specifics,” Lena deadpans.

    “Damn!” Kara whoops. “Do you want me to run to the first aid station real quick to get you some cream for that burn?”

    Alex is looking marginally less entertained than her sister. “Act your age, Luthor,” she grumbles.

    Lena spots Sam and Ruby waving, making their way toward them, and straightens, putting a respectable distance between Kara and herself. “You could try showing a little gratitude, Danvers,” she says. “I’ve handed you the perfect setting for your grand romantic proposal. You only need to muster up the courage to actually see it through.”

    “Shut up.” Alex coughs, flustered, but she recovers quickly. “There’s a far more important matter at stake here,” she says, dark eyes glinting.

    Lena smirks and nods, matching her grim tone. “Agreed. If we let Sam win, we will never live it down.”

    Alex’ mouth twitches into something almost resembling a smile. “I’m glad we have our priorities straight.”

    Kara watches them, baffled. “You both need therapy.”

    -

    Sam wasn’t lying about the lodge’s restaurant. The outside terrace is heated, suspiciously uncrowded, and bathed in sunlight save for one shaded spot in the far corner — and that’s where Lena installs herself, arranging her blankets and her laptop and her files around her, content in her own little approximation of an office. Hidden behind her Ray-Ban Jackie Ohhs and an indecently large glass of wine, she’s settled in for the afternoon.

    Burying herself in her work is a comfort, almost meditative. She’s at ease with facts, with figures, takes pleasure in arranging them all in a palatable form. Enjoys, too, reviewing L-Corp’s small triumphs and successes, acknowledging how well it’s managed to recover from her brother’s high profile disappearance and subsequent arrest.

    What Lena hadn’t been counting on was for the view to be so thoroughly distracting. The mountains are breathtaking, sure, and now that Lena’s feet are on solid ground again she’s actually able to appreciate them properly, but she also has a straight shot to the beginners’ class Kara and Ruby have signed up for and it’s hard not to let her attention wander when Kara is goofing off in her peripheral vision, looking like someone threw a bucket of kindergarteners on her. It’s clear that Kara’s got the snowboarding coach, as well as the handful of parents not treating the class as a babysitting service, thoroughly charmed.

    “Oh, that’s more like it,” Jess grins appreciatively, taking in Lena’s surroundings over a crystal clear video call connection. And then, a little jealous, “Why doesn’t the sun ever shine like that in National City?”

    Lena peers critically at her laptop screen. Jess is looking a little flushed, and while she’s talking, a guy Lena has never seen before walks into frame, wearing nothing but a pair of AirPods and shorts. When he pauses in front of what looks to be a full length mirror to take a selfie, Lena narrows her eyes at her assistant. “Are you at the gym?” she asks incredulously.

    Jess hums noncommittally. “It’s called working remote, maybe you’ve heard of it? My boss pretty much started the trend when she took off on a ski trip giving absolutely zero notice.”

    Lena’s attention snags briefly on Kara’s acrobatics again — she’s somehow managing to follow along with the coach’s instructions without dropping the clown act, falling on her ass dramatically before turning the practice maneuver the coach just demonstrated into a silly-yet-effective, knees-and-elbows-swinging dance routine.

    Pulling her eyes back to her screen, Lena glowers at her assistant. “You know just as well as I do that I’m being held here against my wishes,” she reminds her.

    “Really?” Jess' smile borders on predatory. “Because I subscribed to CDOT’s updates, and I heard they opened the pass this morning.”

    Lena keeps from blushing through sheer force of will. Down in the snow below, Kara is making a theatrical production out of chasing after a runaway board, navigating her own board down the practically non-existent incline balancing on one leg while the kids scream and cheer. “I still have a bet to win,” Lena informs Jess primly.

    Jess pulls a long sip from her water bottle. “There’s no way you’ll pull it off,” she says. “Too many variables. One of them is bound to chicken out at the last minute.” And, on the same breath, “Did you sleep with her yet?”

    Lena sighs, pulls up a new email and starts typing.

    Jess frowns, confused. “What are you doing?”

    Lena doesn’t look up. “Emailing HR to file a report against you.”

    “Right! So,” Jess deflects seamlessly, “about those emails we’ve been getting from Max Lord’s legal team—”

    They get down to proper business, then, but not before Lena glances down one last time. Kara has taken Ruby’s hands and they’re spinning leisurely down the ramp together, laughing so loud Lena can hear them, a slow but joyful pirouette.

    Kara is a fool.

    An absolutely lovely, undeniably gorgeous fool.

    -

    It’s maybe an hour and a half later when Lena stretches and yawns, ready to take a break. Jess has brought her fully up to date, muting her mic off and on to do a couple of reps on various intimidating-looking machines. They’re wrapping up their call just as Sam and Ruby saunter up.

    “Is that Jess?” Sam asks. “I want to talk to her. Rubes, sweetie, go get us some food, I’m starving.”

    “Can we get hot chocolate?” Ruby asks.

    Sam sinks down in the chair next to Lena, throws her feet up on the table and starts pulling off her gloves. “Um, obviously.”

    “Some ice cream, too?”

    Sam groans indulgently, leaning her head back against the chair. “Why not.”

    “Fries?”

    Sam waves her off. “Knock yourself out, kiddo.”

    “Bye Ruby!” Jess calls, then waits patiently until Sam gives the all clear. “So did they sleep together yet?” she asks as soon as she has it. “Lena’s not telling, but she’s looking alarmingly relaxed.”

    “I’m recovering from a migraine,” Lena retorts, worried there might be some truth to Jess’ statement. “I’m on a lot of drugs.” She holds up her half empty glass. “Also Sam’s appalling behavior is driving me to drink.”

    “You don’t need anyone to drive you,” Sam counters, taking the glass from her hand. Lena chokes on a high def vision from last night proving quite the opposite. “By the way, Jess,” Sam continues, “Thank you for smoothing things out with the Ritz tonight.”

    To Jess’ credit, she needs only a brief nod from Lena to pick up her cue. “No problem,” she says smoothly. “I hear congratulations are in order. Although, are you absolutely certain you want to go through with this? I hear divorce rates are—”

    “Bye Jess, thank you,” Lena sings, and hangs up. Sam snorts. Lena side-eyes her. “Jackals, both of you.”

    “We love you,” Sam says, reaching over to pat her cheek a little roughly. “We want you to be happy.”

    Lena swoops her long lashes dismissively, before realizing the gesture isn’t very effective when half her face is hidden behind sunglasses.

    Sam sniffs at Lena’s glass of wine before taking a long sip. “You do realize, with this whole hermit thing you’ve got going, you’re essentially robbing other people from the privilege of your friendship, right?” She grimaces, hands Lena back her glass. “That’s not very humanitarian of you.”

    Lena feels her mouth twitching in spite of herself. “Friendship, huh? Is that what you’re hoping will happen between Kara and me?”

    Sam regards her tiredly. “I’d settle for it, at this point, honestly. It’s exhausting work. I could use a backup BFF.”

    Lena throws back what’s left of her wine and studies the mountains, the snow, the ridiculously bright blue sky above them. It’s an almost exact match to the color of Kara’s eyes. Speaking of which, “Where are Alex and Kara, anyway? I thought you were afraid to leave the two of them alone.”

    Sam groans, steering Lena’s attention to the slopes with a tilt of her chin. “You try keeping up with those two. It’s like a dick measuring contest out there.”

    Lena peers out over her glasses, raising a hand as a shield against the unrelenting sunshine. Amidst the constant stream of people coming down the mountain, exhibiting varying amounts of finesse, Lena makes out two tiny figures that are clearly racing each other, one on skis and the other on a board. “Are they— is that them?”

    Out of the two Danvers siblings, it’s obvious that Alex has had more practice — her movements are razor sharp and quick, her balance absolutely impeccable. But Kara is a sight to behold. She’s flowing down the mountain like she’s made of water, demonstrating a sort of languid, almost heavy poise, and she’s only marginally slower than her sister.

    In fact Lena wonders, watching Kara come to a stop inches from her sister and spraying her with a perfect arch of snow, if Kara didn’t allow Alex to win precisely so she could do that.

    Lena’s mouth is dry. Other parts of her are decidedly less so. “Didn’t Kara learn how to snowboard literally five minutes ago?” she asks, her voice faltering hardly at all.

    Sam shrugs. “She’s like that. I don’t know how.”

    Lena blinks. “She’s not human, I think.”

    “That might be it.”

    Lena blames the wine for her soft heart, for the way it’s squeezing in her chest, urging her to clarify that she recognizes Kara for more than just the perfect physical specimen she is. An apology. A defense. “She’s kind,” she says.

    “She is.”

    Lena takes a breath, holds it for a moment before letting it go. “She can do a lot better than me.”

    Sam rolls her eyes dramatically. “Not this again.”

    “It’s true, isn’t it?” Lena asks, not even sure anymore why she said anything at all but clumsily seeing it through. “You’ve said it yourself. I don’t let people in.”

    “Oh, I know. you’ve always had a tendency to disappear into your work. But Lena, since Lex—”

    Lena shifts uncomfortably at the mention of her brother’s name. “I just worked harder,” she says stubbornly.

    “You’ve had a different thing to drive you,” Sam corrects. “You’re no longer just making the world better. You’re making reparations.”

    Lena picks at the sleeve of her sweater, carefully avoiding her best friend’s eyes. “That sounds like the same thing, to me.”

    Sam takes her hand. “Except it’s not. Not when your driving force is guilt rather than idealism.”

    A clinking of plates accompanied by Ruby’s triumphant voice announces the arrival of an outrageous amount of food but before Sam digs in, before she voices her appreciation of Ruby’s eclectic selection, Sam leans in and captures Lena’s eyes, giving her one last heartfelt look.

    “You’re going to have to find forgiveness, Lena,” she tells her. “You’ll run yourself into the ground if you keep punishing yourself.”

    Lena needs to turn away, take a moment, take a breath, before she’s ready to show her face again. Sam is right. She realizes this.

    Lena Luthor may not have the best track record when it comes to choosing romantic partners, but she definitely knows how to pick her friends.

    -

    For two people already committed to each other and more than ready to start the rest of their lives together, Sam and Alex are awfully skittish about the business of actually proposing. Lena is beginning to wonder seriously if, had she not chosen to interfere with their affairs, they would have managed to get it together enough to ask each other at all.

    They hang out in Sam and Alex’s bedroom while the brides to be get ready to go to dinner, Ruby and Kara belly-down on the bed kicking their legs in the air and Lena propped against the doorframe, passing a bag of kettle corn between them while they watch Alex and Sam sprinting back and forth between their walk-in closet and their master bath in an awe inspiring, one-time-only performance of perfectly synchronized desperation.

    It would be hilarious, had Lena not had a stake in the evening’s events taking place exactly as planned.

    “Lena, do you have some concealer I can borrow?” Alex pleads. She’s wearing a pencil skirt and a bra and the most tightly tensed shoulders Lena has ever witnessed on a woman outside of a Madonna music video. “I need something to cover up this hideous burn.” Her cheeks are barely flushed, just a faint remnant from the afternoon sun that only makes her look younger and a little less severe, but Lena doesn’t dare disagree with her. “In the bathroom, upstairs.”

    Sam has methodically tried on every single outfit she’s brought to Colorado and has now moved on to putting them together in new, creative and horribly misguided combinations. “You know you’ll just end up wearing the gray pinstripe Armani,” Lena tells her. “Stop stalling.”

    Sam drops face first on the bed next to Ruby. “I’m going to take just, like, a tiny little bit of a nap,” she nuzzles. “I think it will help me feel better.”

    When they finally get them out the door, only twenty minutes late, it feels like a triumph. “Have fun, kids,” Kara tells her sister with a quick peck on her cheek. “Use protection.”

    Sam pauses on the steps. “Lena, are you sure the suit is okay? I think the turquoise top might be—”

    Kara closes the front door on the rest of her sentence, stepping back into Lena’s personal space in order to do so. “Don’t you kiss me,” Lena warns.

    Ruby giggles. Kara grins. “Maybe later. Burgers first.”

    Big Belly Burger is busy, evidently a major draw even this time of year. Browsing the menu through the rolled down window of the car, Lena tries not to think of the grilled eggplant with cumin confit she could be having instead and bravely decides to go for deep fried chicken wings and a baby spinach salad and, at Ruby’s insistence, a supersized Seasonal Mint Master Blast™.

    Lena has a feeling she’ll be regretting her choice later. Her stomach is already churning, trying to digest the unfortunate combination of relief and mild depression that comes from knowing her job is done here, knowing this night with Kara will be their last.

    “You’re going to taste delicious,” Kara grins into her hair while Ruby relays their order through the intercom.

    Kara’s order is a chicken sandwich with extra pickles, two large fries, one with chili cheese, mozzarella sticks, a double cheeseburger with garlic sauce and three servings of onion rings. “You, maybe not so much,” Lena notes with just a hint of polite-adjacent distaste.

    The food takes half a century to arrive and by the time Ruby and Lena are finished with theirs, it’s almost ten pm. Kara is still eating, the mountain of food in her lap enough to keep her occupied for at least another twenty minutes. “We should head home,” Lena suggests. “It’s late.”

    Kara sucks the tips of her fingers exactly the way she did when tasting her, the night before. The memory hits Lena like a shot.

    “You in a hurry to get to bed?” Kara asks, her eyes sparking with humor.

    “Actually,” Ruby says from the backseat, “I’m kinda tired. I hardly got any sleep last night with the way the wind was howling around the house.”

    Lena’s heart goes still in her chest at the realization that it was a perfect, starlit night; that the only moaning Ruby may have heard would have come from their bedroom.

    “I wouldn’t know,” Kara says breezily. “I slept like a babe.”

    Ruby makes a face. “Lucky.”

    “I sure hope so,” Kara says, giving Lena a wildly obvious wink.

    Lena can’t stand it a moment longer. “Get out,” she tells Kara. “You can eat while I drive.”

    Kara coos before doing as she says. “I’ve never tried that one, before.”

    Later, at the hospital, when attempting to explain to Ruby’s mother exactly how she traumatized her child, Lena will throw Kara under the bus without a moment’s hesitation. It was her fault, after all; if Kara was less of a messy eater, if she hadn’t made an urgent request for napkins while Lena was supposed to be watching the road, there would have been no collision at all.

    There would have been no tires skidding, no frantic swerve, no sudden, eerie silence. Kara wouldn’t have her hand on Lena’s breast, probably, at least not in a reflexive protective gesture.

    There definitely wouldn’t be a lifeless shape lying in the middle of the road a few feet ahead, framed by the headlights of their car.

    Ruby is the first one to speak. “Holy shit, dude,” she whispers, her voice strange in the perfect stillness of the moment.

    “Language,” Lena says, absently, preposterously, her heart having fallen to the floor, rolling around somewhere between the gas pedal and the brake.

    Kara’s hand is still on Lena’s chest. “I’d like to state for the record that bad things happen when you refuse to let me take the wheel,” she says.

    Lena stares blankly ahead at the figure in the road, still hearing the thudding sound it made when she glanced it with her car. Is it alive? Is it human?

    Wait.

    … is it wearing a Santa hat?

    “Uh, aunt Lena?” Ruby asks. “Did we just kill Santa Claus?”

    Excellent, Lena thinks distantly, there’s another one for the wall above the Luthor mansion’s mantle.

    All this time, she’d been expecting Colorado to bring about her own untimely end.

    Never once had Lena Luthor considered that she might actually be the one doing the killing.

    Chapter End Notes

    They will be fiiine.

    Need something to tide you over until the next chapter? I was a guest on Ayaka Spencer’s podcast and we talked about this story quite a bit! You can listen to my episode here.

    Also! Go check out the art these lovely people have made for this story:
    battenthecrosshatches made these spot-on sketches, budgebuttons made these amazing drawings and msdanvers made an incredible gifset!

    When We Finally Kiss Goodnight

    Chapter Notes

    I know, right? Finally! And now I've gone and upped the chapter count again. Whoops.

    Lena Luthor is going to go to prison.

    Of course even prison will be a relief after the drawn out, sensationalized Luthor family circus trial that’s bound to precede her sentence. She’s going to have to speak to John Corben again, that despicable stain-on-mankind lawyer her father had always favored and who has a perplexing ability to turn Lillian into a blushing, eyelash-batting “Oh, Johnny” version of herself that Lena finds deeply disturbing.

    “Are you alright?”

    Lena’s distantly aware that Kara’s hand has moved from her chest to her thigh, making sure both are now thoroughly coated in garlic sauce. Then it’s gone, reaching for the child in the backseat, the child Lena’s sworn to her mother to protect — her best friend, Samantha Arias, the yellow belt Krav Maga (“Which is far more intimidating than it sounds, Lena”) badass who’s going to murder her with her bare hands once she gets word of what happened here tonight.

    So maybe she’ll be spared from having to deal with Corben, after all.

    She should text Jess to send Sam a thank you gift basket while she still can.

    Kara’s voice sounds out again, warm and gentle. “Ruby, are you all in one piece back there?”

    “Yeah, I was wearing my seatbelt.” Ruby’s voice sounds okay, if a fair bit louder than Lena’s grown accustomed to. Loud enough to be heard perfectly over the ringing in Lena’s ears. “Did we just kill Santa Claus?”

    “I’m sure he’s fine, honey,” Kara assures Ruby without a trace of irony. Gingerly, as if careful not to startle Lena, she presses the button that turns on the hazard lights, painting the snow around them a lurid red. “Lena?” she enquires again.

    Just a little way down the mountain, Alex and Sam are having their fancy rooftop dinner under the stars. They’ll be clinking their champagne glasses and laughing, plotting out the rest of their lives together. Lena’s eyes are locked on the shape in the road. It still hasn’t stirred. She can’t breathe. If she tries, she’ll probably vomit.

    Ruby’s foot gently prods the back of her seat. “Try using your words, aunt Lena.”

    Lena swallows, straightens her shoulders, then lets it out. “Fuck.”

    Kara’s hand finds Lena’s knee again and squeezes. “... perhaps try using different words.”

    Lena blinks, nods. Right. Language. “I’m going to prison,” she corrects.

    “No, look, we’re alright.” With the hand that’s not currently occupied, Kara reaches to release her own seatbelt, yanks as it refuses to budge.

    Lena’s really not alright. “I’ll have to pick up chess again.”

    “Everything’s going to be fine.” Kara struggles some more. “Would you actually mind giving me a hand?”

    Lena sighs. Kara would never understand. “I loathe chess.”

    Kara yanks at her seatbelt again, a little more frantically this time. “You’re fine. I mean. I’m sure we didn’t hit—” she pauses, holding her breath for a second as she peers out through the window. “You don’t think that’s actually Santa?”

    Lena sets her jaw, a spark of resolve blooming inside her. “Maybe this time I’ll just try to get really buff, instead.”

    Kara stills. “Wait a minute. This time?” She stares at her. “Are you saying you’ve been to prison before?”

    Lena tears her eyes away from the crumpled mass in their headlights for the first time since they stopped and sets them on Kara, instead. The remains of Kara’s strawberry milkshake are dripping slowly down the front of her jacket and onto the passenger seat. Kara’s never getting her deposit back. “You’re aware Santa Claus doesn’t actually exist, right?” Lena wonders.

    Kara shifts uncomfortably in her seat. “Maybe, um. You could get out and check to see who… or what... we’ve hit?”

    “Me?” Lena looks at her, appalled. “Why should I be the one to go out and check?”

    “My seatbelt’s stuck!” Kara swallows. “And also I thought maybe you’d… dealt with...? This kind of thing, before?”

    Lena can barely speak, she's so baffled.

    Kara’s wide-eyed, but Lena can’t figure out if her expression is apologetic or genuinely fearful. “Don’t look at me like that!” Kara demands, “You just said you’ve been to prison before!”

    And, you know, that’s the thing; everyone thinks Lena Luthor is palatable, at first glance, pretty and rich and a good liar when she needs to be, but there’s a limit to the things she can allow people to know about her before the picture they’ve painted of her shatters and they’re faced with the wreckage underneath.

    She believed Kara would be different.

    Fuck, part of her believes it still.

    Lena works her jaw, blinks a couple of times before she reaches over Kara’s far shoulder, assuring they’re now both covered hip to neck in strawberry shake and garlic sauce. Kara’s breath is on Lena’s lips, syrupy sweet with just a hint of chili cheese, and Lena knows she’d still kiss her, if Kara wanted her to. The thought that she may never do so again turns her disappointment into anger. With a quick flick of her wrist, she releases the catch of Kara’s seatbelt. “I was framed.”

    “Thank you.” Kara’s gaze is as open as ever. Lena is close enough to see the small specks of gold around her pupils, a stray freckle at the corner of her mouth.

    Lena extricates herself from her with a great deal more reluctance than is strictly appropriate, considering their current predicament. “I’m not a monster,” she asserts, struck suddenly, dizzily, by the way the words barely manage to strangle their way out of her throat, leaving it painful and swollen as if they’re stuck there still.

    “Oh, hey, I know that!” Kara throws her arms around Lena without a moment's hesitation. Lena chooses to ignore the various foodstuffs seeping into her shirts in favor of taking in the warmth of her embrace, the comfort of her words. “I’d never think you were!” She’s brushing her hand down the back of Lena’s head in a manner deeply detrimental to Lena’s ability to maintain her composure. “I’m just. Finding out all these new things I didn't know about you.”

    Lena sniffs, horrified to find her nose is running, and then that there are actual tears in her eyes. “You’ve literally known me for two days, Kara,” she blubbers through trembling lips.

    “So that ink I saw the other night,” Kara mumbles into her hair, “Am I right to assume those are actually prison tats? Because that would be pretty freaking—”

    “Kara!” Ruby’s voice calls out to them, far more quiet and decidedly less coming-from-inside-the-car than it was before.

    Lena and Kara part quickly. God. Right. There’s the small matter of her possible manslaughter, and the child she just traumatized. Lena blinks back her tears, blinks back to the reality that Ruby is no longer in the back seat. Her eyes follow Kara’s as she looks up through the windshield and finds Ruby standing in the road ahead of them, stooped low over the lifeless shape crumpled there — or, more accurately, doubled over, shaking, her eyes closed tightly.

    “Ruby!” Kara’s out of the car and at her side in a flash. Is she crying? Is she throwing up? Every muscle in Lena’s body is telling her to go to her, but she seems to have forgotten entirely how to move.

    In the red tinted lights of the car, Lena watches as Kara hugs Ruby to her chest before she joins her in what Lena now recognizes as wildly relieved laughter.

    “Hey aunt Lena,” Ruby calls out, a critical look aimed up at her phone, “this light is really washing me out. Do you think you could turn it off for a minute? Just while I make a quick video?“

    -

    “It isn’t funny,” Lena grumbles. She’s staring at her hands on the steering wheel. She’s been trying to get them to stop shaking for almost ten minutes, but they’re out of control, adrenaline clearly taking its sweet time leaving her system.

    Ruby has tried explaining it to her three times now. Lena can tell from the way her fingers are fiddling with the wet Santa hat she’d plucked from Lena’s faux fatality that her patience is waning. She’d remembered, she says, seeing the fractured snowman on the road; the Santa outfit, the carrot. Some sort of TikTok challenge. Teenagers risking strangers’ lives for something Ruby calls 'clout'. A prank. A joke. They clearly hadn’t wanted to stick around to deal with the aftermath.

    “It’s not,” Kara agrees, sounding properly castigated. “Someone really could have gotten hurt.”

    Ruby rolls her shoulders. “I know,” she admits. “I feel the same way I did the day after they had us do the Cooper run in PE. Are you guys sore too?”

    “We’re taking you to the hospital,” Lena decides, renewed alarm bringing her to her senses instantaneously.

    “Switch with me,” Kara says. “I’ll drive.”

    “Actually there’s a small, tiny problem,” Lena confesses. Kara looks her over, all bright blue eyed concern, until Lena blurts, “I can’t walk.”

    Kara’s expression blooms into a grin. “I’ve noticed that about you.”

    “Shut up,” Lena snaps, scandalized at Kara's tactlessness in light of Lena’s condition. “Can’t you see I’m in shock?”

    Kara takes it in stride. “Come on.” She’s already bent over and pushed into Lena’s personal space, sliding a warm, confident hand underneath Lena’s thighs. “You helped me with my seatbelt, I’ll help you with your legs.”

    Lena should definitely have just crawled her way out of the car. The way she’s feeling now, she probably could have comfortably slithered out onto the asphalt. But instead, for the second time in twenty-four hours, Lena Luthor is lifted up in Kara Danvers’ arms, feeling both light-headed and leaden, her hair whipping around Kara’s face in the snow-freckled breeze. Like she’s some sort of damsel in distress, the love interest to Kara’s square-jawed superhero. As if she’s still worth her protection — as if she’s still worth saving.

    Kara drives. Extra carefully. It’s excruciating.

    Lena sits in the backseat, an anxious hand on the back of Ruby’s neck, feeling more like herself with every mile that passes at the speed of an elderly person on their way to church. “I could run faster than this,” she gripes.

    Kara chuckles softly. “I doubt that.”

    -

    The emergency room is bright and loud, and packed — apparently an intoxicated group of friends on a weekend getaway / bachelorette party decided it would be a splendid idea to go skiing on an unlit ramp, so the wait is quite a bit longer than Lena thinks should be legal.

    Kara has an arm wrapped around Ruby’s shoulders and her eye on the food cart parked just outside the nurses station. “If I say my neck is sore too, would I get to have some of that jello?”

    The receptionist tosses her a clipboard along with a thoroughly charmed smile. “Fill these out, and I’ll see what I can do.”

    Kara looks a little forlorn at her quest for pudding being suspended and having been given a homework assignment instead. Lena holds out her hand. “Give it to me.”

    Kara’s pout melts away, making way for a mock-scandalized expression. “What,” she asks, “Here?”

    Lena breathes, practices patience. “Kara.”

    Kara’s looking down the hall as if she’s checking out their options. “I suppose we could find an on-call room or something. I mean they do it in Grey’s Anatomy all the time, right?”

    Ruby giggles. Lena suffers. “Kara.”

    “Sorry.” Kara straightens and hands over the clipboard apologetically. “Just a joke, Ruby. See, some of us use humor to defuse stressful situations.”

    Ruby doesn’t seem fazed. “I have to pee,” she announces.

    Kara begins fidgeting immediately. “Gosh, me too. Let’s go find a bathroom.”

    Lena is left to entertain herself with the insurance forms. As a properly prepared aunt who takes her duties very seriously, all of the information she needs for a situation exactly like this one is in an easily accessible file Sam created for her when she first started occasionally entrusting Ruby to her care. It’s in the cloud, conveniently named The Good Aunt Rulebook, just a couple of clicks away. Eyeing the names on the staff in/out board behind the front desk, Lena’s fingers hesitate over her messenger app, noting the late hour, before decisively pulling up the ongoing conversation with her assistant.

    Jess’ reply pops up on her screen moments after Lena has delivered the forms back to the receptionist. ER doc on duty = Grace P. Parker, it reads. Graduated 2009 CU Med. 2 formal reprimands for what looks to me like speaking her mind to disruptive patients. Overall favorable reviews. And then, in a separate message: r u ok?

    Lena makes a note to give Jess another raise as soon as HR will allow her to do so again. All good, she tells her. Just a check-up.

    Jess replies almost instantaneously. I don’t think you understand what a vacation is.

    Lena allows her head to fall back against her seat, closes her eyes. What she really needs is to lie down and not get up for seven hundred years. The lack of sleep from the past forty-eight hours is finally catching up to her and she’s at that stage where she has a tiny dream every time she blinks. The current one features the deserted streets of a tiny Tuscan town just after an early morning rain shower, no one around to recognize her except for Kara, who shimmers gold in the first rays of sunlight. She’s wearing a Santa hat.

    “Hey, I know you.”

    It’s a man that’s seated in chairs on the other side of the aisle, dressed sharp but red-eyed, sweating at his temples. He’s swaying slightly, propped up by the police officer at his side. “Luthor, right? As in Luthor Industries?”

    Lena frowns at him. “You’ve got the wrong man.”

    “No, no, you’re the brilliant bastard’s sister, I know you are. I’d recognize that face anywhere.” He throws her a look that’s probably meant to be smoldering but comes off rather more lethargic. When Lena fails to look charmed, he straightens his shoulders. “I’m—”

    “Drunk?” Lena guesses with a pointed eyebrow.

    He looks a little hurt as he tries to offer her his hand, but is foiled by the cuffs around his wrists. “… Morgan Edge.”

    She looks down at his predicament. “That’s nice. Listen, Mr. Edge, I hope you don’t mind—”

    “Not at all, it’s a pleasure. And please, call me Morgan.” Lena doesn’t miss his eyes lingering on her (milkshake-stained) chest before pulling back up slowly. “How about you let me buy you a drink?”

    Lena sighs. “You know, I’m actually good? Seeing as how we’re in an emergency room, and you appear to be under arrest.”

    “Didn’t think that would matter so much to someone with your last name,” Edge leers.

    Lena freezes, taking a beat longer than she’d like to formulate a comeback. Then, blessedly, Ruby pipes up behind her. “Ooh, hey Kara, did you hear that?” Her voice rings out, cheerful and clear and audible to everyone in the waiting room. “That is what we call negging.”

    Edge’s face turns a ripe chili pepper red. The doctor, who has chosen that moment to appear, offers Ruby a high five. “Dr. Parker,” she introduces herself. “You’re looking far too well to be my next patient.”

    She checks Ruby over, thoroughly and professionally and then, at Lena’s insistence, tolerantly does it all a second time after everything comes up perfectly fine. She offers Ruby a lollipop when she’s done. Kara ogles it greedily before aiming an impressive pair of puppy dog eyes at the doctor. “You know," she says, "my neck—”

    “You just ate what I’m pretty certain amounts to a five-course meal,” Lena snaps.

    “I also think the lady at the front desk is holding one of those jello cups for you,” Ruby points out. Lena would tell her not to talk with her mouth full, like the responsible aunt she is, but Ruby is helping her make her point, so.

    “We all cope with stress in our own ways!” Kara argues. “You forgot how to walk!”

    In an instant, Lena finds herself under the unexpectedly heavy weight of Dr. Parker’s scrutiny. Uh oh. “Were you stumbling, after the accident?”

    Lena shakes her head in a manner she believes is most counter-indicative of a brain injury. “I’m perfectly alright. I’m just tired.” But the doctor pulls out her flashlight again, and Lena flinches when it’s pointed directly into her eyes.

    “That’s fine, but I’m hearing balance issues and low energy, and I’m seeing some irritability and light sensitivity as well. Those can all point toward a concussion. Can you be sure you didn’t hit your head?”

    “No,” Kara interjects before Lena can answer, helpful as always, and the doctor gestures for Lena to take a seat on the exam table while she procures a percussion hammer. It’s their unpleasant encounter with the thermal underwear saleswoman all over again, and Lena will never forgive Kara for this. “Can you tell me your name?” the doctor asks.

    “Ruby!”

    Lena sends a silent prayer of thanks to whichever indulgent / forgotten god may still be listening, because the panicked shriek means Sam is finally here to end her life, and hopefully she’ll do it before the doctor gets the chance to measure the circumference of Lena’s chest or something more medically relevant but equally humiliating.

    Sam is wild-eyed and frantic, bringing to mind the image of a deer unexpectedly trapped in a small space, all skinny, infinite limbs and zero sense of direction. She sends a kidney basin clattering to the floor, narrowly avoiding knocking over the entire medical tray, before stopping herself inches from throwing her arms around her daughter. She gentles her hands to cradle Ruby’s face, instead. “Are you okay?”

    Ruby glares at Kara, deeply annoyed. “You called my mom?”

    Lena squashes the ridiculous urge to step in between them, to shield Kara from Ruby’s chagrin. “We had to, honey,” she soothes, “it’s in the Good Aunt Rulebook.”

    This makes Ruby frown at her mother, instead. “Where’s Alex?” she demands.

    Sam has let go of Ruby’s face and is now patting down her arms and her back, checking her for injuries. “She’s parking the car. Oh my god, Rubes, are you sure you're alright?”

    “I gave her a clean bill of health,” Dr. Parker assures Sam. “You’re free to take her home.” And then, taking Lena’s hands in her own, “Squeeze, please.”

    Sam at long last hugs her daughter tightly against her, sending Lena a furious look over her shoulder. “What the fuck happened?”

    Ruby’s words come out slightly muffled by her mother’s jacket. “Aunt Lena murdered a man.”

    Sam freezes. “I — what?” She blinks down Lena’s face, her body, finally seeming to realize she’s in the middle of a medical exam. Lena watches as the outrage on Sam’s face melts down to profound concern.

    “Maybe it's worth mentioning that the man in question was made of snow,” Kara hastily clarifies.

    Sam deflates. “I hate all of you, so very, very much.”

    The next disturbance is provided swiftly and effectively by the resounding voice of Alexandra Danvers, MD, threatening to sue the hospital as well as everyone on duty if she’s not shown to Ruby’s room right this second. “I’m a doctor!” she yells, and when that doesn’t yield the desired results, “You have my daughter!”

    “Someone should probably go calm down my mom,” Ruby snorts, still held hostage in her (other) mother’s arms. Kara is already on her way. Alex doesn’t quiet down until Kara is physically dragging her back into the exam room, still turned away, addressing some unseen adversary as she’s pushed through the door. “That’s my wife in here!” she shouts, “Almost! Whatever! Soon!”

    Sam is silent, her ears pink but her expression oddly guilty, and she’s now purposefully avoiding Lena’s eyes. Lena looks down at her best friend’s hands, and then at Alex’s. Both of them are curiously — alarmingly — engagement ring-free.

    Oh shit.

    It didn’t happen.

    They didn’t fucking ask.

    Sam keeps Alex from repeating Sam’s own theatrics in making sure Ruby’s alright, folding her into their hug briefly before pointing out they’re done worrying about Ruby. “We’re now waiting to find out whether Lena will survive the night, instead.”

    “I’m fine, by the way," Kara says primly.

    Alex steps into Lena’s space, waving a hand in front of her face. “How many fingers?” she asks, when, really? She should know so much better by now.

    Lena merely raises a silent eyebrow in response, and Kara purses her lips and blushes perfectly discreetly. Alex looks like she wants to dropkick the both of them regardless.

    “Yes,” the doctor says, less than charitably, “that’s a much more trustworthy test than the ones I’ve been trying to run.”

    Alex scowls. “For your information, that was me testing blink-to-threat and ruling out double vision.” She straightens her shoulders. “I work fast. I’m a cardiothoracic surgeon.”

    Dr. Parker rolls her eyes. “Why am I not surprised.”

    Kara is looking a little skeptical herself, trying her best to gauge how well Lena’s performing all of the doctor’s little tricks. “Aren’t you going to do, like, some sort of scan?” she asks. “Like one of those fMRI things?” Lena notices Kara’s fingers twitching in Lena’s direction, like she’s keeping herself from reaching for her. “She’s got a very fancy brain in there, we shouldn’t be cutting corners.”

    “We might do a CT,” she doctor says, which is just brilliant, just fantastic, thank you Kara, “if I can’t properly rule out—”

    Enough. “I don’t have a head injury, Kara,” Lena bursts out. “Sweet Christ, I’m irritable and I’m tired and I’m dizzy because I’ve been working eighteen hour days for a month, and you haven’t allowed me to get any sleep for two. Nights. A lesser person than me would be showing symptoms of psychosis, by now.”

    It gets really quiet, after that.

    And it doesn’t even need to mean anything. Right? The migraine excuse is right there, right? Except Kara is beet red, and Lena is pretty sure she is too, and Alex’s face has taken on a similar yet decidedly angrier shade.

    Don’t look at Sam. Don’t look at Sam.

    Lena’s pretty sure they’re all looking at Sam, whose eyes are beginning to go awfully wide, flicking between all of their guilty faces.

    “SO!” Kara yells, far too loudly, far too joyfully, “Congratulations, Mrs and Mrs Danvers!”

    As Kara’s distraction tactics tend to be, Lena’s learning, it’s about as efficient as it is catastrophic. Lena watches it happen in slow motion, like the helpless air traffic control room, seconds from disaster. “Kara,” She hisses, “Shut—” she aims for Kara’s shin and lands the kick, but Kara doesn’t seem to feel it.

    (“Reflexes look to be in fine working order,” Dr. Parker remarks with a smirk.)

    Kara moves to grab her sister’s shoulder jovially, extends a hand to Sam. “I bet you didn’t think you’d be celebrating your engagement in the emergency room, huh? Ha! Ha!”

    “Engagement?” Sam squeaks, admirably, considering she looks like she just swallowed her own heart. “You know Alex just said that, right? For the, something, to get access—”

    “No but,” Kara stutters, “I thought. The fancy dinner, the ring—”

    “Ring? Ha! Ha!” Alex’ hysterical laugh is so perfectly like her sister’s it’s uncanny. “There’s no ring. I don’t have a ring. Do you have a ring? What’s a ring?”

    For a moment, Alex and Sam just stare at each other, frozen in a bubble of mutual, outrageously ineffective deception.

    Sam looks the most frightened Lena’s ever seen her, and she’s seen her through some objectively scary times; against almost this exact same backdrop, in fact, except Sam had been the one on the exam table then. Lena shivers. Sam, however, seems determined to power through. “You mean you also got—?”

    Alex looks like she needs a drink. “You?” She blinks a couple of times. “You were going to—?”

    Sam is the first to pull herself together. She always was the bravest of them all. “Was there something you wanted to ask me, Alex?”

    “I mean.” Alex lets out a wobbly breath, attempts a smile. “Not if you—”

    “Oh my god. Will one of you just fucking propose already?”

    So it’s Ruby, in the end, who saves them from further mortification, and they’re all thankful, Lena’s sure, even though Sam lifts a disapproving eyebrow at her daughter’s language, even though her outburst means Lena just lost her bet with Jess.

    Alex is regaining some of her usual swagger, a tiny smile playing around her lips. “Sam,” she says, stretching out the vowel in a teasing lilt, “is your daughter actually proposing to me for you, right now?”

    “Actually, Alex,” Sam tells her, stepping forward, putting her hands on her girlfriend’s hips, “I think she’s proposing for the both of us. If you want to get technical about it.”

    “I think you’re right,” Alex admits, dark eyes blinking up at Sam almost shyly. “So, um. Do you want to?”

    Sam’s smile is radiant, peaceful and exuberant all at once. She leans her forehead against Alex’s, nudging their noses together. “Yeah,” she says. “I want to.”

    Lena is lost in the moment, present there with them but now also unquestionably outside of their little circle. It’s a happiness that hurts, a transitional sort of nostalgia, a homesickness for a place where she never truly belonged.

    She startles violently when the doctor in front of her pointedly clears her throat. “I have your results, if you're still interested,” she announces.

    Kara comes over, giving the newly engaged couple and their daughter some privacy and delivering a warm side-hug and a brilliant, lopsided smile. “I hope you’re not about to tell her she’s pregnant,” she quips. “That would be a true modern day miracle.”

    “More like a modern day curse,” Lena corrects her tiredly, not minding anything much anymore after witnessing the scope of her friends’ happiness.

    Sam and Alex are too preoccupied exclaiming over their respective engagement rings to hear them, anyway.

    -

    The house is as welcoming as it was the very first night Lena entered it, glowing like a beacon against the snow covered mountains and the star-speckled indigo sky above, but Lena feels more than ever like she’s intruding. No matter the contented smiles her best friend sends her way, or the wink Alex gives her along with a glass of the bourbon Lena was sure she’d stashed where no one would ever find it. No matter Kara’s warm hand, low on her back, when she shoos her sister away, still fussing about the possibility of the doctor overlooking signs of a concussion and insisting Lena doesn’t add alcohol to the mix, or the sound of Ruby’s laughter, following the girl upstairs to bed. It’s all familiar now, and it’s lovely, but it’s never made Lena’s chest feel quite as sore as it does now.

    “I thought we’d seen the last hospital visit for a while.”

    Lena finds Sam standing in the doorway of Ruby’s room when she leaves the upstairs bathroom, ready to turn in too, feeling more jetlagged than she did after that extended business trip to Singapore. Lena knocks her shoulder into hers, both watching Ruby as she sleeps. She whispers, “I’m so sorry, Sam.”

    The self-deprecating laugh Sam sends Lena’s way is a little tearful. “I know it’s dumb,” she admits. “She was wearing her seatbelt. I know she’s okay.”

    “She is.”

    “We’re okay.”

    Lena smiles. “You are.”

    “I kinda have a, uh.” Sam plays with the ring on her left ring finger, looking extra bright-eyed with the tears still clinging to her lashes. She bites her lip, then beams up at her. “A fiancée.”

    Lena rests her head on Sam’s shoulder, takes her hand to bring it closer. She hums. “It’s much prettier than I gave Alex credit for,” she remarks.

    Sam pushes Lena’s head away, but she’s gentle about it. “Lena Kieran Luthor,” she enunciates, “Did you know Alex was going to propose to me tonight?”

    Lena huffs out a breath. “I mean. Did she propose? I did everything in my power to make sure you both would, but. Ruby did most of the asking, didn’t she?”

    Sam looks at her daughter again, now serenely drooling onto her pillow, and smiles fondly. “If you want to get technical about it.”

    Lena sighs. “A crushing defeat.”

    Sam pauses. “You made a bet with Kara, didn’t you?”

    Lena sags back into her side, now unapologetically resting a significant amount of her body weight on her best friend’s shoulder. “Jess, actually. I think Kara’s much too good to want to take part in a wager over her loved ones’ happiness.”

    Sam rolls her eyes so far back that Lena can feel it. “God,” Sam exhales. “This family.”

    Lena feels her heart breaking a little bit, again, at the word. With tremendous effort she peels herself away from Sam’s lengthy frame. “Goodnight, Sam. Thank you for everything.” Even standing on her toes, she needs to pull Sam down a few inches to press a kiss on her cheek. “I’m leaving early tomorrow. Please sleep in. I love you.”

    Sam hesitates, looking a little confused, a little unsure. “Isn’t Kara joining you?” she asks eventually.

    Lena is so tired she must just look clueless enough to fool her best friend. “Kara?” she asks. “I didn’t think she was going to bed yet. Why?”

    Sam looks sad then, much sadder and far more tired than she should, on a night like this. “I love you too, Lena,” she says. “Please take care of yourself.”

    It’s Lena’s first night between these frozen sheets all over again, kicking her legs in an effort to get warm while goosebumps rush up and down her sides. A lack of sleep always makes her feel itchy all over, like going cold turkey, like little insects crawling underneath her skin. Kara takes her sweet time, but eventually Lena hears the sound of her (bare, because she’s not human) feet on the floor outside their room.

    Then she knocks. Lena resists the urge to throw one of Ruby’s snow boots at the door. “Come in, you idiot.”

    “I was hoping you were asleep,” Kara whispers back, entering and quietly sealing the door shut behind her. She’s wearing sky blue pajamas with tiny yellow ducks printed all over, a pattern Lena hadn’t thought would come in adult sizes. “You looked like you needed it.”

    “So you've mentioned,” Lena says. “Come here, you oaf. I need you to warm me up.”

    Kara does the most unexpected thing Lena has ever seen her do in the entire two days she’s known her, and there’s been some surprises along the way: she hesitates.

    Lena pushes upright, aware that it allows her blanket to fall down her shoulders, revealing the fact that she’s not wearing her cursed thermal underwear, or actually, any underwear at all. “What’s wrong?”

    Kara quickly averts her eyes, as if she’s never seen Lena naked, before. “I don’t want to like, assume that that’s what you’re asking—”

    “I’m asking you to get into my bed.” Lena shivers violently, pleased when Kara’s eyes drop to her chest seemingly without Kara realizing it, and linger there.

    “—but I don’t think we should have sex tonight.”

    Lena blinks. Oh. “Okay.”

    Kara flusters. “It’s just that. I don’t think you can give consent if you have a concussion.”

    Lena practices patience, again. She’s really getting quite good at it. “I don’t have a fucking concussion,” she growls, patiently.

    “And also, I’m your primary caregiver now. Unequal distribution of power, and all that.”

    Lena rubs her face in a last ditch effort to stay awake long enough to see the end of this exchange. “Kara,” she says. “It’s okay if you don’t want to have sex with me. I’d still really appreciate it if you could help me not die of hypothermia, however.”

    “Okay.” Kara is at her bedside immediately, then freezes again when Lena lifts her blankets to let her in. “Do you think—” she gestures down Lena’s body. “Could you maybe put some clothes on?”

    Lena comes close to pulling on the reindeer onesie, just for old times’ sake, but she’s well aware that the comfy fleece combined with the heat of Kara’s body would roast her in her sleep. She opts for a discarded t-shirt thrown over Kara’s chair, instead, and notes Kara’s happy blush as she climbs back into bed.

    “Bananarama?” Lena asks. “As in ‘Venus’ Bananarama? Seriously?”

    Kara flushes with righteous indignation. “Uh, ‘Cruel Summer’ Bananarama?” She pouts when Lena laughs. “What? It's still a total bop.”

    Kara effortlessly replicates their most comfortable position to date, Lena splayed across her chest, their hips slotted together. It’s nifty. It’s good. Lena is feeling much warmer, and only mildly tempted to assure Kara that sex is still on the table as far as she’s concerned. Just as a reminder, of course. She would never ask Kara to cross a boundary she’s set for herself.

    It’s not actually something she’s had to consider, before. She’s never been asked to put on clothes before taking a woman to bed. But Lena has learned a lot of things about herself this week. Like. Apparently she’s into blondes, now. That’s new. Also, half naked hugging? Far more intimate than sex. Who would have thought? (Her therapist, probably, who Lena really needs to start seeing more frequently. )

    Kara squeezes her tightly, nuzzles the top of her head. “Feeling okay?” she checks.

    Lena hums. “You’d better not be planning on taking this concussion thing any further,” she warns. “If you wake me up in an hour to check my pupillary response, I reserve the right to claw your eyes out.”

    “Actually that’s a misconception,” Kara says. “You need sleep to recover. Besides, I wouldn’t dare. You’re so sleep-deprived that the doctor actually thought you had brain damage.”

    “You were the one that gave her cause to suspect it. And thanks so much for that, by the way.”

    Kara’s sigh sounds a little too long-suffering for her taste. She starts to say, “I like you, Lena—”

    Lena’s panic is immediate and complete, not waiting for Kara to finish her sentence, zero to one hundred in point two seconds flat. It’s not an unusual feeling, she’s been dealing with episodes like this since her early teens, but she’s never experienced it in the arms of a far-too-familiar stranger telling her exactly what she wants, exactly what she doesn’t need.

    “—I wish you’d take better care of yourself,” Kara finishes, before Lena’s able to fully finesse her tailspin.

    Lena releases the breath she was holding, wills her blood to return to her extremities. “Sam told me the same thing,” she admits.

    “Of course she did.” There’s a smile in Kara’s voice. And then, casually, like it’s not the thing that will bring about the end of Lena’s carefully constructed sense of herself and the world she lives in, “We love you.”

    Lena thinks so long about the correct way to respond to that, listening to the sound of Kara’s heart, thudding steadily, fearlessly under her touch, that she’s asleep before she finds it — and when she wakes up, feeling miraculously rested, marvelously recovered, especially considering the room is still shrouded in the low light of early morning, Kara is gone.

    -

    Kara is outside, with Ruby, wearing her absurdly colorful snowsuit and her LET’S GET LIT beanie. Lena watches, unseen, from the kitchen window, a bowl of steaming coffee clutched tightly between her freezing fingers.

    She’s putting snow chains on Lena’s car.

    She wants Lena to be safe.

    Lena Luthor is nothing if not thorough. Before giving the go-ahead on any major business decisions, she makes a point of speaking with consultants from various fields, often searching for the third, or fourth, or fifth side of a story before bringing the hammer down. So naturally, during the forty-five minutes she’s been up, she’s checked CDOT for updates on the weather seven times already. She knows her gas tank is full, and now, that the Dodge is still running, that Sam chose not to follow up on her threat to sabotage it so Lena might extend her stay. She’s even gone so far as to make sure there’s no actual migraine looming, blinking into the early morning light now pouring in through the window, taking a whiff of the sharply lemon-scented detergent underneath the sink.

    The truth is, no one here requires an excuse for Lena not leaving here today, except for Lena herself — and she already has a pretty good idea why she’s so reluctant to get into that car.

    (It only has about fifteen percent to do with its interior’s putrid smell.)

    It’s just. When she looks at Kara — when Kara looks at her — everything inside her feels as hot as this coffee, bright as the sun’s first rays peeking out over the mountain range. And she can feel it all dimming the more she readies herself to leave this place behind, to return to National City and L-Corp’s eager arms.

    “Don’t jump!”

    Lena screams and flings her beverage, and the bowl right along with it — somewhere, anywhere, her reflexes frantic to free up her hands and face the threat.

    It’s Alex, obviously, and she’s clearly offended by Lena’s reaction. “I told you not to jump!”

    Lena presses her hand against her chest in an urgent attempt to restart her heart. “Aren’t you supposed to save people’s lives?” she yells. “You just took another two years off of mine!”

    “Sorry. Habit.” Alex crouches down and begins picking up the pieces of Lena’s bowl. “Mazel tov,” she says, grinning up at her.

    Lena huffs and joins her, waiting for Alex to clear the shards before deploying kitchen towels to mop up the tragic remains of her coffee.

    “You knew Sam was planning a proposal herself,” Alex says, observing her, her voice much milder now. “What was the rush? Why the theatrics?”

    Lena pulls herself upright, facing her sister-in-law-to-be head on. “Because I’m just like you,” she retorts. “A stubborn asshole with a worryingly dysfunctional idea of friendship.”

    The smile Alex gives her in return is devastatingly fond and, for a moment, the resemblance to her foster sister is striking. “Just so you know,” Alex says. “Between the two of us, Kara’s the better adjusted one.”

    “Really?” Lena deflects. “And you set such a high bar.”

    “Fuck you, Luthor.” Alex is grinning now. “Just, do all of us a favor and stop fooling yourself by thinking Kara is interested in your friendship, be it with or without benefits.”

    And Lena can’t look at her when she says it, because it aches, because she wants it, wants all of it, wants Kara. But wanting like this? Needing, like this? It’s a liability. Lena can allow her body to want hers, can understand and will absolutely indulge this acutely insatiable need to press herself against this goddess of a woman, to come undone in the palm of her hand. But a commitment beyond that? This comfort, this unconditional kind of frankness and whole-hearted acceptance? No. She needs to stand alone, steady and strong. That’s her taking care of herself. That’s how she keeps herself safe.

    Because she suspects, if she starts relying on someone else, really leaning on someone that way? She might never be able to hold herself up again.

    -

    Alex sees her out onto the driveway, shrugging on her coat. Lena feels more unsteady than ever in her trusted Louboutin's, but she’s foregone her original pencil skirt in favor of her new favorite (though garlic-scented) jeans for the journey home.

    Kara’s smile is as kind as always, small creases at the corners of her eyes, holding her gaze a little longer like she’s in on a secret Lena is too afraid to tell.

    “This should make sure you get to Denver safely,” Kara boasts, patting the Dodge’s hood and kicking a tire for good measure before presenting Lena with the keys.

    “Thank you, Kara,” Lena says. “I’ll see you around.” She smiles a little, like she’s trying to make a joke. “At the wedding, for sure.”

    Kara looks briefly stricken, but recovers quickly with a hopeful smile. “Perhaps a little sooner than that? Alex likes to plan ahead. She may take a year just drafting the guest list.”

    Lena can’t quite manage to meet her eye, fears being met with the earnestness of that gaze, already feeling like she’s breaking, like she’s going to do something profoundly foolish, like cry, or ask Kara for a hug. “This was fun,” she assures herself as much as she’s assuring Kara, and she’s like Alex, picking up broken glass, the careful way in which she’s choosing her words. “It was really good to meet you.”

    “Yeah.” Kara sniffs, digs the heel of her boot into the snow. She nods. “I had a good time, too.”

    On a wild impulse, Lena digs into her messenger bag for one of her business cards and, in a truly unprecedented move, scribbles her private number on the back before shoving it between Kara’s uncharacteristically clumsy fingers. She regrets it the second she does it, but it’s too late to take it back. “So that, you know,” she clarifies. “You know where to find me. If you want.”

    “I will.” Kara’s smile looks a little brighter when she takes the card. “I also hear your name is on the building.”

    “Not— exactly.” Lena smiles down at her hands, feeling an uncomfortable pressure behind her chest.

    “Stay warm out there,” Kara says, her voice soft and gentle, like the hug Lena wants her to pull her into.

    “I will try,” Lena tells her instead, digging her hands into the pockets of her jacket, tracing the car keys with the index finger of her left hand.

    They stand there, about a foot too far apart, the distance small but impossible to cross. It feels stunted, awkward, a piece of music cut off right after the bridge.

    She leaves, after hugging Ruby, and Alex, and even a surly bathrobe-clad Sam complaining it’s much too early for anyone to be awake.

    She leaves.

    It’s quiet, on the ride back, incrementally colder the further away she gets from the cabin, and Lena fiddles with radio stations until settling on the least offensive holiday classic she can find, which might actually be the definition of offensive, now that she’s listening to it more closely. Instead of her usual justified anger, however, she only feels wistful.

    How can you do these things to me?

    Chapter End Notes

    I promised you a happy ending. I assure you I will deliver.

    If Only In My Dreams

    Chapter Notes

    Quick recap, because let's face it, you probably need it: everyone’s keeping secrets, everyone’s placed their bets; Kara doesn’t know her vegetables, Lena doesn’t know her own heart. They still somehow managed to stumble into each other’s beds, but then Lena bounced right back out and all the way back to National City. I know, right?

    Lena Luthor is going to die alone.

    She’s not catastrophizing. She’s merely a reasonable woman, making a responsible choice. She prefers solitude, the safety, the simplicity of it. She'd decided on this solitary trajectory long before Kara Danvers barged into her life, pushing every single one of Lena’s buttons and her entire world off-kilter before leaving it just as abruptly — not calling, or texting, or even granting Lena the courtesy of letting her know she made it out of Colorado alive.

    Lena assumes she survived. The last week of L-Corp’s fiscal year have her on long conference calls with Sam every day, sometimes several times a day, and Lena imagines her best friend would probably have mentioned something if her fiancée's little sister had tumbled down a mountain and broken all of her bones, or, in a much more likely scenario, finally burned her mouth so badly it had led to her dying of starvation.

    It was a mistake, Lena understands now, sliding into the backseat of the town car waiting for her in the parking garage — a moment of weakness, giving Kara her number. She's grateful Kara seems to have recognized that too. Lena’s actually really impressed with her. Clearly she’s handling this whole thing much better than Lena is. She hasn’t called, and Lena, who has barely managed to survive a week of feeling her stomach twist itself in knots each time she’s checked her phone, unsure if she’d been hoping for a missed call or an empty inbox, should consider it a mercy. Luthors, even the ones born from a respectable man’s momentary lapse in judgment, aren’t built to maintain intimate connections with members outside of their family. Emotional attachments are messy. People are messy, constantly requiring things and making demands. Lena’s life is demanding enough.

    Besides, Lena notes as she inspects her make-up in the mirrored doors of the private elevator taking her up to her office, it’s not as if she’s had time to dwell on their quaint little entanglement. Not as if she misses her. Obviously. She’s spent literal hours in Kara’s company, it’s not as if any one person is able to impact her so profoundly in such a brief amount of time. Lena Luthor has more important things to attend to — most pressingly, at the moment, the intimidating stack of files Jess is carrying to her desk along with Lena’s 8 am Monday beet-and-ginger smoothie.

    Her assistant is scrutinizing Lena through a pair of semi-rimless glasses Lena hasn’t seen her wear before, a large reusable Starbucks cup in her other hand. Lena adjusts the collar of her shirt, knuckles ghosting against the long faded bruises Kara had kissed into her skin, and doesn’t think of the pumpkin spice latte Kara had her try. Doesn’t remember the sugary goodness of it, does not relate it to Kara’s own demeanor, to the soft creases that appear around her eyes when she smiles. Lena takes a heartening sip of her smoothie and flinches when the flavor hits her tongue, an unexpectedly unpleasant contrast to the sweetness of the memory.

    The eyebrow Jess raises as she appraises her boss is perfectly executed, and Lena wonders, not for the first time, if perhaps she has taught Jess too well. “Something wrong with your breakfast, Ms. Luthor?”

    “Stop that,” Lena chides. “If you’re comfortable enough to call me a dick over text, you can address me by my first name in the privacy of my office.”

    Jess narrows her eyes at her, a compact and impeccably dressed picture of undisguised disapproval. “Those vacation days were wasted on you,” she decides. “You look more high-strung now than you did before your little overnight adventure.”

    Lena gulps down another stubborn mouthful of what is supposed to be one of the few treats she’ll allow herself this week and pretends Jess’ choice of words doesn’t inspire a crystal-clear memory of Kara hooking her arms underneath Lena’s thighs and savoring her. She regrets her decision immediately when she’s met with another blast of blinding zest rather than the sweet earthy flavor she thought she’d grown so fond of.

    Lena’s mood brightens considerably when she notices the documents Jess has brought her all boast Maxwell Lord’s pompous letterhead. Normally this would have made an already miserable morning infinitely worse, but today, for what is likely the first time in recorded history, the sight sparks a genuine smile.

    “I won’t be needing these,” Lena says, sliding them back over to the far edge of her desk and sinking back in her chair, proud and petty and pleased with herself. “Sam will be dealing with the Lord account exclusively from now on.”

    “Ms. Arias?” Jess’ eyes go round with wonder. “What do you have on her,” she asks, “and how will you help me use it to my own personal advantage?”

    Lena smirks, contemplates giving her smoothie another chance before deciding to relish her victory instead and setting the paper cup next to the files for Jess to remove. “Clearly you’re both far too invested in what your boss does with her personal time.”

    “I'd argue we’re invested in making sure any of your time is personal,” Jess says primly, ignoring the fact neither she nor Sam has a proper grasp of what ‘personal’, as a concept, might entail. She reaches for Lena’s cup, the sleeve of her turtleneck pullover tugging up just enough to reveal a rose gold watch with an elegantly embossed navy blue dial.

    That’s definitely new.

    Also preposterous.

    “Is that— are you wearing a nautilus watch?” Lena yelps, grabbing her assistant’s wrist, and when Jess only hums and preens a little in response, a small private smile twitching around the corners of her mouth, “How? Are you freelancing for another overindulgent billionaire on the side?” Another, far worse possibility coming to mind, Lena lowers her voice. “Jessica Huang. Please tell me you’re not dating another Hemsworth brother.”

    “Ew, no.” Jess scoffs at her as if she’s said something insulting. “Everything I’ve got I bought it,” she sings, her voice a little thin but perfectly on key, “boys can't buy my love,” before dramatically tossing her hair over her shoulder.

    “You know,” Lena confides, staring shamelessly at the beautiful piece of jewelry adorning Jess’ wrist, “I got an email from Yadid in HR. They said if I give you another raise, you’ll actually be earning more than me.”

    Jess has the gall to laugh like she deserves it, but sobers and straightens a little when she sees Lena’s deadpan. It's a beat too late to be entirely convincing, however.

    Lena sighs. “I do pay you too much, don’t I?”

    “Probably, in all fairness, yes.” Jess takes in a fortifying breath. “Though I should maybe mention that at least half of this was funded by you and your handsome best friend’s exceptional pigheadedness.”

    Lena gawks at her. That can’t possibly be true. “I only bet you twenty bucks I’d get them to propose simultaneously. Either that’s the most exquisite knock-off I’ve ever seen, or Sam gambled away Ruby’s entire college fund.”

    “I know.” It’s gleeful. “Sam’s confidence in your thirst and her sister-in-law’s charm is a thing to behold.”

    Lena drops Jess’ hand and curses far too colorfully for such a bright early morning. “You bet that much money on me not getting laid?”

    “Mmm-hmm.” Jess lovingly strokes the bezel with her thumb. “It’s like Sam doesn’t know you at all.”

    Lena wilts a little at the words. Sam knows her far too well, is the problem, and the only reason Lena’s managed to convince her she doesn’t is that her best friend trusts her too much to even consider Lena might lie to her about who she has or hasn’t slept with. The revelation that Alex didn’t inform Sam of her fiancée's victory either is a little surprising, although Lena supposes she, Kara and Lena may all have simply landed on the same page — that it’s best to act as if nothing happened, so they can all get on with their lives.

    Lena should feel relieved. The absolute worst-case scenario she had imagined — the one that had almost successfully convinced her to keep Kara at arm’s length — was for their little ill-advised hookup to make things awkward between everyone involved. And they aren’t, not at all; Sam and Alex are so wrapped up in their post-engagement-pre-wedding bliss that they barely venture out of their little cocoon, Ruby flits in and out of Lena’s periphery, texting her science problems and perplexing memes, and Kara is where she was before, which is very simply not a part of Lena’s life.

    Lena should be happy.

    When Lena looks back up at Jess, her assistant is openly looking her over, apparently having picked up on the shift in Lena’s mood. Her expression softens some. “Was it really so awful,” she prods, gently, “spending some time with friends, away from work?”

    Lena is hit with a sudden lash of longing, feels it like a bruising in her chest, like the breathless thudding ache of being shot while wearing a bulletproof vest. She remembers the crackling fire, Ruby’s laughter, Sam choking on marshmallows and the fond grin Alex had given her picking up pieces of glass in the kitchen. Remembers Kara’s hands warm on her stomach and her voice against her ear on the aerial tram, quietly coaxing her away from the onset of a panic attack.

    She lets out a breath. “No,” she admits. “It was actually… unexpectedly wonderful.”

    The truth appears to come as a surprise to them both. The smile that blooms on Jess’ face in response warms her — that’s definitely it, Lena is certainly not blushing at her own confession — and Lena surprises herself a second time when she suggests, “Maybe next time you should come,” before considering the impossible implications of a next time.

    Jess sizes her up, gauging the air between them, then smiles, her dark eyes glinting dangerously. “Since I have my prize money already, Lena,” she says significantly, “maybe next time you should, too.”

    Lena wishes she’d held on to the rest of her disappointing smoothie just so she’d have something to throw at her assistant’s hastily retreating back.

    -

    A month passes, and Kara doesn’t call, and Lena’s good with it. She’s fine. After all, Lena Luthor is a woman of business. She doesn’t have time to reminisce. She has obligations.

    She might have forgotten all about Kara Danvers, in fact, if it wasn’t for Sam Arias, who — as if she's sensed Lena’s resolve — decides to launch an honest-to-god, utterly unnecessary unholy crusade in a fresh effort to make Lena lose her mind.

    -

    It starts with a subtle uptick in Sam’s Instagram posts.

    Lena only has the app on her phone because Jess installed it for her, along with Twitter’s echo chamber of despair and the lawless bewilderment that is Snapchat. She uses all of them under an unverified alias — her front-facing profiles are carefully curated by L-Corp’s PR team — and checks them only when she’s feeling reckless (occasionally) or bored (rare), or when she’s skipped dinner and her last glass of whiskey of the evening hits a little too hard (often).

    Sam posts sunsets and prettily arranged breakfasts, mostly, views of the city taken on a morning jog, with the odd #signalboost here and there. Alex makes an appearance or two, silhouetted by a candlelit dinner, a closeup of their hands showing off their engagement rings, three heads of hair (one auburn and two dark brown) poking out beneath a comfortable looking duvet. Sam doesn’t generally do selfies, so when one late Thursday night in March (is it March already?) Lena takes a sip of her drink and opens the app to a perfectly clear image of Sam’s wide grin, Lena is a little surprised.

    Her surprise turns to shock, and then into a violent coughing fit, when she realizes the person in the picture with her — blonde hair and light eyes forming a pleasing contrast to Sam’s dark features — is Kara.

    She has more freckles than Lena remembers — early spring in National City already rivaling the summers Lena grew up used to — but everything else is exactly the same, including the sympathetic pull Lena feels at the corners of her own mouth at the sight of Kara’s genuine smile, and the heat curling further down as her eyes track the arm Kara doesn’t have wrapped around Lena’s insufferable best friend, where the delectable curve of Kara’s bicep stretches the fabric of the t-shirt she is wearing.

    Lena bites her lip. It’s the one Lena had slept in, their last night together — the one with the Bananarama print.

    bootcamp with my bestie! Sam’s caption boasts, this girl’s endurance will give you a real workout, and Lena’s finger was already hovering over the like button, a treacherous reflex, and at some point during her desperate struggle for breath it has turned the exact same shade of red as Lena’s profound mortification.

    -

    It’s only the first shot fired, a warning, a courtesy really, before Sam begins waging an all-out war.

    Apparently Sam no longer goes on those morning runs alone. Every Tuesday and Thursday, like clockwork, there’s a new distracting image of Kara stretching her hamstrings, or flushed, drinking a Gatorade, the line of her throat lit lovingly by the amber morning sun. Lena fastidiously avoids the heart at the bottom of the posts, careful not to make the same mistake twice, but she knows her silence is just as damning, and Sam absolutely knows she’s watching.

    Lena Luthor is renowned for her impeccable professionalism. During her impressive career she has bravely and elegantly navigated stock market crashes, failed product launches and the discontinuation of her favorite brand of nitrile gloves. Even when the assistant district attorney herself had walked into Lena’s office two years ago and privately presented her with a fat stack of files containing irrefutable evidence of her brother’s corporate crimes, artfully covered up by the very same Lex Luthor who once had his little sister flown halfway across the globe just so she could celebrate her twenty-first birthday with him, Lena had barely missed a beat. She got to work, doing what needed to be done to not only mitigate the damage he had caused, but to make reparations wherever possible. Lena is conscientious, imperturbable, and she carries on.

    But her equilibrium has never once in her career been tested like this.

    Now, in between drawn-out business meetings and boardroom brawls, there’s Kara, rewarding herself with an ice cream cone at 7 am, a glimpse of her pink tongue setting the roots of Lena’s hair on fire. Kara in low-slung sweatpants and a crop top, the v-line of muscle trailing off underneath the waistband doing no doubt lasting damage to Lena’s cardiovascular system. One Saturday morning just after dawn, while Lena is chugging coffee in the backseat of her town car on the way to cardio ballet, Kara joins Sam for her Krav Maga class and is this time captured, lamentably, on a seven-second video reel that Lena plays twenty one times in a row before freezing and frantically googling whether Sam can see how many times she’s watched it and then, reassured, watching it another twelve times for good measure.

    It’s torture, pure and simple, and how Sam has thus far evaded trial at the international criminal court in The Hague Lena does not understand.

    Speaking of crimes against humanity, her mother manages to ambush her in the middle of one of these momentary lapses, an incoming call screen popping up one evening just as Lena is staring down at an uncharacteristically modest closeup of Kara’s downcast eyes, gently cradling a tiny Labrador puppy to her chest. look who made a new friend this morning! Lena reads, right before the first bars of Carmina Burana — the ringtone Sam had chosen for Lillian and Lena had never found reason to change — make her nearly bounce her phone out of her hands in a scramble not to drop it as well as her drink.

    “Mother,” Lena manages to answer evenly, gracefully sidestepping the pool of bourbon that’s now slowly spreading on the slate tiles of her kitchen floor. She’s blinking fast to dispel the lingering visual of Kara’s enamored expression, but Lillian’s disaffected timbre sobers her instantly.

    “I’m surprised you still have my number,” her mother muses. “I can’t recall a single instance where you've found reason to dial it.”

    “You know how it is,” Lena counters coolly, dropping a towel to soak up the remains of her drink. “Long days at the office, trying to keep the family company afloat after the prodigal son tries his hardest to sink it.”

    Lillian takes a moment before responding, but Lena knows better than to believe she has so easily won. It’s been a minute since Lena has had to face her mother in the flesh, but she’s able to picture her without any trouble at all, her face a perfectly smooth mask of detached appraisal.

    Lillian hums, studiously aloof. “I’m sure you’ll manage to turn things around soon,” she says, “Once you finally find your sea legs.”
