    A lesser adversary / more well-adjusted adult would probably balk at her mother’s condescension, but Lena’s training in navigating the obstacle course that is her mother’s idea of casual conversation rivals that of an Olympic athlete. She calmly reaches for a new glass, pouring herself a double this time. “Would you like me to put you through to my chief financial officer to discuss the record profit L-Corp turned this year?” she asks Lillian genially. “Since you clearly had better things to do than to read the annual report?”

    Lillian makes a faint sound of distaste, and Lena wonders if she will call Sam insufferable again, tell Lena what an imbecilic decision it had been to fire Lex’s CFO and hire her best friend instead, but she settles for a curt “No, thank you.”

    And then, as if it isn’t one of the furthest out-of-left-field curveballs Lillian has ever punted her — and there is an objectively storied history of them — she continues, nonchalant as ever, “I think it’s time for you to start dating again.”

    Lena stands in the wide expanse of her open-plan kitchen, a half-full tumbler clasped perilously between her fingertips, and blinks at the National City skyline twinkling across the bay. “What.”

    Lillian sighs impatiently. “You’ve been single for an unflatteringly long time, Lena. People are beginning to wonder what’s wrong with you.”

    A voice in the back of her mind that sounds suspiciously like Sam urges her to tell her mother you are what’s wrong with me, but Lena takes a deep drink, and it fades away obediently.

    Lillian seems to take her silence as petulance. “Don’t tell me you’re still mourning that little Sapphic sideslip you got so caught up in,” she croons. “What was her name? Aurélia?”

    In spite of herself, Lena feels annoyance flare hot at her neck at her mother’s goading. “Andrea.”

    “Isn’t that what I said?” Lillian asks. “No matter. What’s important is that it was almost two years ago. I will not allow my daughter to wallow so publicly any longer.” She takes a breath, huffs it out briskly. “Consider this an intervention.”

    Unbidden, a memory surfaces that Lena had long ago convinced herself must have been a bizarre fever dream, based on how utterly out of character it had been, her mother getting caught off her guard. Lena had gone home on some forgotten errand for Lex, six weeks after their father’s funeral, and found the mansion dark, the staff missing. Joni Mitchell’s 'Case Of You' was playing on Lionel’s old record player upstairs, and that’s where she had found Lillian, barefoot in a light summer dress Lena had never seen her wear before or since, somber and languid as if half a bottle deep, but no alcohol on her breath, and she’d said, "There’s no point to it now, is there? No point to any of it."

    “You never publicly dated anyone again after Father.” It’s a blurted thing, a Hail Mary, and Lena barely recalls where it came from.

    Lillian’s voice seems to crinkle at the corners. “I’m a widow, dear. And a powerful woman in my own right. Not all of us have earned the privilege of enduring singlehood.”

    “And yet you make it sound so easy,” Lena mutters into her glass.

    “Stop mumbling,” Lillian chides. “Honestly. Rebellion may have been a good color on you when you were younger, but your excuse of youthful foolishness expired years ago.”

    Lillian’s voice fades out for a moment, then returns, close and clear as if she’s right there, her intimidating frame looming over her. “I’ve forwarded you an article you may find… motivating,” her mother says vaguely. “And I trust you’ll show up for your next charity fundraiser. Maybe this time consider taking your stylist’s advice about asymmetrical designs. They’re so much more flattering for someone of your size. It may even save you from having to attend this event without a date.”

    Lena grits her teeth, feeling the muscle of her jaw working. “Why bother trying to outshine you, Mother?” she wonders. “I hear your outfit made quite the impression at the NCMC’s Children’s Hospital luncheon last month.” According to Lena’s Google alerts, Lillian had shown up wearing her Gucci blouse styled the wrong way around, the buttons running down the front rather than the back. The tabloids had called it a catastrophe.

    “That was intentional,” Lillian snaps, and Lena smiles at finally having managed to chip away some of her mother’s stony façade.

    She doesn’t get to enjoy her small triumph for long. “I did have the committee put a stop to the ridiculous company donation that awful CFO of yours signed off on,” her mother continues, as if it’s barely significant enough to mention. “What would the shareholders have thought if that had been allowed to go through? You really should keep a closer eye on that woman.”

    Lena grips her glass so tightly her knuckles have turned white. “People are counting on L-Corp’s donation,” she says, wincing at the irritation coloring her voice. “Some of those funds have already been allocated. There was an official announcement.”

    Lillian tuts, her voice dripping with disapproval. “You really should have considered that before making promises you can’t keep.”

    “Consider what?” Lena demands. “That Luthors can’t possibly do something that benefits someone other than themselves?”

    Her mother’s voice takes on a faux-warm tint, only making Lena’s hackles rise further. “You can’t give it all away, Lena,” Lillian soothes. “People might see it as an admission of guilt.”

    Lena laughs, a sharp, humorless bark. “Oh, no. What could be worse than acknowledging the truth?”

    ”You could stand to be a little more grateful,” Lillian nettles, “considering you’re still enjoying the benefits of your brother’s so-called crimes.”

    Lena breathes through an almost overwhelming impulse to scream and / or trade in all of her L-Corp shares for NFTs. “Manslaughter,” she says instead. “Embezzlement. Bribery of public officials. Gross negligence causing extensive environmental damage. Would you like me to continue? I keep a copy of Ashworth's Principles of Criminal Law at home in case another member of my family needs a refresher.”

    Lillian hums. “Are you still staying in that ostentatious penthouse on the waterfront?” she asks. “You’ve always had expensive taste. And I hear Frank is still your chauffeur. Such a loyal man.”

    Lena flinches. On the other end of the line, Lillian takes a sip of her own drink. Lena imagines it’s champagne.

    “Oh, look at the time,” her mother says. “I’m going to have to cut this short. Some of us have social engagements on Friday nights.”

    “Pity,” Lena says flatly. “It was such a pleasure.”

    “Remember what I said, dear,” Lillian presses right before disconnecting the call. “Tick tock.”

    Lena sinks down onto the white leather couch in her living room, knocks back the rest of her drink, and breathes.

    She needs Sam.

    She needs other friends, better friends, now that her best one has recently been too blinded by her own bliss to acknowledge the fact that Lena’s already been tasted — wait — tested, and deemed unlovable, even by the most promising romantic prospect she’s encountered in years.

    She could call Jess instead, but her assistant is a little too prickly when it comes to personal validation — the last time Lena had tried was after Lillian had delicately pointed out that Lena’s hair had looked a little thin in her latest Forbes profile ("You just can’t know with that particular set of genetics, dear") and Jess had very unhelpfully suggested maybe Lena was developing a bald spot from stress combined with her chronically tight ponytail. It had made Lena capital P Paranoid for weeks, obsessively googling the benefits of weaves vs extensions vs surgical intervention, and when she’d eventually asked Jess to book her an appointment with an endocrinologist to discuss the possibility she was entering premature menopause, Jess had sighed and silently put her through to Sam, instead.

    The article Lillian sent her isn’t an article at all. When Lena opens the attachment, she finds herself squinting down at a picture of a page from last week’s National City Enquirer featuring a paparazzi picture of Lena, the words RECLUSIVE BILLIONAIRE in the headline underlined twice by her mother’s red fountain pen.

    She swipes it away impatiently, and there, still open on her screen, is Kara; each individual lash and freckle perfectly, almost lovingly, captured by Sam’s powerful camera — another one of L-Corp’s patents Lex is at least peripherally responsible for.

    You’re a good person, Kara had told Lena, that bafflingly genuine look on her face.

    And Lena had almost believed her.

    Idiot.

    Lena manages to hold out for four more fingers of bourbon, but in the end she calls Sam anyway — now draped over her living room couch, one arm slung over her eyes, tired with life.

    “Sam,” Lena says the moment the call connects. “I think I want to sell L-Corp.”

    Sam sighs out a low chuckle, as if Lena didn’t just drop a bomb. It’s a little irritating. “You know,” she says, “I’d happily take it off your hands myself if I hadn’t just lost a wad of cash on what I was led to believe would prove to be a fantastic investment.”

    “About that,” Lena remembers, frowning, “How worried should I be that my chief financial officer gambled away her daughter’s entire college fund in one reckless bet?”

    “Eh,” Sam shrugs, “I have a rich fiancée. I’m a kept woman now.” And then, after barely a breath, “What is Lillian up to, these days?”

    Lena narrows her eyes at Sam’s cocksure tone. “How did you know?”

    “It’s the third month of the year and you're in hardcore self-destruct mode. It’s not hard to figure out. Is she still talking to the board behind your back? Ooh — did she give any more indication that she’s sleeping with that skeezy lawyer?”

    Lena rubs her forehead. Sometimes she thinks a migraine might be preferable to having to deal with Sam’s antics. “I am at this point begging you to stop bringing that up.”

    “Okay. Go on, let’s hear the psychological wounds she inflicted this time.”

    Lena takes a deep breath. “She told me I should start dating again.”

    Sam lets out a shocked laugh. “Lillian? Lillian Luthor?”

    “Apparently the National City Enquirer called me a hermit last week,” Lena explains. “She says people are going to think there’s something wrong with me.”

    “Fuck people,” Sam suggests, which is, bewilderingly, pretty much the exact same message Lillian seemed to be conveying to Lena during her phone call. “There’s nothing wrong with you, and you looked hot, anyway. You know what those yoga pants do for your ass.”

    “You saw the article?” Lena’s a little annoyed. “And you didn’t tell me about it?”

    Sam is quiet for a moment, confused. “Since when do you care what the tabloids are writing about you?”

    Since Kara might be reading them, Lena realizes. She feels her stomach fall between her feet at the thought that Sam may have shared it with her already, during one of their very many, very sweaty athletic endeavors. “Surprisingly,” she says, “Lillian had no comments on my ass.”

    “I’m still mad they never released your mug shots,” Sam sighs. “I bet you looked amazing in those, too.”

    Lena thinks of the picture on her driver's license, and how some things should never be shared with Sam no matter how many times she builds her back up after Lillian tears her down. “I’m sure Mother would disagree. She essentially called me an aging ingenue.”

    Sam snorts, and Lena can practically hear the corner of her best friend’s mouth ticking up in a crooked smile when she says, “That reminds me. Will there be a big birthday celebration this year? It’s her seventieth, is it not? She must hate that.”

    Lena feels her own mouth twitch in spite of herself. “Maybe we should throw her a very public party.”

    “You know,” Sam drawls, stretching the words out about three seconds longer than necessary, “if you’re interested in a little malicious compliance, I know just the person you should bring as your date to Lillian’s Seventieth Birthday Extravaganza.”

    Lena almost laughs, imagining introducing Kara to her mother — Lillian’s stoicism and sharp appraisal facing off with every joyful, golden inch of Kara’s blonde athleticism and her blind ‘I put my high school teaching career on hold to write a book’ ambition.

    Sam’s glee is contagious, but Lena feels the blunted knife of but she doesn’t want me sliding into her ribcage, and her amusement wilts away until only a cold void remains.

    “I know what you’re doing,” she tells Sam, “and it has to stop. Consider this your cease-and-desist.”

    “Is that why you’ve been giving me the silent treatment?” Sam asks. “It’s been almost two months since you reached out to me for anything other than to talk shop.”

    Lena tries not to take it as the sulking teenager the observation makes her out to be. “I’m allowing you your space as a newly engaged woman.”

    “And I’m doing my job as your second in command,” Sam counters. “Keeping an eye out for the bottom—” she pauses significantly, “—line.”

    Lena groans. “I should have known so much better than to make my ex the CFO of my company. It makes you far too powerful.”

    “I’m not your ex,” Sam argues. “I’m your very current, very best, and very handsome friend, and I miss you.” The words are simple, genuine. “This newly engaged woman still needs her best friend.”

    It rubs at her, the little raw patch inside of her chest usually reserved for fond memories of her brother. Lena hesitates, picks at an already torn cuticle. “Actually, Sam,” she decides, her heart beginning to pound in her chest. “I’ve been meaning to tell you—”

    — everything, Lena intends to say, intends to tell her every single embarrassing, vulnerable thing; how Kara knocked the wind out of her with her kindness, fucked her more earnestly and devotedly than Lena Luthor expects to ever be fucked again, and how they’d both decided — Kara a little more cleanly than her, but still — to leave it at that, and then Sam will stop, knowing the whole thing has already played out and ended, and Lena will be able to look at her notifications again without risking an aneurysm.

    But Sam interrupts her, clearly misreading what Lena’s about to say. “Save it,” she says, “it’s okay. I just want to remind you that you are allowed to do things — and people! — for fun, sometimes. I’m not going to be another person telling you how to live your life.” She pauses, seems to reconsider. “Except you’re coming over Sunday morning. I’m taking you and Ruby hiking.”

    “Absolutely not,” Lena scoffs.

    “You can borrow Ruby’s old boots. She just grew into a new pair.”

    “Sam,” Lena tells her kindly, “I say this from the bottom of my heart: no.”

    “Come on,” Sam pleads. “Don’t you think it would be good to spend some time with normal people?”

    “Sure,” Lena says, pretending to consider her suggestion, “but I don’t think I know any of those. Do you?”

    Sam exhales impatiently. “Fine. Breakfast at our place then. Ruby’s been asking after you too. We’ve all been worried.”

    “Really,” Lena remarks dryly. “Alex. Has been worried about me.”

    Sam takes a moment too long to answer. “Sure.”

    It’s not the worst way to spend her Sunday morning, Lena thinks. She does need to talk to Sam, and doing it face to face — soberly, this time — may be a better idea anyway. “Okay,” she says. “But I’m cooking.”

    “You’re also bringing the groceries,” Sam informs her. “We’ve been— busy.”

    Lena is almost proud of her best friend for insinuating rather than oversharing, this time.

    “Also wine,” Sam continues.

    Lena smiles. “I thought we were having breakfast.”

    “We live in the mountains,” Sam explains. “It’s always wine o’clock.”

    -

    Lena blinks awake that Sunday from an already half-forgotten dream-memory of Kara’s mouth hot against her thighs. Her phone informs her it’s at 3% battery power, that it’s 6.43 am, and it’s empty, blissfully, peculiarly, in the space where the notification that handsomesam just posted a photo would usually be.

    In retrospect, Lena really should have recognized that for the red flag that it was.

    She takes a shower, grabs the groceries she had delivered yesterday, pours herself a mug of dry cheerios she can’t remember buying and snacks on them in her Toyota Prius Prime, which she drives herself, Mother, thank you very much, all the way out of the city, through the suburbs (still mostly slumbering on this early Sunday morning) and into the hills until she finally pulls into the driveway of Sam and Alex’s Spanish-style home.

    She grabs the paper bags with groceries from the trunk, sets them on her hip to knock on the door, and waits.

    She knocks again.

    There’s no answer.

    Lena fumes for a minute, filled with righteous irritation and with no place to put it. For a second, it’s so quiet Lena can hear the birds that tend to gather in the Ficus trees in their backyard.

    And then she hears yelling from inside the house.

    It spurs Lena into action immediately, her instinct to freeze conquered at long last after so many life lessons learned. She leaves the bags by the door, avoiding the windows as she soundlessly rounds the house, relieved when she finds the sliding door to the back patio unlocked. She opens it as quietly as possible and steps into the kitchen.

    It’s not breaking in, not exactly, not if she’s acting out of concern for her friends.

    The kitchen is cool, empty, pristine. “Sam?” she ventures, hushed. “Ruby?”

    Another sound rings out, and Lena is upstairs in the space between two heartbeats when she recognizes it as Ruby’s voice.

    “Leave me alone!” Ruby yells, and for a moment Lena hesitates, unsure now whether Ruby is actually arguing with some unseen assailant or if it's Lena herself who’s being addressed.

    But then someone is behind her, grabbing her arm, and Lena whirls around, panicked, ready to scream, wishing she’d taken Sam up on her offer to teach Lena some self defensive moves—

    It's Alex. “Alex.” Of course. “Jesus Christ.”

    Even through her boneless, breathless relief, Lena is aware of Alex’s downtrodden appearance. Her hair is sticking up on one side, like she hasn’t seen a mirror since she first got up, and she’s still in her pajama pants, barefoot. Lena notes with immense amusement that Alex’s toenails are painted a particularly garish shade of pink.

    She’s also wearing her tank top backwards and inside out.

    Alex keeps a firm grip on Lena’s arm, as if she’s afraid Lena might run if she lets go. “Lena,” she hisses, sounding more desperate than Lena thinks she’s ever seen her before, “Thank fuck.” She promptly begins maneuvering Lena down the hallway, closer to Ruby’s door, explaining absolutely nothing.

    “I told you to leave me alone!” Ruby yells.

    Lena freezes abruptly, prompting an unhappy grunt from Alex when she stumbles into her back.

    Alex sighs. “Ruby, baby,” she pleads sweetly through tightly gritted teeth, still clinging to Lena like she’s a life raft, “if you don’t tell me what’s going on, I can’t help you.”

    “I don’t need your help,” Ruby yells from her bedroom, “and I’m not a baby!”

    “Uh, so, here’s the thing,” Lena says softly, now a mere two feet away from Ruby’s door, “I’m here for breakfast, and also maybe a little bit to yell at Sam. But it sort of sounds like there’s already enough of that going on.”

    “Don't you leave me,” Alex warns. “Please. Sam was supposed to be back an hour ago, but I’m beginning to think it was a mistake giving her a heads up about—" she glares at Ruby’s bedroom door, “this whole situation.”

    Lena hesitates. “This really feels like more of a family matter,” she tries.

    Alex is regarding her like she's a little bit irked at her for trying to escape her clutches, or like she’s just generally not very fond of her right now. Lena supposes she understands, what with Lena sleeping with her sister and all — although Alex had seemed to be coming around to the idea, the last time they spoke.

    Alex blows a lock of hair from her face, glinting a fiery red in the early morning light. “Don’t act like you aren’t part of this family, Luthor,” she grits out.

    And it’s just— Lena knows Alex is only saying it to get her to not run out the door and drive straight back to her apartment and return to her bed, but god damn her, it’s an effective move. A tiny seed of joy sprouts in the hollow of Lena’s chest, burrowing tiny tendrils of warmth into her coldest, darkest parts.

    “Besides,” Alex glares, “You owe me one. I’m still mad at you.”

    Lena frowns. “What are you talking about?”

    Alex ignores her. “Honey,” she calls out, “Your aunt Lena is here to see you.”

    Lena sighs, collects herself. She steps forward, taps her knuckles gently against the door. “Hey Ruby,” she calls out softly, “good morning.”

    Ruby is quiet for a moment. Then Lena hears a tiny, slightly watery “Hi.”

    Lena traces a knot in the wood with her thumb. “You know, I’ve just had the worst week,” she confides. “Do you think maybe I could come in and talk to you about it?”

    “Your week was the worst?” Ruby asks dubiously. “Why? Did someone call you a freak and talk about you behind your back?”

    Lena grimaces. “That — actually pretty much exactly sums up what happened,” she admits. “So trust me, I understand perfectly why that would make you so upset.”

    “I’m not upset,” Ruby insists, her voice shrill and teary, and sometimes Lena entertains the thought that maybe Sam lied to her about her daughter’s parentage, that maybe Ruby has a little bit more of Lena in her than can be explained away by prolonged exposure alone.

    (It’s a much more preferable scenario than facing the unfortunate possibility that Lena has the emotional maturity of a fourteen-year-old.)

    Alex has slumped to the floor beside her, feet planted in front, back against the wall. She closes her eyes, crosses her fingers and shakes her head. “Was it—" she asks, looking at Lena like she’s being actively tortured, “the boycrush?”

    It’s quiet on the other side. Alex is holding her breath, frozen in terror.

    “No,” Ruby says in a small voice.

    Alex drops her hands, her entire body sagging with relief.

    “… Aiden’s not my ‘boycrush’. He’s a buttfaced dickwad, and—” Ruby seems to steel herself — “he isn’t worthy of my time.”

    Alex’s eyes find Lena’s. She looks like she’s aged five years since she first sat down. “You know,” she tells her, “I’d tell her to cool it, with the language, but that’s actually a surprisingly accurate description of the kid.”

    Ruby’s voice sounds closer to the door now, and marginally less agitated. “You’re making fun of me,” she accuses.

    “Oh, sweetheart,” Lena hushes, “We would never. In fact I have just the thing that I think will make us both feel better.”

    To Lena’s pleased surprise, the lock snicks, and then the door opens just enough to reveal Ruby’s pale, red-nosed little face. “Pancakes?” she chances.

    Lena grins at her, then tucks the girl tightly to her chest. “Even better,” she says, pressing a kiss into her hair. “Animal pancakes.”

    -

    They regroup in the kitchen, Ruby still looking a little red around the edges, Alex like she’s just come home from war. Lena brings in the groceries while Alex texts Sam again, and when Lena starts mixing ingredients, Ruby starts talking, and then just. Kind of doesn’t stop.

    Lena Luthor has been called a mathematical genius before. She has doctorates in Computational Science and Mechanical Engineering as well as an MBA in Biotechnology. As a child, she received extensive tutoring in Greek and Latin and she’s fluent in German and Mandarin to the point where she can hold her own conducting business with L-Corp subsidiaries in Munich and Beijing. Her grasp of Russian is comfortable enough to read Dostoevsky’s work untranslated. In boarding school she’d been briefly infatuated with her drama teacher, familiarizing herself with the ins and outs of stage direction, choreography and major versus minor character dynamics in an effort to gain her approval.

    And yet, even with this objectively impressive list of qualifications, Lena is no match for Ruby Arias’ breakneck narration of the modern social tragedy playing out over a single week at Davis Middle School.

    Ruby manages to lose Lena approximately two run-on sentences and seven characters in. Fortunately Alex appears to be at least marginally more familiar with the names she mentions — half of which seem to be some sort of variation on the name of former-boycrush, current-buttfaced-dickwad Aiden — so Lena can focus on creating the most spectacular breakfast the world has ever seen. And it’s important work — Ruby’s mood lifts a tiny bit further with every pancake Lena puts on her plate, her frantic energy settling down a little more with each scoop of strawberry jam Alex adds onto it, and by the time Ruby’s plate is empty, she is smiling.

    “So,” she says, turning to face Lena, narrowing her eyes in question, “Who’s the jerk that called you names?”

    “Um,” Lena barely has time to stammer, before there’s the sound of the front door opening.

    “Honeys, I’m home!” Sam calls out from the hall.

    “We’re in here,” Alex calls back, and then Sam is in the kitchen, greeting Lena with such thrilled delight it can only mean catastrophe is about to strike.

    “We stopped by Big Belly Burger to bring home coconut cream shakes,” she grins, not taking her eyes off Lena. “Alex’s texts made it seem like it might be a matter of life and death.”

    … We?

    Kara steps out from behind Sam, her eyes on the tray of milkshakes she’s carrying. “There’s literally nothing a coconut cream shake can’t fix,” she says brightly, before spotting Lena and promptly depositing a gallon of it on Alex’s previously pristine kitchen floor.




    Chapter End Notes

    I'm just a girl, standing in front of a reader of no specified gender, asking them not to murder her.

    Do You See What I See?

    Chapter Notes

    Deep breaths.

    It’s. Well.

    It’s about as bad as Lena had anticipated it would be, seeing Kara again.

    First, there’s coconut cream shake absolutely everywhere; splashed on the saltillo tile floor, splattered up against the sage green kitchen counters, trickling slowly down the tan skin of Kara’s sculpted, downy legs. Lena is being made to perceive that she’s wearing shorts — a well-worn, soft-looking pair that’s loosely held around Kara’s hips by a drawstring Lena’s fingers twitch at, even from where she’s standing at the stove, twelve feet and three months of silence stretched wide between them.

    In a raw, heart-stopping moment, Kara’s eyes catch Lena’s own.

    Lena takes a breath, and watches as Kara does the same. The top she’s wearing is lamentably sleeveless and just tight enough around her chest that Lena can make out the first set of abs that lies beneath. Lena is completely normal about it, although it does take her a moment too long to become aware of the chaos that’s erupted around them.

    Sam and Ruby both got caught in the splash zone, and are having very different reactions to that fact. Sam is cracking up while her daughter is yelping, slip-sliding on socked feet to the kitchen sink in order to rinse off her cream-covered sneakers. Alex is absolutely not having it, take that to the back porch faucet, thank you very much. In between ushering Ruby out the patio doors and sending nervous looks between Kara and Lena, she is shooting absolute daggers at Sam — who is, at this point, nearly pissing herself laughing.

    Kara hasn’t moved. She’s anchored amidst the anarchy, staring at Lena like a dying man in the desert. Lena feels like water — dense, and briny, and possessing unusually high surface tension.

    “I’m sorry,” Kara stammers, and Lena has a feeling it’s not about the shakes, which she really should be apologizing for. “I didn’t realize,” Kara says. “Sam didn’t mention—”

    “I bet she didn’t,” Alex snarls from the sink. She lobs a wet mop at her fiancée’s head — for the milkshake or the accident Sam is about to have, Lena doesn’t know — and although it’s aimed right at Sam’s obnoxious mug, Sam manages to catch it as if it had been a lovingly blown kiss. “Love you too, babe,” she winks, before bending down to wipe off her sandals.

    Alex is fuming, an uneasy ally to Lena’s own ire. Kara is still staring, cheeks almost as pink as Alex’s toenail polish. Every beat that passes silently between them makes Lena feel more out of place. Why is she even here? What was she thinking, allowing herself to fall back into the delusion that, within this perfect little biosystem Sam’s created for herself, Lena could ever be anything other than an outsider, an external object waiting for the foreign body response to kick in and reject her?

    “It’s fine,” Lena lies, reverting to her usual icy poise. Luthors wobble, but they don’t fall down. “I wasn’t told you would be joining us today, either.”

    “No kidding,” Alex says, with a tired roll of her eyes that Lena doesn’t understand.

    “We, um.” Kara’s remorseful eyes flit from her sister back to Lena as she pulls at her own fingers, mesmerizing Lena briefly with the interplay of tendon and bone beneath the skin. “We do yoga, Sunday mornings.”

    Lena is achingly aware.

    And therefore also furious with herself for so feeble-mindedly wandering right into Sam’s trap.

    “So,” Kara smiles, a little uneven, a little unsure, still dripping ice cream onto the kitchen floor, “how have you been?”

    And no. Lena is absolutely not doing this. Not with her.

    Because it’s Kara, and her ludicrous hair, and her eyes, and her— everything else. Lena doesn’t even think that she has it in her — a deeply insulting, thoroughly bewildering realization — to perform one of those stilted, awkwardly polite exchanges with someone she’d believed to be so painfully genuine.

    “Busy,” Lena says simply, pointedly, keeping her gaze level and guarded. “I imagine you have been, too.” Too busy to pick up the phone, even.

    Kara starts fidgeting again, her expression just edging towards worry when Sam jumps to her aid. “Kara’s off building worlds,” she winks, clueless bastard that she is, “while Lena’s ensuring our current one’s continued existence.”

    Kara grins at Lena at that, eyes crinkling at their corners. “Ever the hero,” she says, and it’s too. Fucking. Much.

    “I should go,” Lena says, gracelessly. There’s something inside her chest that’s not working as it should, something chafing and grinding where it should be smooth, and the way Kara’s face falls in response to her announcement is the exact opposite of helpful.

    “Hey, no, wait,” Sam tries, taking a precarious couple of steps toward her on the ice rink that is now her kitchen floor.

    “Let her go, Sam,” Alex says sharply. “You’ve done enough.”

    Lena sends her a grateful look, but Alex meets it with narrow-eyed rancor. It reminds Lena of the way she used to treat her when Alex and Sam had first started dating — brimming with distrust, bordering on outright hostility — and the realization that their hard-won camaraderie can be so easily rolled back makes Lena blink at the sudden heat prickling behind her eyes.

    Ruby steps in front of her, looking between the four of them and frowning. She wrinkles her nose. “Why are you all being so weird?”

    Lena gives Sam a withering look before slanting her lashes down at Ruby. “Because your mother is my buttfaced dickwad, honey,” she explains matter-of-factly, “and she should know when to take no for an answer.”

    Alex looks at her, alarm and scandalized reproach flickering on her face in quick succession. She puts her hands on Ruby’s shoulders, gently steering the girl out of Lena's warpath. “What—” Ruby asks, but Lena is already gone, storming down the hall.

    Sam’s footsteps slap after her, wet and sticky on the terracotta tile. “Come on, Lena, stay,” she barters. “I’m sorry. Look, I didn’t mean to set you up when we talked on the phone. It just worked out so well—”

    “Nothing is working out well, Sam,” Lena bites, spinning around with one foot out the front door. Sam catches the latch to keep it from slamming into her chest. “Because she doesn’t—”

    Lena presses her lips together, clenches her jaw. A couple of strides behind her best friend, Kara has stepped into view, somehow managing to look both guilty and confused at the same time.

    “We’re not having this conversation here,” Lena decides. “Enjoy your Sunday.”

    “Lena, please.” They wrestle for the front door a moment, but Sam won’t let go, so Lena just leaves, stomping down the driveway and back to her vehicle.

    Before she slams the car door shut behind her, Lena hears Sam call out to her fiancée, “A little help?”

    “Oh, I am not helping you clean any of that up,” Alex answers her. “This is your mess.”

    -

    Lena doesn’t talk to Sam for a week and a half. She removes Instagram from her phone, ignores Sam’s calls to her private number, leaves her messages on read and instructs Jess to only allow her to be put through to her office if Sam can provide physical proof that it’s an L-Corp related emergency.

    Which is why it’s such a shock when on Wednesday morning, 11am, Lena is startled out of her bubble of quiet concentration by her office doors being thrown open wide and Jess — tiny, furious, perfectly put together — blazing through, followed closely by Samantha Arias. Her best friend saunters in with her hands in her pantsuit pockets, casually surveying Lena’s office before making herself comfortable on Lena’s couch, crossing her legs at her ankles and picking up some decorative trinket to play with.

    “Are you fucking kidding me?” Jess demands. She’s marched all the way up to Lena’s desk, holding out her phone in clear accusation. “This is the woman Ms. Arias has been trying to set you up with?”

    Lena almost has to roll her chair back to keep her assistant’s phone from colliding with her face. She smooths her features into a mask of perfect indifference before she glances at the screen, which is, much to Lena’s private consternation, displaying Kara’s personal Instagram account — the very one Lena had bravely resisted visiting, in spite of Sam tagging it incessantly.

    “Really, Jess?” Lena says, pointedly returning her attention to her work. “Cyber stalking? I thought you’d given that up for Lent.”

    Sam snorts.

    “Ms. Luthor,” Jess says solemnly, “I swear on baby Jesus, if you’re not going to fuck this woman, I am.”

    Lena rolls her eyes up at her assistant with severe skepticism. “You told me you were a Kinsey zero.”

    “Well not anymore, obviously.” Jess is looking at her phone like it’s a new religion. “Holy shit.”

    “So Lena,” Sam finally speaks up, smiling benevolently. “How are you doing? You look well. Very open, very accessible.” She sweeps her eyes over at Jess. “Not at all like you’re in emergency meetings all day and unable to take my calls.”

    Lena picks up her phone, fingers hovering over the speed dial. “You have ten seconds to leave my office,” she says.

    Sam smirks. “Or what?”

    “Or I’m calling security to have you both removed from the premises.”

    Sam has the audacity to laugh. “I own half this company.”

    Not exactly true.

    Jess is actually looking a little intimidated, her eyes flitting between Sam and herself. “And I… know things,” she blurts.

    Unfortunately that one is a hundred percent correct.

    Lena gazes at her assistant a moment longer, but when Jess sets her jaw and bravely meets her eye, Lena relents. “Alright,” she says. “You can have it, it’s all yours. I’ve been meaning to move, anyway.” She closes her laptop and grabs her purse from underneath her desk, pulling it into her lap. “I was thinking Canada,” she muses. “I’ll go off-grid, renounce the Luthor name. People will know me only as the wicked witch of the woods.”

    “The West,” Sam corrects her, bafflingly.

    “What?” Lena frowns. “No. Canada’s up—”

    “No, it’s the Wicked—” Sam shakes her head. “God, never mind. But while we’re on the topic of your evil nature — Jess, on a scale of one to what the actual fuck, where would you rank Lena giving Kara a fake phone number?”

    Lena’s stomach swoops so dangerously at the preposterous accusation that it distracts her entirely from her dramatic exit strategy.

    Jess flicks her eyes up at Lena, sharply suspicious. “You gave her your number?”

    Lena freezes, trying desperately to think of a way to make a conversational U-turn. She blinks. Entirely too many times. “Kara?” Blink. “... No?” Blink-blink. Oh for fuck’s sake, Luthor.

    Jess stares at her, silent. Sam just waits, tossing the trinket in the air and catching it again.

    Lena deflates, sweeping an inconspicuous hand over her eyes. “Fine. Yes.”

    Sam sighs, deeply disappointed. “Oh, honey. That’s cruel, even for you.”

    Lena flusters at the implication. “I didn’t give her a fake one! I’m not— is that what she told you?”

    Sam purses her lips in dry disapproval. “Alex is never going to forgive you for making her have the ‘she just wasn’t that into you’ conversation with her sister.”

    Had that been the source of Alex’s animosity when Lena was at their house last week? “Sam, I have no idea what you’re talking about.”

    “I’m talking about you getting that poor girl’s hopes up for absolutely no reason.” Sam eyes her. “Seriously, who raised you?”

    “I did no such thing,” Lena contends, bristling at Sam’s nerve to even hint at Lillian’s influence on her character.

    “Oh really?” Sam‘s brow knits in defiant disbelief. “Then why was I forced to sit through that sad, awkward family breakfast last week, desperately trying to steer the topic away from how Lena ‘superhero’ Luthor is probably far too busy to date at all, and even if she wasn’t, so far out of poor little old Kara’s league that she may as well be from a different planet?”

    “That was your fault, putting me on the spot like that,” Lena says, feeling vulnerable and fraught. “And you’ve got it all wrong. I did give Kara my number,” she glowers at Sam, “my real number. And she never used it.” She sets her shoulders, juts out her chin. “If anyone is entitled to a modicum of friendly commiseration, it’s me.”

    And, oh. Now that Lena’s said it out loud like that, it looks like there might be a slight possibility that she does feel a little hurt — a tiny bit burnt, maybe, just around the edges.

    The corner of Sam’s mouth tugs up in a small, disbelieving smile. “Fuck,” she says. “I knew it. I knew you’d fall for her.”

    “Wait,” Jess says. “So you’ve been waiting for her to call, and all the while this living, breathing thirst trap selfie has been silently pining after you?”

    “Would you stop?” Lena begs her. “Kara isn’t pining.”

    “She certainly hasn’t been doing it silently,” Sam says, her dark eyes sparkling. “She brought you up seven times on our flight from Denver to National City, and our seats were nowhere near each other.”

    Lena stares at her, disbelieving. “Then why didn’t she just call me?”

    Jess, pragmatic as ever, doesn’t miss a beat. She holds out her hand. “Give me your phone.” Lena glances at the one on her desk, but Jess glowers at her. “Your private phone.”

    Lena slides it from her purse, hesitating before handing it over, feeling stupid.

    Jess taps furiously at the screen until she turns it to face her, displaying Lena’s own number. “Write it down.”

    Lena flounders. “Jess, I don’t—”

    Jess is unyielding, giving the pen on her desk a quick pointed look. “Write. It. Down.”

    Lena frowns, but dolefully jots her number down on the back of something unimportant, sliding it over when she’s done.

    Her assistant rolls her eyes so hard at her inscription that Lena almost flinches in sympathy. “You see?” Jess scoffs, turning to Sam, then back at Lena. “You see?”

    Lena doesn’t see.

    Jess jabs the paper with one very tiny, very furious, perfectly manicured index finger. “This. Is not a four. This is a nine.”

    “Um.” Sam frowns, joining them at the desk. “No,” she says, pointing at a different digit, “that is how Lena writes a nine.”

    “That is actually a seven,” Lena sighs, pinching her brow, realization dawning.

    Jess does an alarmed double-take between Sam, Lena’s handwriting and her phone, then drops her forehead in her hand. “Lena Luthor, ladies and gentlemen,” she groans, “the world’s first illiterate genius.”

    An email notification pops up on Lena’s screen minutes after she’s finished wrestling a gloating Sam out of her office, Jess conscientiously returning to her desk. It contains only ten digits, and the words Learn to write, Luthor.

    -

    Here’s the thing: Lena Luthor doesn’t like being the one who makes the first move. Growing up in her brother’s shadow may have been rough, but it had also allowed her a little bit of latitude. As Lex’s baby sister, she was never asked to take the lead or to pioneer anything, never assumed to aspire to the kind of greatness her brother had always been expected to attain. Her father had preferred her docile — perfect, but pliant; unmatched, but polite. Even in fencing, evasive moves were where Lena really shined; toward the end she’d become so renowned for her phenomenal stop hits that her mother had begun calling her a one trick pony. Lena may have adjusted excellently to her role as CEO, fast learner that she is, but she has always preferred problem solving, tackling issues as they arise. Given a choice in the matter, shameful as it may be to admit, she’d choose reaction, rather than action, any day.

    So Lena drags her feet, for days. Stares at Kara’s number, leaning against her kitchen counter completely sober or sitting on the bench at the foot of her bed, two tumblers deep. Digs her fingernail into the rubber of her OtterBox Defender as she stands on her balcony at night, her high heels discarded, the ocean breeze whipping her hair around her face.

    She knows she needs to call Kara. She has to let her know that although Lena is a Luthor, and therefore in possession of a congenital capacity for cruelty, she never intended to inflict it upon Kara. But, Lena considers, that was this time. What is going to happen next, when she really lets Kara down?

    If their brief, catastrophic exchange at Sam and Alex’s house last week taught Lena anything, it’s that she had been right to consider Kara off-limits in the first place. Not only has she lost the ability to be in the same room as her without acting like an idiot — an experience apparently shared by Kara — she’s also been offered a nasty appetizer for the situation she would find herself in should she mess up for real.

    Kara’s crestfallen, coconut-cream-covered confusion now makes Lena ache with guilt, especially in the context of the picture Sam had painted of Kara excitedly hopping down the airplane’s center aisle to gush about her, only to return home and discover the phone number she’d been given didn’t exist.

    And then there’s Alex, who looked ready to disavow her last Sunday, no questions asked. Lena is intimately familiar with Alex’s protective streak, and privately thrilled that Sam and Ruby have someone like that in their corner, but Lena has been on the receiving end of it once before and she’s not ready to end up there again — not eager to learn which side Sam will choose when it comes to that.

    And in this current situation Lena barely carries any blame. How much will it hurt when Lena inevitably ends up disappointing Kara for real? Who will she have left when she fails her, when Kara eventually pushes her away?

    In the end, Lena decides a text message is heroic enough. Her fingers are trembling when she drafts it, her heart beating so fast that autocorrect almost sabotages her effort entirely, but she’s feeling far more settled now that it’s clear to her what she has to do:

    She should try to forge a friendship with Kara.

    It’s the only solution Lena can see. Certainly Kara will agree. They can navigate their way back to safer terrain, find a place where they feel comfortable in each others’ presence again, without the looming threat of cross-wiring expectations and people getting hurt.

    She writes, So it has been brought to my attention that I may have given you a wrong number. Clean, concise, unpresumptuous. Good.

    Much to Lena’s disorientation and distress, Kara responds almost immediately.

    Lena?

    Breathe in peace. Exhale stress.

    Yes, Lena confirms. I believe I owe you an apology.

    No bubbles appear to indicate Kara is writing her back this time, and for a moment Lena almost relaxes, allowing herself to believe in the tranquil fantasy that maybe they’ll be able to leave it at that. Guilt absolved, balance restored.

    But then an incoming call pops up, nearly stopping Lena’s heart.

    Inhale stress, breathe out— wait—

    Lena fumbles behind her for the back of her couch, walking backwards until she finds it. She plops down onto it, digging her fingernails into the leather, and answers, eloquently, her voice sounding not even a little bit breathless, “Hey.”

    “It’s okay,” Kara says, her voice mild with mercy and no small measure of relief. The sound of it hooks into Lena’s lower belly and tugs. “I figured something was fishy when you called me baby and tried to sell me a gram of cocaine.”

    Lena blinks. “… Excuse me?”

    Kara chuckles, a sound so warm and familiar that it returns Lena to their bedroom in Sam’s cabin in an instant. “I mean, we haven’t really graduated to pet names, so that was a dead giveaway that I wasn’t texting you. And even if we had, I couldn’t really see you going with ‘baby’.”

    Darling, Lena thinks, unbidden, struck by how immediately right it feels. It sweeps her inconveniently off-balance, already overwhelmed by having Kara’s voice in her ear again after months of trying very hard not to remember what that was like. Just like that, it’s all rushing back — the sensation of Kara’s breath against the shell of her ear, her mouth hot at her nape, soft lips on her breast, her tongue moving down—

    Friends, Lena reminds herself sternly, moving her strangling grip from the couch to the meat of her thigh. “What’s wrong with ‘baby’?” she demands, distracted and a little too argumentative, a ham-fisted attempt to claw her way back to shore.

    Kara hums in brief consideration. “Nothing,” she allows, casual and light and not at all preparing Lena for the absolute annihilation of her composure when Kara continues, “just seems like that would be my thing, instead.”

    Kara hinting at a situation in which she might call Lena ‘baby’ brings about a wave of lunacy so lascivious it threatens to tip Lena backwards over the couch, rolling her onto her hand-knitted, Scandinavian wool rug. She drives her nails a little deeper into her skin, now hanging on for dear life, covering her gasped response both to Kara’s suggestion and the painful pinch with a laugh that comes out far too breathy to not sound at least moderately flirtatious.

    It only encourages Kara. “I missed you,” she blurts, because she gets to just say these things and pretend they don’t knock Lena’s entire world out of orbit.

    “Kara,” Lena warns, closing her eyes against the squeezing sensation in her chest. It’s impossible, Lena thinks. They’ve known each other for days.

    “I was afraid that maybe—” Kara hesitates. “I thought you’d given me a wrong number on purpose.”

    “I’m sorry,” Lena says, and, in a foolish bid for fraternal vulnerability, “I did wonder, why you never called.”

    On the other end of the line, there’s a soft intake of breath. Lena thinks of Kara’s chest rising with it, thinks of her pink lips parting. “Did you think about me?” Kara asks, brutally candid in her eagerness.

    Lena swerves, a little inelegantly. “I was telling you the truth, the other day,” she says. “I really have been swamped at work.”

    “Oh,” Kara says. “Oh, yeah. I can imagine.”

    It’s uncomfortably quiet, for a moment, and Lena grabs at the first thing that comes to mind. “I’m sure you can,” she says, keeping her tone light, friendly. “How is your writing going?”

    It takes her a moment, but then Kara huffs out a harsh, stuttered breath. “Better than yours,” she says, teasing. “What kind of person loops their sevens?”

    Lena’s heart takes a concerning little tumble when the words sink in. “You kept my card?”

    Kara clears her throat a little awkwardly, a rustling sound coming down the line. “I mean, yeah.”

    Lena lapses, stricken, unable to brace herself. “You thought about me,” she skids, half question, half confirmation.

    “Lena.” Kara sighs, imploring. “How could I not?”

    Lena attempts to resist the dangerous affection Kara’s words threaten to trickle into her chest, like a wild beehive dripping golden honey. She flexes her hand, roughly rubbing her thumb over the stinging, now definitely bruised flesh of her thigh. The soreness of the previous weekend’s misunderstanding still lingers like that, no less tender and every bit as real.

    “I think,” she says carefully, reasonably, “that we should cool things down a bit.”

    Kara falls silent. Then says, “We haven’t talked for three months.”

    “Not—” Lena huffs. “Not like that. I mean we should be friends. Don’t you think we could be friends?”

    “We are friends,” Kara insists, and, bluntly, like it’s fucking easy, “I like you a lot.”

    Lena pushes herself up off the couch at that, suddenly in desperate need of a walk. She doesn’t stop until she reaches the bar. “Good,” she affirms, her voice far steadier than she feels. “I like you too.” Thank god they’re not having this conversation in person. Lena’s not sure how well her concealer would have held up against the force of her blush. “That’s a great place to start.”

    Kara sighs out her reply, a soft, almost wistful sound. “You mean it’s a great place to end up at.”

    See? Good. This is going well. Kara is getting it.

    “Yes,” Lena says decisively. Before people get hurt. “I think that would be best. For… everyone involved.”

    Kara rallies. “Friends,” she reiterates.

    Lena nods. “Friends.”

    “We should go to lunch.”

    Lena falters at the conversational whiplash Kara has just delivered. “Lunch?”

    Kara hums. “Friends have lunch, right?”

    Lena reaches for her nearest bottle of Dalmore single malt. “Right.”

    “I know a great place,” Kara says, her enthusiasm quickly gaining momentum. “I’m trying to get Alex to have them cater the wedding. Are you free tomorrow?”

    -

    Lena absolutely isn't free that very following day — obviously — but she doesn’t manage to delay the inevitable for very long; two weeks and three days, to be exact, during which time Kara single-handedly manages to cultivate an objectively impressive text chain.

    I know you lied about that Batman movie, by the way, Kara starts, later that same night. There were three scenes, not one, and you may not have had much dialogue but your outfits were extremely eloquent.

    Lena makes a face. So Kara watched it. I thought that corset was very modest, she types.

    Tell that to tumblr, Kara replies. There are gifsets, Lena.

    Sam dials her Instagram presence way down, apparently satisfied now that Lena and Kara are communicating directly, and Lena is happy to learn that receiving Kara’s pictures straight from the source makes for a much more tolerable experience. For one, Kara hardly ever purposely puts any of her physical assets on display, opting instead for pictures of cute animals found around her neighborhood, and of plants, and trees, and sunrises, and a lot of trashy food.

    She also sends her inquiries like, Do you ever get that dream where you have to return to high school because they found out you missed an assignment in ninth grade?

    Lena leaves most of Kara’s pictures on read — which Kara seems to be fine with — but she always makes sure to answer messages like these promptly, like the good friend she aims to be.

    Could it be that your brain is telling you that you miss teaching?

    Kara shuts that down quickly, telling her I already have an Alex, thank you though, along with a string of emojis Lena doesn’t even attempt to decipher.

    In one particularly puzzling exchange, Kara shares that she’s been unable to stop humming something called ‘Spider Pig’ for four days, and when Lena admits she has no idea what that is, Kara announces their next friendly get-together should be movie night at Lena’s. You do own a television, right?

    Lena ignores Kara’s cheek and wonders what a night like that might look like. What she would wear, where they would sit. She’s had a couple of glasses of red wine with dinner, so she also imagines how she might direct Kara’s attention to the line of freckles along Lena’s cleavage, the pale skin at the hemline of her dress. She can almost feel the warmth of Kara’s palm on the inside of her thigh, the possessiveness of her grip. Lena sends up a quick prayer for forgiveness before picturing, at long last, watching Kara come apart below her mouth; the way those abs might twitch and flex at the deft slide of her tongue, how Kara might arc and buck into the tight seal of her lips until finally, gloriously, perhaps while stammering Lena’s name — letting go, and going over.

    That night is a late one. She’s just blowing off steam, of course, but it’s still a good thing, Lena thinks, absently wiping her fingers on her sheets as she comes down, that they’ve agreed to go somewhere very public for their very friendly lunch.

    -

    Two weeks go by quickly, and for someone with a walk-in closet the size of what she’s been told is the average person’s living room, Lena sure doesn’t seem to have a whole lot of workable options when it comes to what to wear to this particular event.

    It’s lunch. With a friend. Surely Lena Luthor has had lunch with friends before. Why can’t she remember a single one? Is there really no one Lena’s associated with that hasn’t at one point been in her bed?

    It takes Lena two hours and a quick call to her very tired therapist to make it out the door, but somehow, by the time she gets to the sunny street corner where Kara suggested they meet, she’s still twenty minutes early. It’s a sunny Spring day, the trees all in bloom, late-morning sunlight glinting off the traffic that’s died down some in the lull between breakfast and the lunchtime rush. She’s found herself in the rent-controlled part of town, but it’s a pleasant, walkable neighborhood Lena is familiar with because it’s where she takes the ballet class that she had decided to skip today.

    (When asked, last year, Jess had found her five different dance coaches perfectly willing to tutor her privately and then, when Lena had voiced a preference for a group class, two places closer to her home, but Lena had dismissed them all in favor of a small studio run by a retired Moscow National Ballet veteran, a diminutive drill sergeant who conducts her classes in rapid-fire Russian and reminds Lena fondly of the dance teacher she had as a child.)

    Lena doesn’t need to wait on the corner for long: Kara is early, too.

    Lena finds herself admiring the shape of her before her brain fully registers what she’s looking at. Kara is buoyant and broad-shouldered as ever, the dappled sunlight filtered through the trees setting her blonde waves ablaze. She’s a study in magnetism, Lena thinks, the way she repels and attracts; her imposing build and serene self-confidence allow her to stroll down the sidewalk unimpeded, her fellow pedestrians automatically moving out of her way, but her disarming smile diminishes the effect significantly — in the short time Lena is afforded to watch her undetected, Kara is cheerfully greeted by multiple street food vendors before crouching down to cuddle a passerby’s dog, engaging its owner in what appears to be a lively discussion of their pet’s excellent good looks and character.

    When she finally spots Lena, she looks so merry and bright that Lena can hardly accept it's all for her. When she calls out her name in pleased surprise, Lena feels it in at least fifteen different ways at once. And then she stands before her, beautiful and a little out of breath.

    She’s wearing glasses, Lena notes with some confusion — a horn-rimmed pair of simple, practical, and yet somehow absurdly flattering frames.

    And oh, Kara doesn’t look like a friend at all.

    “Wow.” Kara’s wide eyes trip down Lena’s body, then quickly stutter back up to her face. “You look...”

    Decidedly overdressed, Lena notes with some displeasure. Kara is wearing flip-flops and jeans that are frayed at the knees and rolled at the ankles, and a buttonless, robin egg blue blazer that vaguely matches the faded print of the t-shirt underneath. Lena’s white lace top and tailored pantsuit and her nude designer heels suddenly seem frumpy and overly keen in comparison. Damnit, Luthor. This is a friendly get together, not a high-profile publicity event.

    “You too,” Lena says, deftly sidestepping an opportunity for some mortifyingly flattering exchange. Kara flusters a little regardless, fidgeting with the pockets of her blazer, apparently not yet having realized that they’re fake. Lena finds it impossibly charming.

    “So,” Kara starts, a little apprehensively, “How would you feel about going grocery shopping with me?”

    Lena blinks. Kara blinks right back, mute.

    “Aren’t we going out to eat?” Lena asks, a little cautiously.

    “Yeah so, that’s the thing,” Kara says. “I know you don’t like it when other people prepare your food. So I thought maybe we could, you know. If you wanted. Prepare it ourselves.” She sucks in her lips. “At my loft.”

    Lena’s mind is having a hard time keeping up. “Your loft.”

    Kara nods. “I live right over there.” She points at a mid-rise residential building with a brown-red brick facade. “The one with all the…” she waves her fingers, winces. “...pigeon poop.”

    It’s entirely too much for Lena to handle all at once, so, like a good mathematician, Lena resorts to breaking up the problem into smaller, more manageable parts.

    A: Kara Danvers is a culinary menace. B: It's an honest-to-god catastrophe waiting to happen, putting Lena in close private quarters with Kara the way she is suggesting, with the way she’s looking at her now. C: Remembering such an insignificant character quirk and attempting to navigate around it is possibly the kindest thing anyone has ever tried to do for Lena Luthor, virtually friendless pariah that she is.

    (“Have you considered the possibility that you find it easier to make yourself vulnerable physically than you do emotionally?” her therapist had asked when Lena had vocalized her concerns that morning. Lena had tapped her pen against the clipboard in her lap and weighed her options. Perhaps it’s time for her to find a different therapist, if this one still hasn’t gotten the picture.)

    So Lena Luthor walks her nude Jimmy Choos down the produce aisle of Trader Joe’s, picking out lemons, scallions, kale, all while she grills Kara suspiciously. “You do own a blender, right?” she interrogates. “Do you have a strainer? Will we need to get you orzo pasta? Olive oil? Salt?”

    Kara just hums happily, steering their shopping cart with one hand while using the other to dutifully add every single item Lena mentions to the shopping list on her phone.

    -

    Kara’s loft is much more spacious than Lena had assumed, looking at it from the outside. The windows — admittedly slightly speckled with pigeon poop — are floor-to-ceiling, allowing generous amounts of light to fall on the plants that take up every last corner of the space. A counter-height kitchen table, surrounded by eight barstools, and a sprawling brown suede sectional couch make up the centerpieces of the space, clearly indicating Kara is used to entertaining guests. The space is clean, but cluttered, a well-maintained bicycle parked by the entrance door, books and papers and colorful rugs scattered about.

    Kara sets the grocery bags on the table and then moves to take Lena’s jacket, well-mannered as she is. Decidedly less polite is the way her hands tremble against Lena’s skin as it slips off her shoulders, leaving her only in her strapless white lace top. Lena turns just in time to catch Kara staring, cheeks darkening, eyes slowly drifting south.

    Lena glances down at herself. Oh. Yes. Without the jacket, the top really is a little too revealing, isn’t it. Lena hesitates too, wondering for a moment if it would be prudent to ask Kara to put her jacket back on — but then Kara is shrugging out of her own blazer, unveiling the sculpted arms beneath, and when Kara superfluously starts rolling up her already short sleeves, Lena loses her train of thought entirely.

    Lena can tell she’s leaning forward, almost reaching out in an autonomous effort to touch, but then distantly, providentially, she becomes aware of an unfamiliar odor. She pauses, wrinkling her nose. “Is someone cooking?”

    Kara looks at her watch. “Um, right,” she says. “My neighbors. They celebrate some sort of weekly cabbage-related holiday, I think.”

    “Oh,” Lena says.

    Kara hops up on her kitchen table and starts unloading the groceries. “Don’t worry,” she says with determined optimism, “after an hour, you’ll barely even notice it anymore.”

    “No, I mean, it’s Saturday,” Lena clarifies. “Doesn’t that mean you—”

    “...enjoy a day of leisure? Spend time with loved ones — cherished friends?” Kara winks, and Lena has to take a deliberate breath to still the fluttering in her chest. “Not that strict of an observer, remember?” Kara explains, before continuing, her voice low in mock warning, “Don’t ever try to come between Alex and her Shabbat dinners, though.”

    Strange odors and weekly family dinners, Lena thinks. How different their lives are.

    “Do you have neighbors?” Kara asks, as if having read her mind.

    “I… don’t know,” Lena says, frowning slightly. She imagines she does. She’s never really considered them before.

    Kara is looking at her, fingering the corners of the bag of what portends to be Authentic Imported Italian Orzo. “You know I find you a little intimidating,” she admits, off-handed and apropos of nothing, and Lena thinks she could probably spend a lifetime with this woman and still never manage to adjust to her brand of unfiltered honesty.

    “My place smells like other people’s food,” Kara continues in what Lena hopes will turn into an explanation. “I have pigeons roosting on the windowsill. And you—” Kara waves her fingers at Lena, gesturing down her body, her gaze briefly snagging on Lena’s chest again before bouncing away. “You probably attend, like, gallery openings. I bet you own a matching set of towels and books about wine.”

    Lena does indeed do, and own, all of those things. But. “I’d hoped it would be pretty clear to you by now that what I have isn’t who I am as a person.”

    “Not all that you are,” Kara clarifies. “But it isn’t just that. You're like—” Kara looks around her loft, blowing out her cheeks. “Like I bet you’ve actually read Anna Karenina,” she accuses.

    Lena cocks her head. “You haven’t read Anna Karenina?”

    Kara grabs the bag of pistachios, avoiding Lena’s eyes. She tears it open, cracks a shell between her fingers. “No.”

    Huh. “Aren’t you an English teacher?”

    Kara holds up a finger, chewing, looking serious in spite of the fleck of pistachio skin stuck to her plump bottom lip. “English. Teacher.”

    Lena feels her face softening into a smile she’s unable to contain. “Aren’t you an author?” she asks.

    Kara huffs good-naturedly. “Not yet.”

    Lena steps closer to Kara’s dangling legs, traces her fingertips over the divots in the wood. “You know it occurs to me I’ve never even asked you about your book.”

    Kara’s smile is forgiving, her blue eyes devastatingly fond. “You’ve known me for a week, technically speaking.”

    She’s right, Lena thinks as she drops her gaze. So why does it feel like Kara already knows so much about her?

    Kara’s kitchen isn’t much bigger than Lena is used to seeing in the suites she stays in on her business trips. It has a functional oven and a stove, blessedly, but counter space is a bit of an issue, so they commission the table as their workspace, Kara laying out knives and cutting boards, and every vegetable, freshly rinsed. Lena picks up a serrated knife and a tomato, and then she pauses, looking down at herself.

    Kara, too, is looking apprehensively between what remains of Lena’s spotless white outfit and the tomatoes, the kale, the blender by the sink.

    Lena may not have thought this through.

    She slides her eyes up at Kara. “Would it be too optimistic to hope you have an apron?”

    “It would. But.” Kara chews the inside of her cheek. “I do have some clothes that you can borrow.”

    And you know what, fine, whatever, the day’s already weird enough.

    Lena follows Kara into a sectioned off area at the back of her loft, hidden behind a cloth curtain she courteously holds aside for Lena to pass by. Inside, Lena is met with the sight of Kara’s decadently comfortable-looking king size bed, a modest smattering of furniture, and even more plants, vining up an artfully arranged collection of empty wooden picture frames screwed into the wall. Rather than a wardrobe, there’s a fashion rack of clothes on hangers pushed against the curtainless window. Lena begins to understand why most of Kara’s clothes look so fashionably faded.

    Kara picks out a t-shirt and a pair of sweatpants, holding up the latter in critical assessment before handing them over, and then cheerfully leaves Lena to change. And well, would you look at that — if it isn't the last place Lena had expected to find herself in today, stripped down to her underwear in Kara Danvers’ bedroom.

    The sweatpants Kara chose for her are soft and stretchy, and they smell like her, Lena notes, bending down to roll up the ends to keep from tripping on them. The t-shirt she picked out is the Bananarama one, because of course it is.

    Any lingering traces of stumbling awkwardness on Kara’s part seem to disappear along with Lena’s original outfit. Kara appears much more relaxed when Lena resurfaces, moving around her home with handsome, familiar ease. She ties up her hair in a bouncy ponytail, but keeps the glasses on, and before resuming her duties as Lena’s jeopardous sous-chef, she grabs a snack from one of her cupboards and generously offers Lena something puzzlingly called a ‘miniature peanut buttercup’ from a garishly orange bag.

    “No thank you,” Lena tells her, grimacing, and, when Kara proceeds to pour the entire contents of the bag into her mouth, “I don’t understand how you can put that into your body and still look the way you do.”

    Kara’s reply is smooth and chocolate-covered. “Probably the same way you can put all of this in yours,” she says, wagging a bundle of kale and sending water droplets everywhere, “and still taste the way you do.”

    It’s a shot to the heart of Lena’s carefully cool composure. She stares at Kara, scandalized, sure her face is burning. Kara smiles impishly back. “Too soon?” she asks sweetly, looking a little too smug to be convincingly remorseful.

    Lena straightens. She will not be caught squirming. She clears her throat, hoping it will also clear her head. “Maybe just a little,” she huffs.

    They make it through most of the rest of their meal preparation relatively unscathed. And then Lena learns that, just because Kara owns a blender, doesn’t mean she knows how to use one.

    Lena doesn’t realize what’s about to happen until it’s already too late to intervene. “No wait, Kara,” she still tries, “not so—” but the damage is done; the lid has blasted off the jar, and everything — Kara’s counters, Kara’s wall, and a fair portion of Kara herself — is covered in fragrant, vibrant green goop.

    Such is life with Kara Danvers.

    Lena pulls a stray drop from her lashes, but she and Kara’s Bananarama t-shirt appear to have been largely spared. Kara, splattered green and lemon-scented and standing altogether too close, licks out at the corner of her mouth. “Not bad,” she observes calmly. She reaches for Lena and plucks a piece of pistachio out of her dark hair. “Bet you’re happy now that you let me talk you out of that white suit.”

    So lunch is a bust, unfortunately. Kara withdraws to the bathroom for a quick shower and a change of clothes, promising Lena she’ll take her to the Vietnamese food truck just down the block, where Lena will be able to watch her food being prepared. The casual consideration warms Lena all over again.

    “Don’t clean up,” Kara calls out to her from behind her bedroom curtain. “But if you feel like putting on some coffee, I wouldn’t be opposed.”

    Lena wipes down the walls and the counters at least, setting the blender in the sink. She only remembers the coffee when she hears the sound of the shower turning off, quickly grabbing two mugs from the cupboard and placing one under what she assumes to be an espresso maker but what, after Lena turns the crank to start — admittedly an interesting mechanism — bafflingly deposits a gumball into her cup, instead.

    Lena blinks.

    “Kara,” she calls out, warily, “Why do you own a gumball machine?”

    Kara saunters back into the kitchen barefoot, her blonde hair soaked black, dripping icy water onto Lena’s shoulders as she leans in behind her to grin at the contents of Lena’s coffee cup. Lena shivers and tries unsuccessfully to move away, trapped as she is between Kara’s body and the kitchen counter. “Did you take a cold shower?” she asks, disbelief clear in her voice.

    Kara shrugs. “Hot water kind of comes and goes around here,” she explains. “I’m used to it.” Standing as close as she is, Lena can tell her skin is rosy, likely from the freezing water. She’s also vaguely vanilla-scented, and when she reaches past Lena, brushing their arms together, Lena can feel every tiny hair on Kara’s forearm tickling the sensitive skin of Lena’s own.

    Kara turns the gumball machine crank again, and this time a little plastic capsule falls out. Lena huffs out a laugh, says, faintly, inanely, “Lucky you,” trying to swallow down the heart that hammers in her throat when Kara hums into her ear, pleased.

    Kara takes her prize between the fingers of both of her hands, at this point fully enveloping Lena — shamelessly? obliviously? — and twists it open with a soft pop.

    It contains a tiny snow globe on a chain, a miniature cabin encased inside, sparse flecks of white fluttering through the water when Kara’s fingers twitch.

    Oh, come on.

    “The machine was a gift from Alex,” Kara says, her voice almost as delicate as the tiny toy held in her hand. “When I first met her I had just lost… everything that made me happy. But she pointed out how the gumball machine at the mall always made me smile.” She shakes the little globe again, on purpose this time, and Lena watches as the snowflakes whirl through the water. She can hear warm amusement in Kara’s voice when she continues, “She bought me my own when she moved out, and it’s been with me ever since.”

    There’s a beat, where neither one of them moves, Lena riding out the urge to lean back into Kara’s warm and solid body, to turn around and put her arms around her shoulders and hang on tight. And then Kara says, simple and straightforward and breaking effortlessly through every barricade Lena has so carefully erected around her heart, “When I find something that brings me joy, I make an effort to keep it around.”

    Lena’s heart is now thudding so loud she can hear it, the whooshing in her ears strangely reminiscent of the tsunami sirens along the Pacific coastline that L-Corp’s funding had reinstated. Move out of the water, she thinks. Seek higher ground. Instead, she turns to face Kara, slowly, goosebumps rising as she moves in the loose cradle of Kara’s arms. Kara doesn’t move away, keeping her body close to Lena’s own, her posture open and inviting. For a moment they just look at each other, Kara’s eyes dancing between Lena’s own.

    “I’ve never met anyone like you before,” Kara says quietly. “Sometimes I’d talk to Sam or Alex about you just to make sure I didn’t make you up.”

    It leaves Lena breathless, her heart cracking open like the plastic capsule at the pressure of Kara’s fingers, now resting gently at Lena’s hips. Her jaw drops, lips parting before she’s even sure what she’s about to say.

    It definitely isn’t “Good morning loser, which fake Facebook cooking hack are we debunking today?”

    Alex is standing in the doorway, leather-clad, a keychain in one hand, motorcycle helmet in the other and a look of slowly dawning horror on her face.

    “Alex!” Kara greets her sister cheerfully, not moving an inch. “I didn’t know you were stopping by.”

    Alex blinks. Lena is tragically aware of what they must look like, pressed together at the counter as closely as they are. “You left your phone at our house last night,” Alex says faintly. She fishmouths for a moment, then finally manages a feeble, “What are you doing.”

    “We’re eating out,” Kara announces, still cheerily standing her ground. “We just decided.”

    Lena recoils in shock, pressing her hands to Kara’s forearms in an effort to put some space between them. “No, we’re not.”

    Kara returns her focus to Lena at that, immediately taking her hands off Lena’s hips and looking deeply disappointed. “We’re not?”

    Lena’s heart lurches. Being the cause of Kara’s disappointment is nearly unbearable — but how could Lena believe she’d ever be capable of being anything else? If there’s one thing that’s certain in this world, it’s that Lena Luthor will always, always, ultimately disappoint. “It’s not— like that,” she promises Alex. “We’re having lunch. As friends.”

    “And showering,” Alex observes dryly with a look at Kara’s hair, then raising an eyebrow down Lena’s body. “And wearing each others’ clothes. As friends.”

    “Lena,” Kara says, gently. She takes off her glasses, sets them down on the counter. “Don’t you think that maybe we—”

    “No,” Lena says, now nudging Kara’s body more forcefully out of her way, “no. This isn’t how this day was supposed to go at all.” She manages to duck past Kara, putting seven feet and the kitchen table between them. The water droplets on the lemon rinds glitter in the noontime sun, serenely refracting light.

    Kara and Alex are both looking at her, eerily similar expressions on their faces. “Oh,” Alex says. “I should go.”

    “No, you should stay,” Lena asserts. “I’ll go.” She makes a beeline for her purse, briefly debates the merits of a detour to Kara’s bedroom to grab her clothes, ultimately deciding it will take too long and also look preposterous.

    As if she isn’t embarrassed enough already, wearing rolled up sweatpants and Jimmy Choos.

    “This was fun,” she announces cheerfully, like a deranged person, fingers already fumbling for her phone to call her driver before she remembers she’s her own chauffeur, these days. “Maybe refrain from trying to cater the wedding, though.” She attempts a smile, but something in Kara’s expression tells her she doesn’t quite manage.

    Kara sends her off with a befuddled little goodbye.

    As the door clicks shut behind her, she hears Alex demand, “What just happened?”

    Kara sounds a little forlorn. “I don’t know.”





    Chapter End Notes

    Just a few more to go! Reader, you're doing so well! (You should totally congratulate yourself by hitting that kudos button!)

     

     

    (a few drawings I did of Jess, Sam and Lena in their scene in Lena’s office)

    When Friends Come To Call

    Chapter Notes

    You’ve seen those notes at the beginning of particularly sexy chapters saying ‘absolutely nothing interesting happens in this one’, right? Yeah, so in this case, it’s 100% true.

    Lena doesn’t plan to flee the city.

    Controversy erupts around L-Corp’s acquisition of Metropolis Medical Tech, and while Lena could have sent an associate or chosen to handle the crisis from the relative safety and comfort of her office, she decides to fly out personally. It’s in L-Corp’s interest, she rationalizes. Establishing a little bit of goodwill and all that. It’s not as if she’s going out of her way to avoid Kara — Lena even makes sure, like the good friend she still believes she can be, to let Kara know right away that she will be very busy for a while, and that perhaps it’s for the best if they postpone their next get-together indefinitely, you know, just until things settle down.

    Kara even agrees with her, understanding as she is.

    Their text chain stutters to an awkward halt after that final, curt exchange. Lena no longer wakes up to new portraits of freshly unfurled monstera leaves or updates on the pigeon eggs that are surely about to hatch. No mystifying non-sequiturs await her when she returns to her suite exhausted, face-planting into her bed at the end of every day.

    Lena is frustrated with herself, knowing she’s the culprit of their dwindling affiliation but unable to think of a way to talk to Kara without feeling like she’d just be confusing matters more. Maybe it was too soon, she thinks, maybe they’re too raw, the nerve of their attraction still exposed. Lena hates it, this constant tightness in her chest. Why should it be so uncomfortable, so hard, when she and Sam had segued effortlessly into friendship almost a decade ago?

    She returns to National City on Tuesday night, late in the evening, and finds her best friend loitering in her lobby. Sam is wearing jeans and a soft sweater and a contrite but hopeful smile on her face. “I’d like to state for the record that I could have come to gloat,” she says, “but I’m here to apologize, instead.” She raises the bottle of Viñedo Chadwick she’s carrying and says, “Will you accept a drink from a very remorseful friend?”

    Sam lies down on her sofa as Lena walks the wine into her kitchen, kicking off her heels and shrugging out of her blazer before finding two Bordeaux glasses and filling one a little too close to the rim. She hands the other one to Sam and sits down by her head, waiting as Sam wriggles over and puts her head into her lap. Sam looks up at her, soft. “I’ve been a shithead, haven’t I,” she says.

    “Yes.” Lena brushes a lock of hair from her best friend’s forehead and admits, “But there’s been a lot of that going around.”

    Sam bites her lip. “Will you tell me? All I know is that two of my friends are sulking, and Kara keeps insisting everything is fine but her eyes twitch every time I mention your name, and Alex refuses to talk to me about it anymore because she seems to think it’s somehow all my fault.”

    Lena studies the movement of the wine swirling in her glass. “We aren’t sulking,” she says. “We’re trying our best to get along. And,” she chides, glaring at Sam’s skeptical expression, “you can’t honestly believe you’re innocent in this. You’re the one who practically threw me into her bed that week in Colorado.”
