    “Uh, I distinctly remember you telling me you climbed into it all by yourself, that first day,” Sam says. “And I don’t understand how you go from that to this—” she wiggles her fingers, “perplexing awkwardness.”

    It’s quiet for a moment. National City’s warehouse district glitters white and yellow across the moonlit bay. Kara’s building is back there somewhere — Kara probably typing away at her laptop, blissfully undisturbed in the odorless interlude between two Cabbage Saturdays.

    “We’re not lovers,” Lena insists. “I’m trying to be her friend.”

    Sam looks at her from Lena’s lap, eyes dark and knowing. “You may want to tell that to your face. I haven’t seen you look this starry-eyed since we first got word that Spheer had successfully developed artificial antibodies.” She waits, tapping a finger against her glass. “I know something went down the weekend before you left for Metropolis. Tell me.”

    Lena tugs at her own ponytail until the pins dislodge and massages her hair down with one hand, sighing with relief as it tumbles loose over her shoulders. “We met for lunch,” she offers. “Things got— messy.”

    Sam raises her glass over her head to tink it against Lena’s own. “That I am willing to believe.”

    Lena laughs humorlessly. “We didn’t have sex,” she says.

    One of Sam’s eyebrows twitches up at her. “You didn’t?”

    Not this time. “See,” Lena hedges, still on the fence about owning up to everything, “this right here is exactly why they tell you not to sleep with your coworkers.”

    Sam gives her a significant look.

    “Or with your friends,” Lena amends.

    Sam’s eyebrows shoot up and knit together, Lena’s best friend and coworker now thoroughly amused.

    Lena groans, rubbing her temple, beginning to feel the strain of jetlag and nine days sequestered in a conference room with a board of directors that had insisted on treating a friendly merger like a hostile takeover. “Alright fine,” she says, “This is why I shouldn’t have slept with the sister of the fiancée of the last friend I have left in what I’m sure must look like some sort of desperate bid to secure a place in your family.”

    Well. That’s— hmm.

    Lena frowns and subtly sniffs at her wine, almost wondering whether her best friend could have slipped her something stronger. Lena almost wishes she had a neighbor with a love for fragrant food, just so she might have something to distract Sam from the things she’s just disclosed.

    Sam’s brain seems to snag on Lena's steamier admission, her eyes and her smile widening triumphantly at the revelation that Lena did sleep with Kara — but then the rest of Lena’s message seems to sink in and Sam sobers instantly, circling gentle fingers around Lena’s wrist. “Hey. You are my family,” she says. “Nothing will ever change that.”

    Lena’s mouth twitches in derision, a bitter pebble in her throat. “You say that now.”

    “Wait,” Sam says. “Are you for real?” She sits up, careful not to jostle Lena’s wine, then sets both of their glasses down on the coffee table before turning her whole body and every single one of its outrageously gangly limbs toward her.

    “It's okay, Sam,” Lena tells her, aching and a little hoarse, trying not to feel the weight of her world pressing down on her. “You’re getting married. And I’m thrilled for you. But I’m also scared—” she’s interrupted by an abrupt closure of her throat, the smooth pebble lodged there growing large and painful. The last word comes out a mangled, embarrassing sound, and Lena slams a hand over her mouth in an effort to suppress it. She stares at Sam, scandalized and bewildered and attempting desperately to prevent the sudden tears in her eyes from spilling over.

    Sam lays her hands on Lena’s cheeks, then starts stroking her hair, uncomprehending. “What, Lena?” she asks, worried. “What is it?”

    Lena squeaks out — because that’s apparently a thing she now does — “I’m losing you.”

    Within a second, Sam’s eyes turn to jet. “I’ve told you this before and I will tell you again right now,” she says, her voice now low and almost dangerous. “I am not going anywhere.”

    Lena Luthor can count on one hand the times she has truly been afraid — and she’s lived through kidnappings, assassination attempts, and her mother’s annual Easter brunch. The truth is that she’s never particularly feared meeting her own end; has been anticipating it, in fact, ever since that summer Rhea had ripped out her heart along with some of her stomach lining. But once upon a time, in the not-too-distant past, the person that matters most to her in the world — who until recently had considered Lena to be the most important adult in hers — was being told by a soft-spoken, kind-faced specialist that, without medical intervention, she might have months to live. Lena had held her best friend’s hand as they listened to him patiently discuss her prognosis and treatment goals — and for what had seemed like the first time in her life, Lena had felt a feral, suffocating dread.

    Tonight the woman in question sits in front of her, a couple of years along the line, happy, healthy, miraculously recovered, and Lena is losing her after all.

    “You don’t understand,” Lena croaks, attempting to pull her French cuffs down over her hands, feeling juvenile and deeply unattractive. “You’re about to have a wife. A real family. What would you need to keep me around for?”

    Sam stills, then gasps, tears springing to her own eyes. “Oh,” she soothes, “Oh, kiddo.” She’s gripping Lena’s hair now, in what Lena is sure must be the world’s most unflattering pair of pigtails. “Is that what you think love is?” she asks, earnestly, but also a little like she believes Lena is an idiot.

    When Lena fails to answer, not understanding what her best friend is asking, Sam tugs her into an uncomfortably tight hug, one of her knobby knees digging hard into Lena’s thigh. She sighs, “Sometimes I forget you never had family, before.”

    Lena protests, mouthing into Sam’s sweater, “It’s not as if you were so much better off.”

    “Listen,” Sam says, pulling away to look Lena in the eye. “I will always—” she squeezes Lena’s shoulders, then shakes her. “Always. Make room for you.” She ducks her head when Lena tries to look away, stubbornly maintaining eye contact. “Do you hear me?” she pushes, loosening her grip only when Lena nods obediently.

    Lena sniffles, surreptitiously wiping her eyes on her already lipstick-stained cuff. “Has anyone ever told you you’re a bully?” she asks.

    Sam takes Lena’s hand in her own, bending and stretching her fingers one by one. “You’re my family whatever you decide to do,” she promises, “but I still think you should try to work things out with Kara. There’s something there, I think you know it, too, and Lena, I understand how hard it is for you to believe this, but you deserve good things.”

    Lena manages to suppress a fresh surge of feeling, swallowing hard and waving the hand Sam isn’t currently holding at the room — indicating the designer sofa Sam’s thoughtlessly put her loafers onto, the original art on her walls, her magnificent ocean view. “I have good things.”

    Sam rolls her eyes at her. “Good people.”

    Lena can feel her smile wrinkling her nose. “Apparently,” she says, “I have you.”

    “You know what,” Sam says, smacking Lena’s knee, “you’re right. You don’t need sex or romance. You need therapy.”

    Lena takes a deep breath, relenting. “I know.”

    “You still need to talk to her.” Sam’s voice is stern. “She’s sad and confused. If you’re really her friend, you will do something about it.”

    Lena reaches over to the coffee table to retrieve both of their glasses, but Sam waves hers off. Lena settles in next to her, her own wine glass in hand, and leans her head on her best friend’s shoulder. “I know.”

    “Consider it a personal favor to me,” Sam presses. “I’m going to be stressed out enough about the wedding. I don’t want to have to worry about your drama on top of everything else. My mother is coming, did I tell you that?”

    Lena gasps. “Patricia?”

    Sam grimaces. “Patricia. She’s already sent me a picture of the dress she’s wearing.”

    Lena sips her wine. “Is it white?”

    Sam snorts. “No. A classy burgundy red.”

    “That’s my color.” Lena frowns, a little petulantly.

    “Wear it, I don’t care.” Sam’s shrug lightly jostles Lena’s head. “I’m sure you’ll outshine us all.”

    Lena rubs a thoughtful thumb down the stem of her glass. “Does that mean you’re not putting me in a bridesmaids’ dress, then?”

    Sam looks down at her, raising her brows. “Would you even let me?”

    Lena finishes her wine in one last, decisive gulp. “I’m not sure what to tell her,” she confesses quietly. “Luthors don’t apologize. They double down.”

    Sam’s grin is wide and groundlessly confident. “I’ll help.”

    Lena laughs at her. “Great,” she says. “The blind will lead the blind.”

    -

    Lena Luthor may not know how to apologize, but she’s had some practice making reparations. She texts Kara, two days after she speaks with Sam, Are you free for lunch next Wednesday? There’s a place near my office that serves these mini spring rolls I think you’d really love, and they require far less cleanup than kale pesto apparently does.

    When the incoming call comes through this time, Lena’s screen lighting up like a beacon inside the fading twilight in her bedroom as she’s shrugging out of her dress, Lena hardly even flinches — simply leaves the garment pooled on the floor of her dressing room, and picks up the phone.

    “So the strangest thing happened yesterday,” Kara greets her, voice pitched high with exaggerated wonder. “Plumbers came by to repair our building’s water heater. No more random shut-offs.”

    Lena is pleased, but not surprised. “Really.” She sits down on her bed, absently tracing her thumb along one of the clips securing her garter belt to her stockings.

    “You made a call, didn’t you?” Kara asks. “You didn’t have to do that.”

    “Yeah, I did.” Lena fiddles with the clasp as she considers confessing she’d done a little more than that; how she had contacted the landlord personally and insisted on being present for an immediate inspection, horrified at the heating system’s poor maintenance work; how she hadn’t even bothered to wait for the landlord to get his crew on it, simply sending in her own team of engineers to make sure the system received a full overhaul overnight. “That’s what friends are for,” she tells Kara instead.

    She’d still sicced her lawyers on him, though.

    “Friends,” Kara repeats, gently. And after a moment, just as gentle, “Is that really all you think we can be?”

    Lena takes a fortifying breath, closing her eyes and imagining she can taste the salt on the evening breeze coming in through the sliding doors to her balcony. Thinks of needing, and failing, of losing and leaving. Thinks of Lex, and Andrea, and the nebulous memory of a loving mother taken from her before she'd learned the word for grief. Thinks of everything Kara’s already come to mean to her, and all the ways in which Lena is liable to screw it up.

    “It’s what I’m able to offer you,” she answers eventually, decisively, pressing the garter clip into her skin. And then, small, weak, useless lesbian that she is, she amends, “... at least at this point in time.”

    “Okay.” Kara’s voice is still kind, but a little steadier, as if she’s resolved something for herself.

    Lena closes her eyes and tries to keep her tone light, casual, not like she’s tearing at the messy stitches that hold her heart together. “Relationships aren’t exactly my forte,” she says. “Friendship is already a fairly significant ask.”

    “I think it’s going to be a bit of a learning curve for me, as well,” Kara says with just a flicker of mirth in her voice. “I’ve ever had a friend like you before.”

    Lena bites back a smile. “Repairing the water heater was a matter of public safety, Kara,” she gently rebukes. “Someone could have gotten hurt.”

    Kara hums. “And the sex?” she asks, amusement now fully saturating her words. “Was that a safety issue, as well?”

    The way the allusion alone is enough to set Lena’s heart racing has her seriously wondering what her physician would have to say about it. “It may very well have been,” she says, making a mental note to talk to Jess about scheduling her next checkup. “You did save me from freezing to death out there.”

    On the other end of the line, Kara chuckles; a low, familiar sound. “But,” Lena proceeds, bravely pushing past the effect it still has on her, “much like I hope the heater repairs will be — a one-time type of thing.”

    There’s a beat, long enough for Lena to become aware of the sound of a pair of seagulls gliding by outside her window, their wings reflecting the bright light of her living room. Then Kara says warmly, almost fondly, “I don’t regret what we did.”

    Lena nods to herself, one errant thread tugging loose inside her chest. She breathes cautiously into the space it frees up. “Neither do I.”

    “Good.” Kara sighs deeply, as if waking from a refreshing nap. “So. No matter what it was, sending those plumbers, a friendly gesture or whatever—” (atonement, Lena thinks, but doesn’t say), “I’m grateful. Showers are a huge part of my writing process.”

    Lena lets out a mocking Hah, grateful for the subject change. “No they’re not.”

    “I mean it,” Kara insists. “If I get stuck somewhere, I take a shower about it, and ninety nine percent of the time the issue is fixed before I even have time to get completely lathered up.”

    “Well,” Lena rasps, definitely not picturing the image Kara’s just painted of herself in resplendent 8K, “In that case, I will be looking for my acknowledgement when your novel finally comes out.” She pauses, wondering if she’s earned the right yet to tease. Then she remembers Kara, forward and flirtatious in her loft the last time she’d seen her, and avenges, “If your novel ever comes out. You still haven’t told me a single thing about it, and I’m beginning to think you’re slacking off.”

    “Maybe that’s for good reason,” Kara says, not the least bit fazed. “What if you decide to run off with my material the same way you ran off with my favorite shirt?”

    Lena grimaces at the reminder of her graceless exit. “Considering I technically traded you for the suit,” she argues, “I say you got a bargain.”

    “You can’t put a price on comfort,” Kara pouts. “Or on eighties vintage tees.”

    Lena sighs a little regretfully, splaying her hand over her duvet until her fingertips touch the waistband of Kara’s sweats, currently tucked under her pillow where Lena had left them before her shower that morning. “Fine,” she says. “We can make the trade next Saturday, when you meet me for lunch. And then you’re going to tell me absolutely everything there is to know about this novel of yours.”

    Kara laughs. “It’s a deal.”

    -

    Minutes before her alarm goes off the next morning, purple and peach streaking the 5am sky, Lena's private phone alerts her to a new text message from Kara. It’s a picture of a shabby nest, rather more of a random collection of twigs in her window, now home to a pair of plump infant birds. They look like wet cement, dark gray and naked save for a sparse smattering of bright yellow lint. Their beaks remind Lena acutely of the Bad Dragon™ novelty dildo one of Andrea’s more flamboyant friends had presented to her on her birthday.

    AREN’T THEY ADORABLE? Kara demands.

    They are, objectively, the ugliest things Lena has ever seen in her entire life.

    She doesn’t hesitate to tell Kara exactly that.

    -

    Lena Luthor has made some questionable decisions throughout her relatively brief time on this earth. There’s the horseback riding accident she’d orchestrated when she was ten, breaking her arm in an effort to get out of spending the summer away at camp, wanting to spend it with Lex, home from university, instead. Lillian had shipped her off anyway, forcing her to spend four hellish, humid weeks painting one clumsy mug after another while everyone else was off having an adventure. There’s the prominent tattoo on her lower back she’d acquired on a sleepless whim just after Lionel had died, when she’d unearthed his letters to her birthmother — a clunky, halfhearted Celtic design she certainly wouldn’t have opted for today. Hell, she’s often wondered whether choosing to take over the family company after Lex’s arrest and attempting to steer it toward a brighter future, rather than just taking her cut and running for the hills, might not in fact be dysfunctional behavior indicative of some unresolved childhood trauma.

    And still, out of the many mistakes Lena Luthor has made, choosing to befriend Kara Danvers might just be the most ruinous one to date.

    It’s to Kara’s credit that she takes Lena’s petition for friendship in earnest, following her instructions almost to a fault. When they meet, Kara’s hugs are as warm as ever, but they no longer linger a moment too long. When she invariably offers Lena a bite of her food — whether it’s the spring rolls Lena had recommended, or the fish tacos Kara had been raving about for weeks — she doesn’t try to do something ridiculous like feeding it to Lena from her fork or from her fingers, simply cutting or tearing off a bite-sized piece for Lena to take.

    Kara’s conduct is as wholesome as the button-down shirts and chinos she seems to favor, as comfortable as her flat oxford shoes — and, Lena realizes, not noticeably different from the easy affection she had become accustomed to in Colorado, in the leadup to their brief affair. The only difference is that Kara now keeps her glasses on and her overtures chaste — save for a few slip-ups so subtle Lena can easily convince herself she’s imagining things — and she seems to have settled into a casual, undemanding kind of amicability Lena is finding it increasingly difficult to match.

    When it’s Lena’s turn to decide where they’ll see each other next, they sit down in swanky establishments near Lena’s office, popular with businessmen trying to impress potential investors; all starched white linen and crystal chandeliers. When Kara gets to choose where they go, she brings Lena to her favorite dog park for breakfast, making faces at the fresh-pressed vegetable smoothies Lena brings them; or leads her, with barely contained excitement, to a Korean street food vendor in a park near the financial district for a mid-summer, late-afternoon treat, sitting them down on a shaded bench where Kara watches the ducks while Lena watches ice cream slowly dripping down Kara’s fingers.

    Whatever they do, wherever they meet, two things remain constant: there is always food involved (at Kara’s insistence) and it’s somewhere public (by Lena’s own unspoken design). They talk about things that never seem to matter much in the moment, the boring details of their everyday — a product failure, a press release, the sweet nostalgia of an abandoned pigeon nest — and then, weeks later, Kara will recall some careless, throw-away comment Lena had made and since forgotten about, and Lena will feel entirely upended.

    “It’s no wonder your mother is so frustrated with you all the time,” Kara will say for instance, casually devastating, her mouth half-full, honey ginger glaze smeared red and sticky along her bottom lip, “all of your best qualities are the exact opposite of what she wants you to be,” and Lena will hide behind her sunglasses, citing photosensitivity — not a total lie — and pretend she isn’t thinking about pulling Kara in by her collar and licking the sweetness straight from her mouth.

    The easier Kara makes it look, smoothly moving past their romantic chemistry, the harder it seems for Lena to ignore. Lena is the one who finds herself fumbling along the fringes of their friendship, who needs to remind herself repeatedly — in a stern inner voice eerily similar to her mother’s — to sit up straight and stop fidgeting with her food, to stop staring, for heaven’s sake, you’re being impolite. It’s hopeless. Kara is a spray of golden ochre on a grayscale canvas, the way she stands out from the board members and bland businessmen Lena is usually made to associate with — never shy about showing her appreciation for her food or her genuine delight at seeing Lena again. Where Kara shows up hungry whenever they meet in a very real, very literal way — the woman should weigh at least twice as much as she does, with the amount of food she can put away in a single sitting — it’s Lena who is famished, never getting quite enough of what she’s starving for.

    After everything that Lena Luthor has come to learn about herself — realizing she’s a lesbian, discovering tequila brings out a latent proclivity towards stripping in public places, being informed she’s actually her thought-to-be-adoptive father’s biological bastard child — by a mile, finding out that she can’t be trusted to be left alone in a room with a kind-faced, beefy blonde has to be the most perplexing.

    -

    Time crawls past an endless summer, claws its way through fall, and then it’s November; an early autumn evening, cool and dry following an afternoon of intermittent rain. Lena is in front of her dressing room mirror, popping her lips and critically appraising their freshly painted Louboutin Velvet Matte tint as she answers her best friend’s call on speakerphone. “I don’t have time to talk, Sam,” she says. “I’m about to head out.”

    “Ooh, that’s right,” Sam sing-songs. “The National City Symphony Gala is tonight.” Unfortunately. “You must be so excited.”

    Lena huffs, lowering her dramatically kohl-lined lashes. To say she’s dreading the evening would be an understatement. She’s never been fond of these events, but they’ve been near unbearable since she’s been expected to attend them without her formerly only mildly megalomaniacal brother and the life raft of his snarky running commentary. Tonight Lena will be alone, adrift in a sea of bedazzled opportunists and self-loving men gathered like a shiver of sharks ready to frenzy at the first whiff of blood on the water — and she’ll only have her mother left to stand guard: Lillian Luthor, a stoic sentry, untouched upon her perch, silently overseeing it all.

    Lena makes a last minute detour to her bathroom, grabbing a pair of tampons in case her period chooses this evening to show up. “I’m still mad you bailed on me tonight,” she calls out to Sam.

    Sam hums, as if in complete agreement. “Imagine how mad I’d be if you were to bail on my bachelor party.”

    “Stop.” Lena doesn’t have time for more of Sam’s badgering tonight. She has urgent matters to attend to: most importantly, whether the dress she’s wearing is extravagant-yet-unpretentious enough to hold up to her mother’s sharp-tongued scrutiny. “I never said I wasn’t going,” she tells Sam, shoving one foot into an elegant stiletto heel. “And you promised me your absence tonight wasn’t an effort to extort me into accepting your invitation.”

    “I told you,” Sam says. “I have things to do. Future-wifely duties to fulfill. Plotting a wedding is a lot of work.”

    “Plotting, are you?” Lena remarks, balancing her weight against the vanity while she slips on her other heel. “Taking your usual Machiavellian approach, I see.”

    “It's why you hired me,” Sam smoothly reminds her. “Speaking of which, you and Kara are on good terms now, right?”

    Lena pauses at the unexpected question. “We are,” she recovers. “We’re— friendly.”

    “Friendly.” Lena can hear her best friend’s smile, a tiny wet snap as her lips stretch and part. “So you’re not avoiding each other anymore?”

    “Absolutely not,” Lena says, sounding a little indignant. “We have lunch.” And breakfast. Brunch, sometimes. An early dinner, two weeks back, when Kara had discovered it was Lena’s birthday and security had called Jess to inform her a woman carrying a brown Big Belly Burger bag — the contents of which had been searched thoroughly and deemed harmless, though greasy — was refusing to leave the lobby until she’d spoken to Lena Luthor personally.

    Lena still feels warmth blossom in her chest at the memory of the plain cupcake Kara had pulled out of her jacket pocket, sticking a small, solitary candle into the top. Lena had barely managed to stop her from lighting it, remembering her office’s fire safety measures just in time.

    “So your reluctance to give me an R.s.v.p. on the bachelor party invite,” Sam says, “That doesn’t have anything to do with the fact that Kara will very likely be there too?”

    Lena bites the inside of her cheek, turning sideways to inspect the way her dress gathers at the small of her back. “Of course not. Listen,” she urges, “you’ll be on standby later tonight, right? I’ll need you to stop me from doing something drastic when Lillian introduces me as her derelict daughter again, or tells me I’m wearing an ‘interesting’ dress.”

    “I’m sure you look spectacular,” Sam reassures her. “Did you go with one of the low V necklines I suggested?”

    Lena thumbs carefully down the duchesse lapels, giving herself a final onceover in her dressing room mirror. “The black tuxedo dress,” she confirms, “with the lace sleeves and back.”

    “And the thigh slit.” Sam whistles. “Hair?”

    Lena touches it to her neatly twisted chignon. “Up and out of the way.”

    “Excellent,” Sam croons. “She’ll be rendered speechless.”

    “Maybe.” Lena smiles, a little wryly. “I’m wearing all twelve of my diamond stud earrings tonight. If I play my cards right, Mother might have a stroke before the auction even starts.”

    “And if not, she’ll have one soon after.” Sam laughs, infectious enough to make Lena grin. She really will miss having Sam there. No one else attending the gala tonight — Lillian least of all — is aware how much of L-Corp’s money, traditionally set aside for the Luthor family’s personal expenses, has already been allocated to the starting bid of this year’s assumed-to-be-less-popular items, ensuring all of Lena’s favorite charities will receive a sizable donation this year. Watching Lillian stew in silent outrage would have been far more fun — and perhaps marginally less life-threatening — with her best friend at her side.

    Lena is in her private elevator and on her way to the basement parking garage before Sam finally stops marveling at the thought of Lillian’s imminent demise and seems to remember herself. “Just to confirm, though,” she reiterates, “You’re okay with me inviting both you and Kara to the party?”

    Lena takes a deep, patient breath. The air is thick with humidity, even this far underground, even at this time of night. “Yes, Sam,” she repeats. “We’re adults, in case you hadn’t noticed.”

    “See I know that you think you are,” Sam says gently and, inexplicably, in somewhat of a rush, “and that’s why you’re always pretending to be far too busy to have fun—” (Lena huffs at this) “— but I’m worried Kara may take it personally if you run again.”

    The elevator dings, signaling Lena’s stop.

    “Fine,” she says, stepping out into the garage, ready to hang up. “I’ll come to your stupid bachelor party. Tell Jess to clear my schedule that evening.” She pauses for a moment, considering. “Maybe the morning after, as well.”

    Lena’s phone goes dark, the connection severed. Lena looks at it, confused.

    Then Sam’s voice echoes out, “Consider it done, boss.”

    Lena blinks up. In the place where her Lincoln town car should be parked — black, tasteful, subdued — is a hot pink Hummer limousine the exact opposite of every single one of those things. Frank, her driver, already has his hand on the door, as if this is a sort of thing that he deals with every day. “Ms. Luthor,” he says calmly. He’s wearing a tiny pink party hat.

    Standing next to him, in full makeup and dressed in a black sequined cocktail dress, is Sam, hastily tucking her phone into her bra while glinting her teeth at Lena. She looks frightfully excited.

    Lena stares at her blankly. “What’s happening?”

    Sam pouts, looking a little disappointed in her best friend’s deductive skills. “What does it look like?” she asks. “We’re abducting you.”

     

     

     

    Chapter End Notes

    Imagine getting that sudden, indefinable sense of doom and knowing beyond a shadow of a doubt that it’s Samantha Arias, plotting to derail every single one of your plans.

    Somebody Waits for You (Kiss Her Once for Me)

    Chapter Notes

    Oop, chapter count's up again. I promise I don't know how this keeps happening. I'm usually a much better planner than this.

    So it turns out her mother was right all along: Lena is an embarrassment to the Luthor name.

    Years of experience in being kidnapped or targeted should have honed her reflexes to the point of a sharp-tipped dagger, but in reality they’re more like the blunt button at the end of her fencing foil. Stunned into pliancy and caught completely off her guard, it takes Sam approximately three seconds and little more than a gentle nudge to maneuver Lena out of her comfort zone and into the back of the waiting Hummer.

    It’s like being invited inside Barbie’s Magical Mansion. The gaudy interior reminds Lena of mandated playdates with her elementary school classmates, precisely as pink and sparkly, and — once the door slams shut — exactly as claustrophobic. A chorus of friendly excitement erupts when she slides into the heart-shaped pleather seats, Sam squeezing in behind her. Lena is shuffled toward the loveseat in the back, where she’s faced with an impressively well-stocked built-in minibar and — of course — her good friend Kara Danvers.

    And it’s fine, right? This is fine. It doesn’t even matter that Kara is looking particularly handsome in a starched white button down and vintage suspenders, blonde hair slicked back in a bun and those ridiculously attractive glasses framing her soft features just so. In fact, Lena hardly even notices — she's been microdosing Kara’s good looks for the better part of a year now, after all, inoculating herself against the effect they once had on her. Lena has grown as a person and Kara is her friend.

    So Lena is fine, and it’s friendly, the way Kara lights up like sunshine herself at her arrival and reaches for her immediately, moving in for what Lena assumes is going to be one of their perfectly cordial, carefully rationed hugs. Kara stops herself at the last minute, though, her eyes widening at the plunging neckline of Lena’s dress. She coughs and reddens. “You look,” she wheezes faintly, her extended arm landing on the armrest behind Lena’s shoulder, fingers twitching, “uh—”

    “Very fancy.” It’s Jess, the unrepentant apostate, lounging in the seat across from them, spinning a glass of sparkling wine between her fingers. She’s zippered into a stunning deep-blue flapper dress, her hair twisted around a rhinestone-studded headband.

    Lena shoots her assistant a look of deep disapproval, wondering whether plotting against your boss with your other boss can be considered corporate conspiracy. “I assume you had a hand in all of this?” she demands.

    Sam hums her mild dissent, sparkling tip to toe under the dizzying strobe lights. “A couple of fingers, at most,” she says.

    “Please,” Jess tells her, “I planned this entire event. We'd be on a private plane to Vegas right now if I’d let you call the shots.”

    Lena keeps her posture ramrod straight. Kara’s hand lingers like a flat iron at her back, close enough to scorch through the delicate lace of her dress. “You’re my assistant,” she points out. “A little bit of a heads up would have been nice.”

    Alex snorts at her from the other side of the minibar. “And give you the opportunity to schedule another convenient business trip?” Turning to Jess she needles, “Is she in charge of a tech company, or a military superpower? How difficult can it be to pencil us in for dinner every once in a while?”

    Lena blinks at her soon-to-be sister-in-law. “Assuming this is, in fact, your bachelor party,” she presses Sam, “Why on earth did you bring your fiancée?”

    “Why wouldn’t I?” Sam asks. “All of my best friends are here.”

    The movement of the car jostles them all slightly as it pulls out of the parking garage, tossing Lena back into her seat and leaving her pressed firmly into Kara’s unresolved one-armed hug and with no way to subtly extricate herself again. “You have my deepest sympathy,” she huffs, folding her arms in front of her chest before grumbling out one final protest to her villainous best friend. “You know my mother isn’t going to be happy when she realizes I’ve skipped out on another public appearance.”

    “Honey.” Sam leans in close, her eyes gleaming violet under the pink neon lights. “I think it’s time you realize Lillian Luthor isn’t going to be happy no matter what you do.”

    Before Lena has time to fully digest that remark, someone presses a glass into her hand. It’s Alex. “Drink up, Luthor,” she says. “You look like you need it.”

    Lena looks down at the drink she’s been served. It looks good. Smells pretty amazing, too; citrusy and crisp. But the weight of it is off. She frowns, tapping the glass with a fingernail. “Is this plastic?” she squints.

    “It is,” Alex says, passing a drink to Kara as well.

    Kara's thousand-watt grin is far too close and entirely too tender. Lena feels her heart break into a sweat at the sight of it. “Have you ever even seen a disposable cup before?” Kara asks, her eyes squinting with joy.

    “I would lap this off the floor if I had to,” Jess gushes. “This is tasty stuff.”

    Sam snorts, presenting a bottle that boasts a golden letter ‘S’ and tilting it to top off Jess’ glass. “I should hope so.”

    Lena grabs Sam’s arm, dizzy with dawning realization. “Hold on,” she says, “Are you serving Salon Cuvée S 2007?” She yelps. “In disposable cups?”

    “Just to be on the safe side.” Sam waves a pinkie finger between Lena and Kara, as if in explanation. “The two of you together are remarkably accident-prone.”

    Lena ignores her, sniffs her champagne again, and suffers. “This hurts me.”

    “It’s a good thing you’re a masochist then.” Sam clinks — or rather clicks — Lena’s glass with her own before Lena can stop her, then holds it out to the rest of her friends. “To love’s exquisite torment.”

    Torment sounds about right. Nestled as she is now into Kara’s side, it takes every single trembling fiber of Lena’s rapidly dwindling resolve to not simply let herself melt into it. It’s an involuntary response, this sudden weakness, like a reflex, or an allergic reaction, and Lena supposes she can blame no one but herself — because while she may have built up a tenuous sort of immunity to the sight of Kara, she’d been careful to keep her distance, sparing herself the effort of attempting to resist the feel of Kara’s body, the tickle of her breath.

    In a flash, Lena can see with perfect clarity how this evening is going to turn out: the drinking, the dancing, the company, all merrily whacking away at Lena’s carefully reinforced resistance.

    She takes in a private fortifying breath. This is it, then. With the wedding a mere six weeks away, this must be Lena Luthor’s Twelfth Labor; the final, most dangerous challenge of all. In order to arrive at Sam and Alex’s ceremony with her dignity and pride intact, she will need to survive a night of drunken debauchery without once propositioning Kara Danvers.

    Jess ducks in close to Lena, resting her forearm on Kara’s thigh to do so, and appearing momentarily distracted by the hard muscle Lena has worked very hard to forget is there. Jess blinks. Then she hushes, “Please don’t fire me again. I promise I negotiated her down from a far less manageable celebration. Sam’s starting bid was a week-long bar crawl in Berlin.”

    Lena takes a heartening sip of her champagne, glad for the reminder that all suffering is relative and things could be infinitely worse. “Honestly,” she admits, “speaking as someone who has lived through this type of scenario before? I’ll gladly take drinking from a disposable cup over having a gun pointed at my face.”

    Jess looks suddenly inexplicably terrified. Not saying another word, and without breaking eye contact, she tosses back the rest of her drink.

    -

    Jess is absolutely, 100%, not-a-single-doubt-in-Lena’s-mind fired.

    It turns out their sparkly pink tribulation’s final destination isn’t some fancy dance club downtown. Jess pulls them out of the car and into a dark, cobblestoned passageway just off the side of it, instead, where moths and mosquitos circle a single flickering bulb, and Lena finds herself grateful for Jess’ glittery headdress, guiding their tiny crew like the stars at sea.

    Lena almost trips when one of her stiletto heels gets stuck between two cobbles. She slaps at Sam’s helpfully outstretched hand and promises her, “If this is the abduction that ends with me dead in an alley, I’m coming back as one of those ghosts that buries all of your silverware in your backyard.”

    “I think I saw a horror movie like that once,” Alex says grimly and then, a little louder to Jess, “Are you absolutely sure you know where we’re going?”

    But of course she is.

    And it’s a motherfucking arcade.

    Lena contemplates making a run for it, but she doubts she’ll survive another stroll down murder street with with both of her ankles intact.

    Kara, gorgeous idiot that she is, doesn’t immediately catch on. “Did you take us to a fetish club?” She asks Jess, eyeing the — admittedly eerily anthropomorphic — cartoon animals painted on the light box sign over the entrance. XTREME ADVENTURES, it forebodes. Kara is teetering on the edge between wide-eyed excitement and slack-jawed awe.

    “You have got to be fucking kidding me,” Lena mutters.

    Kara skips over, leaning close to make sure only Lena will hear. “Don’t take this the wrong way,” she whispers, her breath glancing off Lena’s jaw. “Because you do look stunning, in that dress—” Her eyes stutter down Lena’s retrospectively indefensible amount of cleavage and linger where the gown of her dress splits, high on her left thigh, and Lena almost trips again. Kara’s grin brushes her cheek when she catches her, devastatingly casual and casually cruel, when she continues, “—but I think you should have worn that reindeer onesie I bought you. This looks like the type of establishment where it would have been a hit.”

    Kara is right, of course — even if it’s for the completely wrong reasons. The onesie would have been a far more practical choice. Because what they’ll be doing tonight, to Sam’s endless amusement and Alex’s near-orgasmic delight, basically amounts to scaling an obstacle course in five thousand dollars worth of formalwear. For hours. In the dark. While strangers try to shoot them.

    That part, unfortunately, doesn’t sink in until after Lena’s talked herself off the ledge of a very bad idea. As they make their way through the din of carnival music and high-pitched sound effects and into a booth, Lena spots a surprisingly impressive bar and thinks faking food poisoning can wait. At least until she has a couple of drinks in her.

    “Ruby can never know about this,” Sam says. She drives her hip into Lena’s in a forceful demand to give her more room, not caring that it pushes her well into Kara’s personal space again. “She’d never forgive us for not bringing her along.”

    “This place isn’t for kids,” Jess points out. “Adults only.”

    Lena can see why. If it wasn’t for the bowling lanes bordering the well-populated dancefloor and the colorful machines that line the balustrade along the second story, the place could almost pass for a regular nightclub.

    “Where is Ruby tonight?” Lena asks Sam, not at all distracted by Kara’s solid, clean-smelling form, fidgeting next to her. “With your history of unreliable sitters, I feel compelled to ask.”

    “I left her with your doorman,” Sam deadpans. “I told him she could have her very first alcoholic drink.”

    Lena shoves her, watching with satisfaction as Sam falls back in her seat, grinning and rubbing her arm.

    “She’s spending the night at a friend’s,” Alex says. “We were expecting the evening’s activities to skew a fair bit more alcoholic.”

    As if summoned, a waitress appears at their booth carrying a tray of mojito shots in one hand and a tablet in the other. She’s a tiny, severe-looking blonde with dark eyebrows, dressed top to bottom in a white leather bodysuit that looks a little over the top for a venue like this. “Hi!” she says jovially. “Welcome to Xtreme Adventures. My name is Sara. If you’re here to have a good time, we’re going to get along great tonight.” She double-checks her tablet, then peers back up at them. “You’re the, um.” She looks a little pained. “‘The Hung Bachelorettes?’”

    Sam spreads out in the booth, her arms around Alex and Lena, and gives the waitress a liquid grin. “What gave it away?”

    “The name’s Huang, actually,” Jess corrects.

    “But also,” Sam amends with an illustrative wave at their group, “obviously — yes.”

    The woman grins back, her leisurely gaze dragging over all of their outrageously inappropriate outfits before seeming to snag on the plunging V of Lena’s dress. “Right,” she drawls, “Obviously.” She winks at Lena with such a complete absence of shame that she feels her ears turn pink at Sara’s knowing smirk. But something else happens, too, and it’s far more interesting than their host’s audacious flirting — It’s the way Kara’s body reacts.

    Kara has been practically bouncing in her seat since they sat down, begging to go exploring, but Sara’s too-familiar overtures have made her grow silent and stiff, save for a hard thigh that twitches against Lena's own before jumping away again.

    Lena has put her hand on Kara’s knee before she’s fully aware that she’s done it, in some sort of instinctive effort to soothe. It’s effective, and it’s sweet, Lena tells herself, both the familiarity of the gesture and the way Kara softens in response. Lena should take their comfortable connection for the benchmark of friendship that it is, instead of fighting against the temptation to thumb the muscle beneath her hand.

    Sara seems determined to run them through the house rules, as if they aren’t all adults. There’s no alcohol inside the arena, she tells them, which is fair, and it’s not like Lena was planning on leaving their booth anyway. There’s also no running, which is no problem because why would Lena ever want to, and certainly not in these heels. But then she cites a statute against climbing or crawling, and Lena thinks, wait, and when Sara follows it up with a firm “No tampering with your weapon,” Lena blinks.

    “Weapon?” she sputters out.

    “Yeah! ” Sara eyes her, her unwavering smile dimming a little before she pushes it back into place. “Did no one tell you? You’re playing laser tag!”

    -

    Jess is an asshole. Kara is pumped. Lena hates everything and everyone, but especially herself, for not running for the hills the moment she spotted that distasteful limousine.

    They’re in the green room, or the briefing room, in the basement, of which the arena is rumored to take up the rest. Lena isn’t too clear on the details. She’s knocked back two tumblers of whiskey since receiving word of her impending death in an effort to face it with at least a semblance of courage and it’s entirely possible she’s missed most of the video relaying their instructions. It doesn’t matter, anyway.

    Alex is strapping Sam into a contraption that vaguely resembles a bulletproof vest, if bulletproof vests also sometimes doubled as billboards on the Las Vegas Strip. “Why don’t we ever do this anymore?” she laments. “Have we become boring?”

    Sam just grins down at her, swaying slightly as Alex yanks at her straps. “I’d say no,” she says, “Considering the practiced ease with which you’re doing what you’re doing right now.”

    Jess is ogling Kara, in particular the way her shirt stretches across her shoulders, which, who can blame her, truly, but then she comes right out and says, “I think you should take your top off.”

    There’s a lot of gawking and sputtering, in response. Kara’s eyes also flick to Lena, for some indiscernible reason, before returning to stare down at Jess again. “Um,” she says.

    “It’s all blacklight in there,” Jess explains. “You’ll be like a beacon.”

    Sara is back, stepping close into Lena’s personal space. “The bachelorette party, right?“ she checks. “So which one of you is the lucky gal?”

    “These two boring dorks.” Jess gestures at Alex and Sam, who roll their eyes at her as Kara obediently begins unbuttoning her shirt. “The rest of us are happily unattached and free to do whatever the hell we want tonight.”

    Sara purses her lips at that, pleased. Lena’s gaze is bouncing between things to look away from — Sara’s flirtatious stare, Sam’s knowing smirk, Kara stripping in the corner.

    Sara follows Lena’s gaze, sucking in an appreciative breath when she sees Kara in all her heather-gray-tank-topped glory. “Good to know,” she croons.

    “Someone’s working hard to earn their tip,” Lena grumbles the second their host is out of earshot, likely off to flirt with one of the other groups they’ll be sharing the arena with tonight.

    “I don’t know, Lena.” Sam looks thoughtful. “She’s pretty hot, don’t you think? Kind of figured she’d be your type.”

    Lena drops her head into her hands and groans. “Somebody shoot me.”

    Alex’s voice is rich with evil glee. “Oh,” she savors, “I intend to.”

    There’s a renaming ceremony, next. HANDSOME SAM is already on the digital scoreboard above their heads, and so is BRUISER, which is the name Jess has put in for herself. Warily observing her assistant’s familiarity with the clasps of her vest and her — what had the video called it? A blaster? A taser? Anyway — Lena begins to suspect Jess has actually earned the title.

    Kara is back to her bouncing self again. “I want to be Ripley,” she says. “Or maybe Atomic Blonde? Ooh, I know! The Bride! Wait— that’d probably be confusing.”

    It’s an act of mercy when Jess chooses for her. “Let’s go with Supergirl,” she suggests, and Lena assures herself that she's merely imagined Jess’ reverential glance at Kara’s muscle definition.

    It’s Lena’s turn to choose, but she hesitates. She can hardly put Luthor, it’s not like it’s going to be just their private party in there. Lena knows she needs something anonymous, but she feels unable to think of anything.

    “Half Pint?” Jess suggests, helpful fingers already at the buttons.

    “Don’t you dare,” Lena says. “I’m taller than you.”

    Sam considers briefly, smirking at the way Lena is studiously avoiding looking in Kara’s direction. Lena is distantly aware that she isn’t succeeding very well — she sees enough to know Kara’s eyes keep finding her, to know she keeps giving her little reassuring smiles, and Sam has apparently noticed it, too. “We could call you Kryptonite,” she grins.

    “I could call you a shithead,” Lena retorts.

    Kara has missed their brief exchange, busy as she is now staring slack-jawed at the board. “I am not calling you Commander!” she huffs at Alex.

    “Sorry, Supergirl,” her sister snarks. “Rules are rules.”

    “It suits you, Danvers,” Sam tells her smoothly.

    Alex nods her head back at the screen. “That’s Commander Danvers, to you.”

    Sam is smug. “Not for much longer.”

    Alex tilts her chin and regards her fiancée through her lashes. Says, “I never agreed to taking your name.”

    “Okay.” Sam swaggers up close, flirtatious. “How’s this?” she suggests. “We play to win tonight. Loser yields their naming privileges.”

    Lena rolls her eyes. “Yes, because making a bet is a totally sane and level-headed way to decide your future.”

    Sam looks at her pointedly, as Jess inches closer with a bedazzled Kevlar of Lena’s own. “Honey,” Sam says as Lena tries to back away, “I say this with only love in my heart: no one in their right mind would trust you to determine what ‘sane’ or ‘level-headed’ means.”

    Lena tries not to struggle as Jess pulls the polyester vest over the delicate fabrics of her dress. “Any damages will be coming straight out of your paycheck,” she tells her. “Couldn’t you have abducted me during my morning run?”

    “Figured you’d be a better runner when you’re wearing your heels,” Jess says. “They’ve certainly seen more action than your athletic shoes.”

    “Two minutes to start,” Sam calls out, her eyes on the board. “Time to stop crying, princess fussy pants.”

    Jess’ fingers have hit ‘confirm’ before Lena can even blink. “We have a winner.”

    Sam leads them all in a final toast, raising their phasers, Lena remembers now, in lieu of drinks. “To my favorite people,” she says. “I love you all in spite of your many, many faults.”

    After that, the shooting starts.

    -

    Slight correction: Lena’s assistant isn’t fired. Lena is murdering her, instead. Once she locates her, that is. It’s pertinent that she deals with three minor obstacles first.

    One: she’s lost. The basement arena is large, labyrinthine and almost completely dark, save for some scant strips of neon here and there which, to Lena’s great vexation, keep blinking out at random only to reappear elsewhere entirely.

    Two: her friends have abandoned her. They all took off in different directions as soon as they were given the go-ahead, which happened right around the time that Lena’s fight-or-flight response had finally kicked in. She’s alone in a room full of hostile strangers, ducking from cover to cover, practicing her mindful breathing techniques while figuring out a way to outlast the timer without getting tagged.

    It’s not that she could care less about her final score. In fact she envies the dead, and would gladly join them in the upstairs afterlife. She’s sure the bar there is serving consolatory drinks. But Lena would have to drain all of her health points to make it there, first, and each time she’s hit, both her phaser and her vest vibrate and flash, making an awful racket, and it’s doing great damage to Lena’s non-virtual heart and overall presence of mind.

    Oop, there it goes again. Goddamnit.

    And that’s the third thing: Lena is pretty sure some friendless dork is targeting her specifically.

    “What is your problem?” she yells out into the dark, and then Kara jumps out from where she’d apparently snuck up right behind her.

    Kara’s eyes are wide and apologetic above the ghoulish blue gleam of her vest, broad palms turned up in surrender. “I take it back!” she rushes out. ”I wasn’t aiming for—”

    Lena doesn’t linger, doesn’t hesitate, just lines up her shot and takes it. FIRST BLOOD! her display rejoices. It’s almost as rewarding as the wailing of Kara’s phaser and the look of utter devastation in her eyes.

    “You shot me,” she pouts.

    “You shot me,” Lena reminds her. “Isn’t that what we’re supposed to be doing here tonight?”

    But then Kara’s vest flashes a second time, mirrored by Lena’s own a moment later. YOU ARE TAKING FIRE! her display warns, for what is probably the twenty-seventh time this round. Lena’s shriek becomes muffled when a pair of chiseled arms folds her into a more easily transportable shape, before depositing her behind the nearest half-wall. Kara doesn’t let go completely even after putting her down, just crouches beside her, laying an urgent finger to Lena’s lips.

    Lena knocks Kara’s finger away before she can do something stupid like biting it off or licking it into her mouth. “What do you think you’re doing?” she hisses.

    Kara is close enough that her breath tickles Lena’s lips when she tells them, very seriously, “I think someone is following you.”

    “No kidding,” Lena says. “Isn’t it you?”

    Kara blushes hard. “No,” she says. “Or, maybe. A tiny bit. But there’s also someone else. They’ve been on your— um.” She trails off, then recovers. “Pretty sure they’ve been trailing you from, like, the beginning of the round.”

    Lena watches her. Kara seems antsy, like she’s struggling to keep her gaze from flickering back to Lena’s mouth again. It makes Lena feel a little reckless. “You’ve been watching over me, huh?” she teases, because she’s clearly not thinking straight. “I hope you enjoyed the view.”

    “I’m just saying,” Kara huffs, furrowing her brow as if she believes it will hide her fluster. Her cheeks are purple now beneath the blue glow of her vest. “We could make it official and form an alliance.”

    Lena laughs at her audacity. “Didn’t you just shoot me?”

    Kara shrugs. “You shot me. We all make mistakes.”

    Lena drops her head against the wall and sighs, her heels squeaking a little on the rubber-streaked linoleum. It’s the night of the Symphony Gala. She’s supposed to be sipping champagne in the ladies’ restroom, hiding from her mother and listening in on sad women going into far too much detail relaying their latest plastic surgery mishaps.

    She rolls her head sideways to blink up into Kara’s eager eyes.

    For a moment, it almost looks like Kara may do something reckless herself. But then she’s pulling Lena up, strong fingers carefully cradling her wrist, like she’s an actual superhero. “Are you okay?” she asks when Lena half-tumbles into her, gravity doing that thing it tends to do when Lena Luthor is in close company of Kara Danvers. “I know you have little to no natural talent for walking,” Kara says, tugging her lips between her teeth as if trying to hold back a smile, “and those shoes can’t help matters much.”

    Lena glares at her. Kara wouldn’t understand, would she, wearing her characteristic oxford flats, though it looks like a new patent leather set.

    Kara just winks at Lena’s glare. “Come on,” she says. “Hold on to me.”

    It’s as bad of an idea as getting into her bed was, back in Colorado half a lifetime ago, but just as she had during that frigid first night, Lena fails to produce a good enough excuse to resist Kara’s offer. Don’t friends allow friends to keep them from falling in battle? Would it not be a more significant, more vulnerable thing, to refuse?

    “Fine,” she drawls. “Save me, Supergirl.”

    And so she does.

    For the next five minutes Lena’s phaser is blissfully quiet, her vest beautifully still. It isn’t even anything guns-blazing dramatic, the way Kara carries Lena through the rest of the round — she’s just a bit more tactical, a little more deliberate about where she guides them next. It’s also pretty obvious Kara was actually paying attention to the instructional video, muttering things in Lena’s ear about downtime countdowns and shielding her sensors and saving their ammo.

    It's preposterous how safe and grateful it makes Lena feel. She finds herself landing a little closer to Kara each time they fall into cover, and Kara’s hands catch her a little more liberally every time she does, pressing their bodies together, her hand palming Lena’s hip, and if Lena finds herself privately reliving memories of the last time Kara had her pinned against a wall, that’s nobody’s business but her own.

    There’s about a minute left on the timer and Lena is seriously beginning to wonder whether she might actually be having a good time.

    And then Alex ambushes them out of nowhere, making Lena practically jump out of her own skin.

    “Nice dodge back there, Supergirl,” she mocks after the combined shrieks of Lena and their gear have died down some. The glint of Alex’s most winning smile is tinted bloody by the red light of her vest. She looks at them for a moment, Lena clinging close to Kara still, and asks, “What’s going on here?”

    So Lena shoots her.

    It’s really more of a reflex than anything else, but Alex takes it as a personal affront, going from cheerful to vengeful in .01 seconds. “I will destroy you,” she growls.

    Lena ducks, miraculously managing to make Alex miss her shot, and then books it. “Hey! No running!” Alex calls after her. “Why are you running!”

    Lena doesn’t look back, just zig-zags on relevé and yells, “It’s called survival fucking instinct!”

    “Wait,” Kara calls out after her, sounding bewildered and afraid. “How are you running?”

    And that’s when the buzzer sounds, indicating the end of the round.

    “Thank god,” Lena sighs bonelessly, resisting the urge to lay down facedown on the floor.

    -

    Back in the green room, Lena doesn’t give a single thought to the expensive dress she’s wearing, tugging herself free from her private polyester prison while her friends chatter excitedly about their greatest victories.

    “What a brilliant idea this was, Jess,” Lena bites. “Wasn’t I just complaining how I haven’t had a gun pointed at me in a while? I was beginning to feel neglected.”

    Kara stops smiling and stares at her instead. “I wasn’t even— crap, how long?”

    Lena’s a little taken aback by the genuine concern wafting her way. It’s not just Kara — Sam and Jess are both eyeing her warily, and Alex is looking at her like she’s just swallowed her own heart. Fuck. “Oh, don’t look at me like that,” she demands. She’s fine, she was just being a cantankerous brat.

    “I’m sorry,” Jess blurts. “You’re always so nonchalant about it, I didn’t think—”

    “Forget it, it’s fine,” Lena says, trying to make it sound breezy enough to relieve the guilt coloring her assistant’s features. “Come to think of it,” she muses, “it might actually be kind of satisfying, getting a chance to shoot back for a change.”

    Kara’s lips twitch up at that, but her voice is still a little cautious when she says, “I actually don’t recall you doing much shooting of your own.”

    Sam is already grinning, already back to her usual insufferable self. “Well,” she says, “if she’s as fine as she says she is, she’ll have plenty of time to make up for it in the remaining rounds.”

    No. Oh, no no no.

    Lena scrambles after Sam as her best friend takes the stairs up to the bar two steps at a time. “Wait,” she urges, “Remaining rounds?”

    There’s two more of them scheduled tonight, apparently, spaced apart to give them time to drink and recover. Sam has the gall to refer to the blacklit Armageddon they’ve just survived as ‘a promising practice round.’ She rubs Lena’s back in consolation as they make their way back to the bar, where a large LCD screen lists the evening’s rankings thus far. PRINCESS FUSSY PANTS languishes somewhere near the bottom. #1, Lena notes with no small measure of alarm, is JESUS, with BRUISER following unsettlingly close behind.

    “Don’t take it too hard,” Sam coos. “Even if you don’t improve, you’ll still be the world’s best-dressed loser.”

    “If it’s a shared title, maybe,” Jess says. “Because I promise you yours truly will be the only winner here tonight, and Kara’s over there looking like she walked straight out of the latest Terminator movie.”

    Lena doesn’t need to be reminded. Kara’s starched shirt is dangling loosely from her hand — likely creased beyond recognition — and she’s still in her racerback top, an immediately life-threatening amount of golden skin on display. Without her vest, Lena can see Kara is wearing suspenders — a vintage pair that could easily be mistaken for a harness at first glance, straps of well-worn leather looping around her waist and buckling just below the curve of her chest. Kara fidgets a little under their scrutiny.

    “Well,” Lena croaks, suddenly feeling alarmingly thirsty. Must be all that running around. “I guess it’s good some of our personal styles lend themselves so well to navigating a simulated warzone.”

    “And some of them look better on the dance floor,” Jess announces, walking backwards away from them while holding out a grabby hand. Lena waves her off, but Sam happily obliges, Alex following them in search of a bathroom.

    It’s just Kara and herself, then, carrying their drinks back to their table. Kara slides into the booth behind Lena, and even with all that space available to her, she sits almost as close as she had before, when Jess and Sam had been crowding them. Lena really rather wishes Kara hadn’t, but she also doesn’t make an effort to rectify the situation.

    They’ve been in the trenches together, after all. It creates a bond.

    Lena feels herself relax a little bit more the longer she sits there, Kara breathing calmly, quiet beside her as the adrenalin leaves her system and the thrum of alcohol sets in. Jess and Sam are busy making a mockery of themselves on the dance floor, and somehow still attracting the attention of more than a handful of strangers.

    Lena looks back at Kara only to find that Kara’s already looking at her. She smiles. “They look like they’re having a good time.”

    Kara is smiling, too. “Why don’t you join them?”

    “Someone needed to assume custody of these drinks,” Lena says. “Why don’t you?”

    “I would if you came with me.”

    Lena looks at her, looks at those gorgeous freckled arms, a fresh pink flush to her cheeks, and nearly snorts at the suggestion. “No.”

    Kara regards her, a little curious, a little amused. “Why not?”

    Lena speaks her answer straight into her glass. “Because I’ll only end up embarrassing myself.”

    Kara laughs, a rich, melodious sound that Lena savors as if it were resonating inside her own chest. “Didn’t you say you take dance classes?”

    “I do cardio ballet,” Lena corrects her. “I can temps levé for hours, but I promise you nobody wants to see that.”

    Kara playfully nudges Lena’s knee with her own. “Come on,” she goads, her voice rumbling perilously close to a purr. “Are you seeing Jess and Sam out there? No one’s expecting you to be flawless.”

    Lena looks at Kara, thinking she’s like, the epitome of flawless, simply reclining there, and she can’t help the appreciative look she drags down Kara’s frame any more than the fondness blooming in her chest. “That is not what I’m worried about,” she laughs mournfully, not quite realizing she’s said the words out loud until long moments after they’ve left her mouth.

    Oops.

    Lena reaches for her drink, but her fingers are shaking. Also, her glass is empty.

    Kara nudges her again, supportive, friendly, oblivious, the full length of her thigh now pressing firm against Lena’s own. She ducks her head until Lena meets her eyes again, and gives her an encouraging smile. “What are you worried about?”

    Lena sustains their intimate eye contact, not strong enough to break it, too weak to laugh it off. The moment stretches between them until Lena’s heart is pounding, until Kara is no longer smiling, until her question has answered itself.

    Silence settles heavily between them, and it’s there, just like that — everything Lena didn’t mean to admit to, all she tried so hard not to let happen.

    She watches as Kara’s gaze darts to her mouth again, feeling the tiny movement echoed thickly inside her, something twisting taut between her legs. She parts her lips experimentally, allowing a small breath to escape, and Kara’s eyes focus sharply on Lena’s own when she realizes she’s been caught staring.

    Kara fiddles briefly with her glasses, but instead of pushing them more firmly onto her nose like Lena is expecting, she starts to slide them down instead.

    Before anything else world-shattering can happen, Alex pushes into their booth. "So,” she says, scooting in on Kara’s side, “did anyone else just get propositioned by our host, or is that a bride-only privilege?"

    Lena recovers before Kara does. “She hit on you, too?” she gasps. “Whatever happened to respecting the sanctity of marriage?”

    Sam has no idea what’s happening but, as usual, confidently inserts herself in Lena’s business anyway. “Lena,” she chides, scooting in next to her, “let’s do everyone a favor and leave our Catholicism at the door tonight.”

    “Uh, please do not,” Jess interjects as she slides into the booth behind Sam. “All that repressed anger is really going to help us out tonight.”

    “I don’t know what you’re talking about,” Lena says. “I don’t have any repressed anger.”

    Sam clasps a firm hand around the back of Lena’s neck and looks at her with infinite affection. “Lena,” she says. “Buttercup. Honey bun. Completely non-romantic, at least ninety-eight percent platonic love of my life.”

    “Hi!” Alex waves cheerfully. “Future wife, sitting right here.”

    Sam sighs. “This is why combined bachelor parties are such a terrible idea.”

    Jess scoffs. “It was your idea.”

    “Which might explain why it was so terrible.” Lena signals their host, in desperate need of a distraction.

    “Hey,” Sam says brightly, “I just had another one.”

    Lena groans. That grin only ever means one thing. “Please don’t say tequila.”

    Sam yells it, instead. “Tequila!”

    Sara returns to their table with not one but two trays of shots, winking at Sam this time as she tells them the first one is on the house. After Lena knocks hers back, Sam expertly switches her still full glass for Lena’s empty one. Lena frowns at her, still grimacing at the taste in her mouth. “What are you doing?”

    “What does it look like?” Sam asks innocently. “I’m enabling you.”

    Lena narrows her eyes at her best friend. “You are a bad influence, Samantha Arias.”

    “No,” Sam holds up a finger while she hands Lena the shot, “I’m a hedonist. There’s a difference.”

    “A hedonist who is consistently more sober than me,” Lena points out.

    “Not my fault and don’t look now,” Sam says, “But I think you have an admirer.”

    “Is it our host?” Lena asks, finishing off Sam’s second shot for good measure.

    “Please,” Sam scoffs, “that woman is admiring all of us. I’m talking about someone all your own.”

    Lena follows Sam’s eyeline and quints to find an unfamiliar blonde woman at the bar, frozen in the chaos going on around her. “Is she just. Standing there staring at me?” Lena’s nose twitches in distaste. “That’s weird, right?”

    “People stare at you wherever you go,” Jess points out, clearly unimpressed. “Kara is staring at you right now. Fuck, so am I. Have you seen a mirror lately?”

    Kara sputters into her drink, and when Lena looks back over after making sure she’s okay, the woman at the bar has disappeared.

    “Maybe you scared her off,” Sam suggests. “Your eyebrows have always had a way with words.”

    -

    Time flies when you’re having drinks, and all too soon Lena finds herself descending the stairs into their jolly torture chamber for round two.

    Kara’s voice is soft in Lena’s ear. “Stick with me this time,” she says, shoulder bumping gently into hers. “I’ll keep you safe.”

    “Already developing a savior complex, are we?” Lena asks in an incredibly lame attempt to mitigate the magnetism that’s swiftly rematerialized in the small amount of air left between their bodies. She’s beginning to regret knocking back those shots. Letting her guard down in public like this is irresponsible enough. Doing it around Kara? That’s positively moronic.

    Sara is handing out two-way radios in the briefing room. “Alright!” She says brightly. “So for the next round, we’re going to ask you all to pair up.”

    Jess takes one look at their little group, pops her gum and promptly walks away. “I’m out,” she calls over her shoulder with a little wave, “you kids have fun.” Lena watches her as her assistant catches the attention of an admittedly remarkably messianic-looking nearby man, nodding pointedly at the digital scoreboard advertising her ranking. “So,” she says matter-of-factly, “Jesus, right? I’m Bruiser. You interested?”

    Kara is smiling warmly at Lena, no doubt very happy her plan is working out so well, but then an unexpected arm wrenches itself almost painfully tight around Lena’s own. It’s Alex. “You’re with me, Princess,” she announces.

    Lena honestly can’t say if she’s feeling flattered or just profoundly perplexed.

    “But.” Kara pouts. “Sam is your fiancée. And Lena and I kind of—”

    Alex raises a critical eyebrow. “Kind of what? Alliances are fleeting, Supergirl. We’ve all read The Hunger Games.”

    Kara looks back and forth between the brides, crestfallen and confused, but Alex is all steely-eyed determination and Sam looks delightedly triumphant rather than hurt.

    Sam bumps her shoulder into Kara’s. “Looks like it’s you and me, kid.”

    -

    In the field, Lena discovers Alex has all of her sister’s cleverness and courage and absolutely none of Kara’s charm.

    “What on earth are you doing?” She demands, dragging Lena behind cover seven minutes and at least fourteen miles into the round, when Lena’s kill count still sits proudly at zero while Alex is rounding up her targets with the intensity of a hyperactive cattle dog.

    “I’m trying!” Lena tells her, gasping for air. “My phaser. Something’s wrong with it, I think. It’s like it’s not even transmitting half the time.”

    Alex puffs out a breath and takes Lena’s hand. “It won’t work unless you use both hands,” she tells her. “Like this. Did you not watch the instructional video?”

    Lena glares at her. “I was a little busy trying to not have a panic attack, thank you.”

    “Here you go,” Alex says, a little gentler, folding Lena’s fingers along the bottom of what Lena supposes would be considered the phaser’s barrel. “Try it now.”

    They poke their heads out of cover in perfect synchronicity, observing for a moment the pandemonium playing out in front of them. Off in the distance, someone who sounds an awful lot like Jess roars, “Eat it, nerd!”

    “Get the guy with the neckbeard,” Alex instructs, already lining up her own shot. “I’ll get his buddy in the Dylan t-shirt. How much do you wanna bet he couldn’t name more than three of his songs?”

    Alex’s man goes down instantly. Lena’s target takes a little longer. Three, maybe four attempts.

    Which is, in all fairness, the point where Alex decides to shoot him for her, because he’s about to duck out of view entirely.

    “Thanks,” Lena says sheepishly as Alex returns her phaser. Alex’s face is a stormcloud of confusion.

    “So explain to me what’s happening right now,” she says. “Didn’t you win an olympic medal in sharp shooting?”

    Ah.

    Lena hums, nodding in understanding. That’s why her best friend had looked so pleased when Alex had picked Lena as her partner. “Sam tell you that?” she asks innocently.

    Alex’s brow furrows further, but she nods.

    Lena purses her lips to keep from smiling. “I qualified,” she says. “Never actually competed. Also it was fencing, not shooting.”

    Alex throws her head back and sighs, “Motherfucker.”

    She goes a little easier on her, from that point on, with her expectations thoroughly squashed and her sights now set firmly on revenge against the woman she’s about to marry. Lena still gets her cardio in for the next five to seven years, but Alex makes a passable drill instructor — she’s far less careful with Lena than Kara had been, her playstyle confrontational rather than avoidant, taking on opponents preferably in close combat, but Lena has to admit her personal ranking is looking a lot better for it.

    Like most good things in life, it doesn’t last.

    Suddenly, with twelve minutes left on the clock and their ranking steadily climbing up the top ten, it’s as if everywhere they go, wherever they move to, they’re tagged the very moment they rest. They’re pressed back to back now, Alex sharply vigilant, Lena so twitchy she’s shooting at everything that moves — which in a blacklit neon funhouse means pretty much everything, period.

    As Alex drags them around another corner, their vests announce yet another dent in Alex’s honor. “Oh,” she snarls, “now I’m mad.”

    “I think we’re a target because we’re a threat,” Lena wheezes. “We could. You know. Sit out. The rest of the round. Maybe then they’ll leave us alone.”

    Alex looks at her as if it’s the smartest thing she’s said all night, and Lena is at least halfway sure she’d been joking.

    So. For the remainder of round two, Lena Luthor is bait.

    It isn’t exactly Lena’s favorite thing, being a sitting duck, but she does appreciate the break it grants her from running and hiding and the occasional bout of screaming. Alex’s voice is grainy and jubilant over the two-way radio strapped to Lena’s chest, tagging Lena’s would-be adversaries from a sniper hideout somewhere above her. Just before the five-minute mark she single-handedly pushes their tentative twosome to #5, and Lena feels almost proud.

    “Stay where you are,” Alex grunts as she climbs down from her invisible perch. “I want us to meet Sam as a team when the buzzer sounds. I can't wait to see her loser face.”

    Lena almost relaxes, but then she sees the light from someone’s vest emanating around the corner. “Alex,” she hisses, “Do you have eyes on someone just up ahead?”

    “Negative,” Alex answers. “I’m dodging fire. Fucking Supergirl. Give me just a minute.”

    “You may want to hurry,” Lena tells her. “I think I’m about to get killed.”

    “Do what you do best,” Alex advises. “Run and hide.”

    And Lena does, but after her third evasive maneuver it becomes obvious her pursuer won’t be so easily deterred. “Alex?”

    “ETA unknown. I’ll be there as soon as I can.”

    Lena bites her lip, feeling acutely vulnerable without a human shield. “I’m actually freaking out a little,” she confesses. “Kara thinks someone’s been following me since our arrival. What if—”

    Lena turns the corner glancing anxiously back over her shoulder, and finds herself chest-to-chest with a stranger who looks at her as if Lena isn’t the only one whose heart has just jumped into her throat.

    Lena recognizes her — it’s the woman Sam had pointed out in the bar. She’s frozen, her face contorted in shock, and barely seems to notice when Lena readies her phaser with shaky hands and awkwardly takes the shot.

    Alex would be proud.

    “It is you,” the woman breathes, barely audible over the noise erupting from her phaser. “I mean. You are Lena Luthor, aren’t you?”

    And, shit. Fuck. Lena has been compromised. This is bad. This is trouble.

    As if the lit-up vest strapped to her chest hadn’t made her enough of a target already.

    Lena Luthor is used to being an object of people’s ire. When she was younger, it had mostly been about the size of her family’s fortune, and the decidedly less-than-philanthropic way her father’s forebears had come into it. Between Lex’s disappearance and his eventual arrest, Lena had become a public scapegoat for the crimes he had committed. And even after her lawyers had successfully proven her innocence, the Luthor name had never quite managed to lose its bitter aftertaste. At the end of it, Lena’s come to understand that it doesn’t matter whose finger is on the trigger. The bullet will hit her just as hard.

    Alex’s voice is shrill over the radio now, urging her to respond. But Lena is a little too busy negotiating with a potential assailant to come to the phone right now.

    “Look,” she tries, raising her hands in surrender. “I don’t know if it’s my brother or my money that you’re after—”

    The woman doesn’t get to hear the rest of Lena’s sentence.

    Because Alex tackles her to the ground.

    -

    There are actual tears running down Sam’s face. “And so I run over, right,” she cries laughing, “and there’s Alex wrestling with this perfectly harmless-looking blonde — I’m talking full-fledged Boucher cherub — and Lena’s in the corner just shaking, she’s so mad—”

    “Her name is Eve Teschmacher, and she’s a full-grown human woman,” Lena interrupts, taking a stiff little sip from her drink. “And I wasn’t mad.”

    Lena notices her fingers are still trembling when she sets down her glass. Kara has noticed, too. She’s further away now, with Lena bookended by the brides-to-be, but her foot finds Lena’s under the table, taps it three times as if to check in. Lena gives her a wobbly little smile. Wishes it wasn’t Sam’s animated arm currently slung over her shoulder.

    Sam giggles and ignores her, entirely undeterred. “And then!” she continues gleefully, “With a completely straight face, this beautiful infant looks over and says to Lena — ‘I love you.’” Sam swoons exaggeratedly on the last three words, falling into Lena’s shoulder belly-laughing as the rest of the table reacts to her dramatic rendition.

    Kara’s response is outraged disbelief. “What?”

    “‘I love you,’” Sam squeaks, wiping at her eyes. “After which she immediately tries to backpedal, of course. As if the damage isn’t already done, she goes, ‘I mean I love your work.’”

    “Boo,” Jess judges, apparently deeply disappointed in this stranger’s lack of game.

    Lena pushes Sam off her shoulder. “She was referring to L-Corp’s advancements in induced pluripotent stem cell technology,” she explains. “She’s hardly, what did you call it — a fangirl.”

    Alex is unconvinced. “She told you she envies the ‘stability’ of your ‘macromolecule substrates’,” she says, with an unnecessarily pointed set of air quotes.

    Kara blinks. “Well,” she says. “That’s— specific.”

    “Let me guess,” Jess says, sipping her cocktail through a crazy straw that loops out the word ‘bitch’. “Lena hired her on the spot.”

    Lena feels her ears burning. “I offered her an interview,” she rectifies.

    Sam softens a little. “You need to stop acting like everyone who treats you with anything less than outright hostility is entitled to financial compensation,” she says. “You aren’t somehow in her debt just because she didn’t actively try to hurt you.”

    “Alex might be for hurting Ms. Teschmacher, however,” Kara points out.

    “She was fine,” Alex waves her off. “I’m a medical professional. I know how to take a person down without breaking the Hippocratic oath.”

    Jess spins her straw into the air like she’s trying to rewind their conversation. “So hang on, wait a second, is that why a Dr. Grace P. Parker in Colorado mysteriously appeared on L-Corp’s payroll nine months back?”

    “Listen,” Lena says, steepling her hands on the table in front of her. “Dr. Parker is very good at what she does.”

    “Ruby’s doctor at the emergency clinic?” Alex inquires with disdain. “She’s a GP!”

    “She’s an ER doctor. But more to the point,” Jess argues, “she doesn’t work for us.”

    Sam shakes her head. “No, I’ll allow that one. I mean if someone saves my daughter’s life—”

    Lena breathes, letting the conversation filter in and out until the remaining tension of the hectic encounter has bled away. Kara’s foot is still pressed against her own, and it grounds her. She wonders if there’s danger in this letting go, this breathing out, this sitting back and letting her friends support her.

    She’s also realizing that she’s missed Kara, and that she’s actually missing her still, with the whole half hour they spent apart, the way she’s sitting so far away. Before tonight, Lena had forbidden herself to indulge in any of the senses with which she’d experienced Kara almost a year before, and their reintroduction has made her hungry — the feel of her urging her to take in her scent, the scent of her tempting Lena to taste.

    She’s suddenly feeling ravenous.

    So when what feels like only a brief moment later Lena finds herself locked in a cage, Kara’s fingers circling loosely around Lena’s wrists as she tells her quietly to spread her legs, Lena Luthor is far less surprised than she probably should be.
